The classification of complex algebraic surfaces
Ciro Ciliberto

Abstract
. These notes contain the classification of complex algebraic surfaces following Mori’s theory. They includes the P 12 P_{12} –Theorem, Sarkisov’s programme in the surface case and Noether–Castelnuovo’s Theorem in its classical version.



1.
Introduction

This text grew up from the notes of a graduate course I gave at the University of Roma “Tor Vergata” in the academic year 2018–19. The subject is the classification of complex algebraic surfaces following Mori’s theory. It includes the
P_{12}
–Theorem, Sarkisov’s programme in the surface case and Noether–Castelnuovo’s Theorem in its classical version. I am indebted to the first chapter of Matzuki’s book
[
16
]
, though I simplified and streamlined his exposition in some points. For the
P_{12}
–Theorem I partly followed the unpublished paper
[
10
]
by P. Francia and the recent paper
[
7
]
by Catanese–Li. The classification of bielliptic surfaces follows the short and elegant exposition by Bombieri–Mumford in
[
5
]
. The classical version of Noether–Castelnuovo’s Theorem follows Calabri notes
[
6
]
.

This text is intended for people who have a good knowledge of basic algebraic geometry. Generally speaking, acquaintance with basic parts of books like
[
11
,
12
]
should be sufficient. In any case I tried to make the text as self–contained as possible. For this reason a first chapter is devoted to some preliminaries.

2.
Some preliminaries

This section is a non–exhaustive collection of some preliminaries which we will later use. The expert reader may skip this section.

In what follows, by a
surface
we will mean a smooth, irreducible projective complex variety of dimension 2.

2.1.
Projective morphisms

We will use the following basic:

Proposition 2.1

(cf.
[
12
]
, Chapt. III, Prop. 7.12)
.

Let
X,Y
be schemes and
g:Y\to X
a morphism. Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a vector bundle on
X
. To give a morphism
f:Y\to{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})
such that the diagram

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
id
\textstyle{X}

commutes, is equivalent to give a line bundle
L
on
Y
and a surjective map of sheaves
g^{*}({\mathcal{E}})\twoheadrightarrow L
.

In case
X
is a point, this expresses the well known fact that to give a morphism of a variety
Y
to a projective space of dimension
n
is equivalent to give a line bundle
L
on
Y
which is base point free and
\dim(|L|)\geqslant n
.

2.2.
Basic invariants

Consider the exponential exact sequence

(1)

0\longrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2\pi i{\rm exp}\cdot}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{S}\longrightarrow 0

which induces the exact cohomology sequence

0\to H^{1}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})\longrightarrow H^{1}(S,{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{S})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm ch}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow H^{2}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})

One sets
H^{1}(S,{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{S})=\operatorname{Pic}(S)
, the
Picard group
of
S
, which is the group of line bundles on
S
, modulo isomorphisms.
One denotes by
q(S)
(often simply by
q
) the dimension of the vector space
H^{1}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})\simeq H^{0}(S,\Omega^{1}_{S})
: this is called the
irregularity
of
S
. It is well known that
H^{1}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
is a maximal rank lattice inside
H^{1}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})
, so that

\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(S):=\frac{H^{1}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})}{H^{1}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})}

is a complex torus of dimension
q
called the
Picard variety
of
S
. Note that the
first Betti number

b_{1}(S)
, i.e., the rank of
H^{1}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
, is
2q
.

The map
{\rm ch}
is called the
Chern class
. One sets
NS(S):=\operatorname{im}({\rm ch})
, which is called the
Nèron–Severi group
of
S
, and is a finitely generated abelian group. Then we set
\operatorname{Num}(S)=NS(S)/\equiv
, where
\equiv
is the
numerical equivalence
. This is a finitely generated free abelian group, whose rank is denoted by
\rho(S)
(often simply by
\rho
). Then we denote
N^{1}(S):=\operatorname{Num}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}
, a real vector space of dimension
\rho
.

We denote by
Z_{1}(S)
, or by
\operatorname{\rm Div}(S)
, the free abelian group generated by all divisors on
S
. There is an obvious epimorphism
\lambda:Z_{1}(S)\to\operatorname{Pic}(S)
, which sends a divisor
D
to the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(D)
. One has
\lambda(D)=\lambda(D^{\prime})
if and only if
D
and
D
’ are
linearly equivalent
, denoted by
D\sim D^{\prime}
. The epimorphism
\lambda
induces an epimorphism
\Lambda:(Z_{1}(S)/\equiv)\to\operatorname{Num}(S)
, which is an isomorphism. We set
N_{1}(S)=(Z_{1}(S)/\equiv)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}
, which is isomorphic to
N^{1}(S)
, so it has also dimension
\rho
. In fact we will often identify
Z_{1}(S)
and
\operatorname{Num}(S)
, and
N_{1}(S)
and
N^{1}(S)
, via the isomorphism
\Lambda
. So we will speak of numerical equivalence class of divisors or equivalently of line bundles.

A divisor
D
in
N_{1}(S)
is called
effective
, or a
curve
, if
D=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}D_{i}
, where
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
are distinct, irreducible curves on
S
and
m_{i}\geqslant 0
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.
We define
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)
to be the closure in
N_{1}(S)
of the convex cone generated by all curves on
S
. This is called the
Kleiman–Mori cone
of
S
. The cone
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)
is
strictly convex
(see §
2.10
below).

On
\operatorname{Pic}(S)
there is the bilinear form determined by the
intersection product
. It induces bilinear forms (also called
intersection products
) on
\operatorname{NS}(S)
, on
N^{1}(S)
and on
N_{1}(S)
. The intersection product is non–degenerate on
N^{1}(S)
and on
N_{1}(S)
.

The dimension of
H^{2}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})\simeq H^{0}(S,\Omega^{2}_{S})
is denoted by
p_{g}(S)
(or simply by
p_{g}
) and is called the
geometric genus
of
S
, and
p_{a}(S)=p_{g}(S)-q(S)
(also denoted by
p_{a}
) is called the
arithmetic genus
of
S
. Note that

\chi(S)=h^{0}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})-h^{1}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})+h^{2}(S,{\mathcal{O}}_{S})=p_{g}(S)-q(S)+1=p_{a}(S)+1

(also denoted by
\chi
).

The line bundle
\Omega^{2}_{S}
is the
canonical bundle
of
S
, and one denotes it by
K_{S}
. Often
K_{S}
denotes also any
canonical divisor
, i.e., any divisor such that
\Omega^{2}_{S}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(K_{S})
. Note that
p_{g}-1=\dim(|K_{S}|)
. Moreover, if
p_{g}=0
, then the map ch is surjective and
NS(S)=H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
, i.e., any 2–cycle on
S
is homologous to an algebraic one: one expresses this by saying that
any 2–cycle is algebraic
.

The bundles
K_{S}^{\otimes n}
with
n
a positive integer, are called
pluricanonical bundles
and
P_{n}(S):=h^{0}(S,K_{S}^{\otimes n})
is called the
n
–th plurigenus
of
S
(also denoted by
P_{n}
).

The
Betti numbers

b_{i}(S)
(also denoted by
b_{i}
) are related to the above invariants. First, by Poincaré duality, one has
b_{i}=b_{4-i}
, hence
b_{0}=b_{4}=1
and
b_{1}=b_{3}=2q
. Moreover, since
NS(S)\subseteq H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
, one has
\rho\leqslant b_{2}
. We denote by
e(S)
(or simply by
e
) the
Euler-Poincaré characteristic
of
S
.

Recall that Hodge decomposition gives

H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{C}})=H^{2,0}\oplus H^{1,1}\oplus H^{2,0}

with
H^{2,0}=\overline{H^{2,0}}
and
H^{2,0}\simeq H^{0}(\Omega^{2}_{S})
. Hence
b_{2}\geqslant 2p_{g}
.

The
Kodaira dimension

\kappa(S)
of
S
(sometimes simply denoted by
\kappa
) is defined in the following way:

\bullet

\kappa=-\infty
if
P_{n}=0
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
;

\bullet

\kappa=\operatorname{max}\dim\{\varphi_{|nK_{S}|}(S)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in\{0,1,2\}
if there is an
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
P_{n}>0
.

Theorem 2.2
.

If
S
is a surface for which there is an
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
P_{n}>0
, then
\kappa(S)=k
if and only if there are two positive real numbers
a,b
and there is an
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
one has

an^{k}\leqslant h^{0}(nn_{0}K_{S})\leqslant bn^{k}.

For the proof, see
[
13
, Thm. 10.2]
.

Note that the group
\operatorname{Pic}(S)
is multiplicative the group operation being the tensor product of line bundles, whereas
\operatorname{Num}(S)
is additive. We will often abuse notation and use the additive notation also for the elements of
\operatorname{Pic}(S)
, i.e., for line bundles. For example we will denote by
nK_{S}
the pluricanonical bundles.

2.3.
The ramification formula

A local computation proves the following:

Theorem 2.3

(Ramification Formula)
.

Let
X,Y
be smooth, irreducible, projective varieties of the same dimension
n
and let
f:X\to Y
be a surjective morphism. Then

K_{X}=f^{*}(K_{Y})+R

where
R
is the locus where
df
has rank smaller than
n
, with its natural scheme structure.

It should be remarked that a classical result by Zariski–Nagata, called the
purity of the branch locus theorem
, proves that in the set up of the above theorem
R
is a divisor, called the
ramification divisor
of
f
. The divisor
B:=f(R)
is called the
branch divisor
of
f
.

The
Riemann–Hurwitz formula
is the particular case of the above theorem in the case in which
X
and
Y
are curves.

2.4.
Basic formulas

Adjunction formula
says that if
C
is a curve on the surface
S
, then

K_{S}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(C)_{|C}\simeq\omega_{C}

where
\omega_{C}
is the
dualizing sheaf
of
C
. In particular

(K_{S}+C)\cdot C=2p_{a}(C)-2.

The
arithmetic genus
of a curve
C
is defined as

p_{a}(C)=1-\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})-h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})+1.

One has
p_{a}(C)\geqslant 0
as soon as
C
is
algebraically connected
, i.e., if
h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})=1
. Note that if
C
is reduced and topologically connected, then it is also algebraically connected.
The
geometric genus

p_{g}(C)
, also denoted by
g(C)
(or simply by
g
if there is no danger of confusion), of a reduced curve
C
is the arithmetic genus of the normalization of
C
. One has
p_{g}(C)\geqslant 0
if
C
is irreducible and one may have
p_{g}(C)<0
only if
C
is reducible.

Noether’s formula
says that

(2)

12\chi(S)=K^{2}_{S}+e(S).

Riemann–Roch Theorem
says that if
L
is a line bundle on
S
, then

\chi(L):=h^{0}(L)-h^{1}(L)+h^{2}(L)=\chi(S)+\frac{L\cdot(L-K_{S})}{2}.

Serre duality
says that

h^{i}(L)=h^{2-i}(K_{S}-L),\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leqslant i\leqslant 2.

Riemann–Roch Theorem for vector bundles on a smooth irreducible curve
C
of genus
g
, says that if
\mathcal{E}
is a rank
k
vector bundle on
C
, one has

\chi(\mathcal{E})=h^{0}(\mathcal{E})-h^{1}(\mathcal{E})=\deg(\mathcal{E})+k(1-g),

where
\deg(\mathcal{E}):=\deg(\wedge^{k}\mathcal{E})
.

As a general reference, see
[
12
, Chapt. V, §1]
and
[
12
, App. A, §4]
.

2.5.
Ample line bundles

A line bundle
L
on
S
is
ample
if there is a positive multiple
nL
of
L
which is
very ample
, i.e., the map
\phi_{nL}
determined by
|nL|
is an isomorphism of
S
onto its image.

Theorem 2.4

(Nakai–Moishezon Theorem)
.

A line bundle
L
on
S
is ample if and only if
L^{2}>0
and
L\cdot C>0
for every non–zero curve on
S
.

Theorem 2.5

(Kleiman’s Criterion)
.

A line bundle
L
on
S
is ample if and only if
L\cdot C>0
for every
C\in\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)-\{0\}
.

For the proofs, see
[
15
, Chapt. 1, §1.2 B and Thm. 1.4.23]
.

A line bundle
L
on
S
is said to be
nef
if
L\cdot C\geqslant 0
for every curve on
X
. Note that
L
is nef if and only if
L\cdot C\geqslant 0
for every
C\in\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)
. From Kleiman’s Criterion follows that if
N
is ample and
L
is nef, then
L+N
is ample. This implies that if
L
is nef, then
L^{2}\geqslant 0
. Indeed, if
N
is ample, for any positive rational number
\varepsilon
one has that the class of
L+\varepsilon N
in
NS(S)
is ample, hence
(L+\varepsilon N)^{2}>0
and letting
\varepsilon
go to 0, proves our assertion.

Theorem 2.6

(Kodaira Vanishing Theorem)
.

If
L
is an ample line bundle on a smooth projective variety
X
of dimension
n
, then

h^{i}(K_{X}+L)=0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

For the proof, see
[
15
, Chapt. 5, §4.2]
.

There is a powerful generalization of Kodaira Vanishing Theorem which is useful to record. Let
X
be a smooth projective variety of dimension
n
and
L
a line bundle on
X
. As in the surface case,
L
is said to be
nef
if for any curve
C
on
X
one has
L\cdot C\geqslant 0
. Moreover,
L
is said to be
big
if, in addition,
L^{n}>0
.

Theorem 2.7

(Kawamata–Viehweg Vanishing Theorem)
.

If
L
is a big and nef line bundle on
X
, then

h^{i}(K_{S}+L)=0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

For the proof, see
[
14
,
19
]
.

2.6.
Hodge Index Theorem

Theorem 2.8

(Hodge Index Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a surface. The intersection product on
\operatorname{Num}(S)
has signature
(1,\rho-1)
. In particular, if
D,E
are divisor classes such that
D^{2}>0
, then

\ D^{2}\cdot E^{2}\leqslant(D\cdot E)^{2}

and equality holds if and
D
and
E
are numerically equivalent.

For the proof, see
[
12
, Chapt. V, Thm. 1.9]
.

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
S
,
X
be algebraic surfaces and
f:S\to X
a morphism. If
E
is a curve on
S
contracted to a point by
f
, i.e.,
f(E)
is a point of
X
, then
E^{2}<0
.

Proof.

Let
A
be a very ample line bundle on
X
and set
H=f^{*}(A)
. Then we have
H^{2}>0
and
H\cdot E=0
. By the Hodge Index Theorem we have
E^{2}\leqslant 0
and equality cannot hold because
E
is not numerically equivalent to
H
. ∎

2.7.
Blow–up

Let
S
be a smooth surface and
x\in S
a point. The
blow–up
of
S
at
x
is a morphims
\pi:\widetilde{S}\to S
with a curve
E\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
such that
E^{2}=-1
(called the
exceptional
(-1)
–curve of the blow–up
such that
\pi(E)=x
and
\pi:S-E\to S-\{x\}
is an isomorphism, hence
\pi
is a birational morphism. It enjoies the following
universal property
: For any morphism
f:X\to S
such that the schematic counterimage of
x\in S
in
X
is a divisor, there is a morphism
g:X\to\widetilde{S}
such that
f=\pi\circ g
(see
[
12
, Chapt. II, Thm. 7.14]
).

Basic properties of the blow–up
\pi:\widetilde{S}\to S
are the following
[
12
, Chapt. V, Prop. 3.2, 3.3, 3.4, 3.5]
:

\bullet

\operatorname{Pic}(\widetilde{S})=\operatorname{Pic}(S)\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}[E]
;

\bullet

if
C,D\in\operatorname{Pic}(S)
, then
\pi^{*}(C)\cdot\pi^{*}(D)=C\cdot D
;

\bullet

if
C\in\operatorname{Pic}(S)
, then
\pi^{*}(C)\cdot E=0
;

\bullet

K_{\widetilde{S}}=\pi^{*}(K_{S})+E
;

\bullet

\chi(S)=\chi(\widetilde{S})
;

\bullet

P_{n}(S)=P_{n}(\widetilde{S})
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, hence
\kappa(S)=\kappa(\widetilde{S})
;

\bullet

q(S)=q(\widetilde{S})
.

Theorem 2.10

(Castelnuovo’s Contractibility Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a surface and
E
a smooth, irreducible curve on
S
. Then there is a morphism
\pi:S\to X
, with
X
a smooth surface and a point
x\in X
such that
S
is the blow–up of
X
at
x
with exceptional divisor
E
if and only if
E\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
E^{2}=-1
, or equivalently
E^{2}=K_{S}\cdot E=-1
.

For the proof, see
[
12
, Chapt. V, Thm. 5.7]
.

2.8.
Rational and birational maps

Let
S,S^{\prime}
be smooth, irreducible, projective surfaces. A birational map
f:S\dasharrow S^{\prime}
is determined by an isomorphism between two Zariski open subsets of
S
and
S^{\prime}
.
Then we can consider the
set of definition
of
f
, which is the maximal open subset of
S
where
f
is well definied, i.e., it is a morphism. The complement of the set of definition consists of finitely many points of
S
, called the
indeterminacy points
of
f
.

The following main properties of birational maps have to be recalled:

\bullet

the indeterminacy locus of a birational map consists of isolated points (see
[
3
, Rappel II.4]
;

\bullet

if
f:S\to S^{\prime}
is a birational morphism (i.e., the indeterminacy set is empty), then
f
is the composite of finitely many blow–ups (see
[
3
, Thm. II.7]
);

\bullet

[Resolution of indeterminacies] if
f:S\dasharrow S^{\prime}
is a birational map, then there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}}

with
X
a surface and
p,q
birational morphisms (see
[
3
, Thm. II.7]
;

\bullet

accordingly, if
f:S\dasharrow S^{\prime}
is a birational map, then
f
is composite of finitely many blow–ups and inverse of blow–ups.

By taking into account the properties of blow–ups, we have that, if
f:S\dasharrow S^{\prime}
is a birational map, one has:

\bullet

q(S)=q(S^{\prime})
;

\bullet

P_{n}(S)=P_{n}(S^{\prime})
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, hence
\kappa(S)=\kappa(S^{\prime})
;

\bullet

\chi(S)=\chi(S^{\prime})
.

If
f:S\dasharrow C
ia a dominant rational map, where
S
is a surface and
C
a smooth curve, then we still have the resolution of the indeterminacies, i.e., a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C}

where
p
and
q
are rational morphisms, the former one birational. An important remark is the following: if
C
is not rational, then
f
has no indeterminacies. Indeed, assuming that
p
is composed with the minimal number of blow–ups, we see that the exceptional curve of the last blow–up has to be mapped dominantly on
C
, because it cannot be contracted to a point. This proves that
C
is unirational, hence rational, by Lüroth’s theorem.

2.9.
The relative canonical sheaf

Let
S
be a surface,
C
a smooth projective curve, and
f:S\to C
a surjective morphism with connected fibres of genus
g
. Consider the
relative canonical line bundle

\omega_{S|C}=K_{S}\otimes f^{*}(K_{C}^{\vee})

and its image

f_{*}\omega_{S|C}=f_{*}(K_{S})\otimes K_{C}^{\vee}

which is a rank
g
vector bundle on
C
.

Theorem 2.11
.

In the above setting, if
f
is relatively minimal, i.e., no fibre of
f
contains a
(-1)
–curve, the singular fibres of
f
are reduced and have at most nodes as singularities, one has

\deg(f_{*}\omega_{S|C})>0

unless
f
is
isotrivial
, i.e., unless the fibres of
f
are all isomorphic.

For the proof, see
[
2
, Theorem (17.3)]
.

2.10.
Cones

A
cone
in a euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a set
{\mathcal{C}}
such that if
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}
then also
\lambda x+\mu y\in{\mathcal{C}}
, for all
\lambda,\mu\geqslant 0
. Note that a cone is convex. The cone will be said to be
strictly convex
if it contains no non–zero vector subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

A hyperplane of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, with equation
f(x)=0
, will be said to be a
supporting hyperplane
of
{\mathcal{C}}
if
f(z)\geqslant 0
for all
z\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
A
face

F
of
C
is the intersection of
{\mathcal{C}}
with a supporting hyperplane, i.e., there is a supporting hyperplane with equation
f(x)=0
such that
F=\{z\in{\mathcal{C}}:f(z)=0\}
. A
ray
is a half–line of the form
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}x
, for
x\in{\mathcal{C}}-\{0\}
. A ray
R
is said to be
extremal
if it is a face, in which case clearly
x+y\in R
implies that
x,y\in R
.

A cone
{\mathcal{C}}
is said to be
polyhedral
if it is the convex one generated by finitely many rays, i.e., if there are rays
R_{1},\ldots,R_{h}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}=R_{1}+\ldots+R_{h}
.

2.11.
Complete intersections

A more general form of
adjunction formula
says that if
X
is a smooth variety of dimension
n
and if
Y
is an effective divisor on
X
, then

\omega_{Y}\simeq(\omega_{X}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(Y))_{|Y},

where
\omega_{X}
and
\omega_{Y}
are the dualizing sheaves of
X
and
Y
.

If
n\geqslant 3
, if
Y
is smooth and
L:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}(Y)
is ample (or even big and nef), from the exact sequence

0\to L^{\vee}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\to 0

and by Kodaira Vanishing Theorem (or by Kawamata–Viehweg Vanishing Theorem), we have

0=H^{1}(L^{\vee})\to H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\to H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})\to H^{2}(L^{\vee})=0

hence
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
.

Iterated application of the adjunction formula implies that if
Y\subset X
is the
n-h
dimensional, complete intersection of divisors
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{h}
, one has

\omega_{Y}\simeq(\omega_{X}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(Y_{1}+\ldots+Y_{h}))_{|Y}.

Similarly, iterated application of the Kodaira Vanishing Theorem tells us that if
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{h}
are ample and if
Y
is smooth of dimension at least 2, then
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
.

Since
\omega_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(-n-1)
, if
Y
is the complete intersection of dimension
n-h
of hypersurfaces of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
of degrees
d_{1},\ldots,d_{h}
(in this case
Y
is said to be a complete intersection of
type

(d_{1},\ldots,d_{h})
), one has

\omega_{Y}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(d_{1}+\cdots+d_{h}-n-1).

Since
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}})=0
, if
Y
is a smooth complete intersection of dimension at least 2, then
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})=0
.

Let now
X
be a surface of degree
d
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
, with
ordinary singularities
, i.e., with at most the following singularities: a curve
\Gamma
of double points generically the transverse intersection of two branches, with at most finitely many pinch points, with
\Gamma
having at most finitely many triple points as singularities, with three independent tangent lines, which are triple points also for
X
. Then the normalization
\nu:S\to X
is smooth. A suitable application of the adjunction formula implies that the canonical bundle
\omega_{S}
of
S
is the pull–back to
S
of
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma|{\mathbb{P}}^{3}}(d-4)
, off the pull back of
\Gamma
on
S
. This implies that for any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, there is a map

r_{n}:H^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma|{\mathbb{P}}^{3}}(n))\to H^{0}(\omega_{S}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(n-d+4))

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(m):=\nu^{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(m))
for any
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The map
r_{n}
is surjective for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

For this, see
[
21
, Mumford’s appendix to Chapt. III]
.

2.12.
Stein factorization

Let
f:X\to Y
be a dominant projective morphism of noetherian schemes. Then there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}}

where
f^{\prime}
is a projective morphism with connected fibres and
g
is a finite morphism.

See
[
12
, Chapt. III, Cor. 11.5]
.

2.13.
Abelian varieties

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
A
be a complex torus,
X
a variety and
f:X\to A
an étale cover. Then
X
is a complex torus.

Proof.

Let
n
be the dimension of
A
and
X
. The étale cover
f
corresponds to a subgroup
G
of finite index of
\pi_{1}(A)=H_{1}(A,{\mathbb{Z}}):=\Lambda\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}^{2n}
. Then
A\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{n}/\Lambda
. Hence
X\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{n}/G
. ∎

Theorem 2.13

(Poincaré Complete Reducibility Theorem)
.

Let
X
be an abelian variety and
Y
an abelian subvariety. Then there is an abelian subvariety
Z
of
X
such that
Y\cap Z
is finite and
Y+Z=X
, i.e., there is an isogeny

(y,z)\in Y\times Z\to y+z\in X.

Moreover there is a morphism
\phi:X\to Z
such that
Y
is contained in a fibre of
\phi
.

For the proof, see
[
17
, p. 173]
.

2.14.
The Albanese variety

Let
X
be a smooth, projective, irreducible variety. We set
q(X):=h^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{X})
(also simply denoted by
q
), which is the
irregularity
of
X
.

There is an injection

\int:H_{1}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{X})^{\vee}

sending a cycle
\gamma
to the linear map

\int_{\gamma}:\omega\in H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{X})\to\int_{\gamma}\omega\in{\mathbb{C}}.

The injection
\int
realizes
H_{1}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})
as a lattice of maximal rank in
H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{X})^{\vee}
, in particular
b_{1}(X)=2q(X)
. The
q
–dimensional complex torus

\operatorname{\rm Alb}(X):=\frac{H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{X})^{\vee}}{H_{1}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})}

is called the
Albanese variety
of
X
.

Fix a point
p\in X
. Then we have the well defined morphism

\alpha_{X}:x\in X\to\int_{p}^{x}\in\frac{H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{X})^{\vee}}{H_{1}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})}=\operatorname{\rm Alb}(X)

which is called the
Albanese morphism
of
X
with base point
p
, often simply denoted by
\alpha
. By changing the base point
p
,
\alpha_{X}
changes by a translation in
\operatorname{\rm Alb}(X)
. The Albanese morphism enjoyes the following
universal property
: for every complex torus
A
and for every morphism
\beta:X\to A
, there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\operatorname{\rm Alb}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{A}

Moreover the map

\alpha^{*}:H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{\operatorname{\rm Alb}(X)})\to H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{X})

is an isomorphism. This implies that
\alpha(X)
spans
\operatorname{\rm Alb}(X)
as a complex torus.

If
X=C
is a curve, then
\operatorname{\rm Alb}(C)
is denoted by
J(C)
and called the
jacobian variety
of
C
and the map
\alpha_{C}
is an embedding in this case.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
X,Y
be smooth, irreducible, projective varieties and
f:X\to Y
a surjective morphism. Then
q(X)\geqslant q(Y)
.

Proof.

By the universal property of the Albanese variety, there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{Y}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{\rm Alb}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\operatorname{\rm Alb}(Y)\,\,}

Since
\alpha_{Y}(Y)
spans
\operatorname{\rm Alb}(Y)
, then
g
is surjective, hence

q(X)=\dim(\operatorname{\rm Alb}(X))\geqslant\dim(\operatorname{\rm Alb}(Y))=q(Y).

∎

2.15.
Double covers

Let
X
be a variety,
B
be an effective divisor on
X
and
L
a line bundle on
X
with a section
s\in H^{0}(X,L^{\otimes 2})
such that
{\rm div}(s)=B
. Let
T(L)
be the
total space
of
L
with the projection
\pi:T(L)\to X
. Then
s
defines in a natural way a section
\bar{s}
of
\pi^{*}(L^{\otimes 2})
on
T(L)
. Set
Y={\rm div}(\bar{s})\subset T(L)
, with the projection
p=\pi_{|Y}:Y\to X
. We claim that
p:Y\to X
is a finite cover of degree
2
, i.e. a
double cover
, which is
branched
along
B
and
ramified
along
R:=p^{-1}(B)
.

Indeed, let
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a covering of
X
with affine open subsets, in which
L
is defined by the cocycle
h_{ij}\in H^{0}(U_{i}\cap U_{j},{\mathcal{O}}^{*}_{X})
. Then
T(L)
can be covered by open subsets of the form
\{U_{i}\times{\mathbb{C}}\}_{i\in I}
with transition functions
(u_{i},z_{i})\to(u_{i},h_{ij}z_{j})
. The section
s
is locally given by functions
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that
s_{i}=h_{ij}^{2}s_{j}
. Then the section
\bar{s}
is locally given by functions
\{\bar{s}_{i}=z_{i}^{2}-s_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. In fact we have the identity

\bar{s}_{i}=z_{i}^{2}-s_{i}=(h_{ij}z_{j})^{2}-h_{ij}^{2}s_{j}=h_{ij}^{2}\bar{s}_{j}.

Then
Y
is locally defined by the equations
\{z_{i}^{2}=s_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. If we have a point
u_{i}\in U_{i}
, then the counterimage of
u_{i}
via
p:Y\to X
consists of the points
(u_{i},\pm\sqrt{s_{i}(u_{i})})
, thus obtaining two points, unless
s_{i}(u_{i})=0
.
Thus the branch divisor is defined by the equations
\{s_{i}=0\}_{i\in I}
, i.e.,
{\rm div}(s)=B
, and the ramification divisor is defined by the equations
\{z_{i}=0\}_{i\in I}
, i.e.,
R:=p^{-1}(B)
. The above computation also imply that
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(R)=p^{*}(L)
.

Assume now
X
is smooth. Then
Y
is singular exactly at the points of
R
corresponding to singular points of
B
. In particular,
if
B
is non–reduced, then
Y
is non–normal, and its normalization is the double cover of
X
branched along the divisor
B_{\rm red}
.

Note that if
U_{i}={\rm Spec}(A_{i})
, then

p^{-1}(U_{i})={\rm Spec}(B_{i}),\quad\text{with}\quad B_{i}=\frac{A_{i}[z_{i}]}{(z_{i}^{2}-s_{i})},

hence

(3)

B_{i}=A_{i}\oplus z_{i}A_{i}

as an
A_{i}
–module. This tells us that

p_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}\oplus L^{\vee}.

Indeed
p_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
is clearly a rank 2 vector bundle on
X
. There is an obvious injection

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\hookrightarrow p_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
, which gives rise to the sequence

(4)

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to p_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})\to F\to 0

where
F
is a line bundle. From (
3
) we see that (
4
) splits: indeed
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\hookrightarrow p_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
corresponds to the inclusion
A_{i}\hookrightarrow B_{i}=A_{i}\oplus z_{i}A_{i}
in (
3
), and the projection

B_{i}=A_{i}\oplus z_{i}A_{i}\to A_{i}
is locally well definied and glue in order to give a surjection

p_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
splitting (
4
). Moreover, by (
3
),
F
is locally defined by sections
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that

\sigma_{i}z_{i}=\sigma_{j}z_{j}
. Hence

\sigma_{i}z_{i}=\sigma_{j}z_{j}=\sigma_{j}(h_{ij}^{-1}z_{i}),\quad\text{i.e.,}\quad\sigma_{i}=h_{ij}^{-1}\sigma_{j},

which proves that
F=L^{\vee}
.

Note that, by the ramification formula, one has

K_{Y}=p^{*}(K_{X})+R=p^{*}(K_{X}\otimes L).

Then the projection formula implies that

p_{*}(K_{Y})=K_{X}\oplus(K_{X}\otimes L).

In particular we have

p_{g}(Y)=h^{0}(K_{Y})=h^{0}(K_{X})+h^{0}(K_{X}\otimes L)=p_{g}(X)+h^{0}(K_{X}\otimes L)

and

(5)

q(Y)=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})+h^{1}(L^{\vee})=q(X)+h^{1}(L^{\vee}).

Example 2.15
.

In the case
B=0
, we have an
étale double cover

p:Y\to X
, which is never ramified. Such a cover is determined by a non–trivial line bundle
L
on
X
such that
L^{\otimes 2}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, i.e., by a non–trivial element of order 2 in
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
.

2.16.
Riemann’s Existence Theorem

We will give two version of this theorem, which are not the most general, but are sufficient for our applications.

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
be distinct points of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and let
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}
be permutations of the set
\{1,\ldots,m\}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, such that:

\bullet

\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}
generate a subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
transitive over

\{1,\ldots,m\}
;

\bullet

\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n}={\rm id}
.

Then there is a smooth, irreducible curve
C
, uniquely defined up to isomorphisms, and a
morphism
f:C\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
of degree
m
, with branch points
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
and local monodromy around
p_{i}
given by
\sigma_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
be distinct points of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, let
G
be a finite group of order
m
and let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
elements of
G
of order
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}
respectively, such that:

\bullet

g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
generate
G
;

\bullet

g_{1}\cdots g_{n}=1
.

Then there is a unique irreducible curve
C
with a
G
action such that
C/G={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and the quotient morphism
f:C\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is ramified at
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
and over
p_{i}
there are
\frac{m}{m_{i}}
points, each with ramification index
m_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

In the set up of the above theorem,
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
are also called the
local monodromies
at
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, respectively.

For informations on this classical subject, see
[
21
, Abhyankhar’s Appendix 1 to Chapt. VIII]
.

2.17.
Relative duality

Theorem 2.18

(Relative Duality Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a surface and
f:S\to C
a surjective morphism onto a smooth curve, with connected fibres. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a locally free sheaf on
X
. Then there is a natural isomorphism of sheaves

f_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{S|C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{F})^{\vee}.

For the proof, see
[
2
, Theorem (12.3)]
.

3.
Characterization of the complex projective plane

We start with a theorem which characterizes the complex projective plane
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
S
be an irreducible projective surfaces with
H^{1}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})=0
,
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
and
P_{2}=0
. Then
S
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

Proof.

Since
q=0
and
p_{g}=0
, since
P_{2}=0
, we have
\operatorname{Pic}(S)\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
, so we can choose a generator
L
of
\operatorname{Pic}(S)
such that a multiple
nL
with
n\gg 0
is the class of a very ample line bundle. Hence
L^{2}>0
.

By the hypotheses we have
e=3
and
\chi=1
because
q=0
and
p_{g}=0
. By Noether’s formula (
2
) we have
K^{2}_{S}=9
.

On the other hand
K_{S}\simeq aL
, for some integer
a
, hence
9=K^{2}_{S}=a^{2}L^{2}
, which implies that either
a=\pm 3,L^{2}=1
or
a=\pm 1,L^{2}=9
.

If
a>0
we have

(6)

\chi(2K_{S})=\chi+\frac{2K_{S}\cdot K_{S}}{2}=10.

Since
2K_{S}\simeq K_{S}+aL
, and
aL
is ample because
a>0
, then
h^{i}(2K_{S})=0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 2
, by Kodaira Vanishing Theorem
2.7
. By (
6
) we have
P_{2}=h^{0}(2K_{S})=10
, a contradiction.

So
a<0
. Then

\chi(L)=\chi+\frac{L\cdot(L-K_{S})}{2}=1+\frac{1-a}{2}L^{2}.

If
a=-1,L^{2}=9
, we have
\chi(L)=10
. But

h^{2}(L)=h^{0}(K_{S}-L)=h^{0}(-2L)=0,\quad h^{1}(L)=h^{1}(K_{S}-L)=h^{1}(-2L)=0,

so
h^{0}(L)=10

thus
\dim(|L|)\geqslant 9
and all the curves in
|L|
are irreducible and reduced, since
L
is a generator of
\operatorname{Pic}(S)
. Moreover
L\cdot(L+K_{S})=0
, hence the curves in
|L|
have arithmetic genus
1
, thus geometric genus 0 or 1. Let
x
be any point of
S
. We can impose to the curves in
|L|
a triple point at
x
, which imposes at most 6 conditions, and we thus have a linear system
\Sigma
of dimension at least 3. But since a triple point drops the geometric genus by 3, the curves in
\Sigma
must be reducible, a contradiction.

In conclusion we must have
a=-3,L^{2}=1
. Then
\chi(L)=3
. Moreover, as above,
h^{i}(L)=0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 2
, hence
h^{0}(L)=3
, and the linear system
|L|
defines a rational map
\phi:S\dasharrow{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. Recall that any curve in
|L|
is irreducible and
L^{2}=1
. Moreover
L\cdot(L+K_{S})=-2
hence the curves in
|L|
have arithmetic genus 0, thus they are smooth and rational, so isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Let
C\in|L|
. By the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow L_{|C}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(1)\longrightarrow 0

and since
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=0
, the map
H^{0}(L)\to H^{0}(L_{|C})
is surjective, so that each curve in
|L|
is isomorphically mapped to its image in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. It is then clear that
\phi
is a birational morphism mapping the curves in
|L|
to the lines of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. Moreover there is no curve
E
contracted by
\phi
because then we would have
L\cdot E=0
, impossible because
L
generates
\operatorname{Pic}(S)
. In conclusion
\phi
is an isomorphism. ∎

4.
Minimal models

Recall that, if we blow–up a smooth surface
X
at a point
x
we get
\pi:S\to X
, with an
exceptional divisor

E\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
on
S
which is contracted to
x
and
\pi
induces an isomorphism between
S-E
and
X-\{x\}
. One has
E^{2}=-1
and
K_{S}\cdot E=-1
by the adjunction formula. Such a curve is called a
(-1)
–curve
.
Conversely, by Castelnuovo Contractibility Theorem
2.10
, an irreducible curve
E
on a surface
S
is the exceptional divisor of a blow–up if
E^{2}=-1
and
K_{S}\cdot E=-1
.

Example 4.1
.

If we blow–up two distinct points of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
we find a surface
S
on which there are two (-1)–curves which are blown–down to the two points. There is another (-1)–curve though, which is the strict transform
\ell
on
S
of the line joining the two points.

Similarly, blow–up
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
at a point
x
, then blow–up the resulting surface at a point
y
on the exceptional divisor
E
which is blown–down to
x
. The point
y
is called an
infinitely near point
to
x
(see §
16.1
below). On the resulting surface
S^{\prime}
we have a (1)–curve
E_{2}
which is contracted to
y
, and the strict transform
E_{1}
of
E
, which is smooth, rational, with
E_{1}^{2}=-2
(such a curve is called a
(-2)–curve
). Consider the unique line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
passing through
x
and having the tangent direction corresponding to the infinitely near point
y
. The proper transform
\ell
of this line on
S^{\prime}
is also a (-1)–curve.

If we blow–up five general point of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, we find a surface
S^{\prime\prime}
with five (-1)–curves, i.e., the five exceptional divisors of the blow–ups. The proper transform of the unique conic of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
passing through the blown–up points is also a
(-1)
–curve.

A smooth, projective surface
S
is said to be
minimal
, if
S
does not contain
(-1)
–curves.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
S
be a smooth, projective surface. There is a birational morphism
f:S\to X
with
X
minimal.

Proof.

The morphism
f:S\to X
is a sequence of blow–downs of
(-1)
–curves. This process terminates reaching a minimal surface because if
f:S\to X
is the blow–up of a smooth point, then

0\leqslant\dim(H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{R}}))\leqslant\dim(H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{R}}))-1.

∎

The surface
X
in the statement of Proposition
4.2
is called
minimal
or a
minimal model
of
S
.

Remark 4.3
.

The minimal model is in general not unique. If we blow down the (-1)–curve
\ell
on the surface
S
in Example
4.1
, we get a surface which is minimal, but not isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, which is also a minimal model.

Similarly, if we blow down the curve
\ell
on the surface
S^{\prime}
in Example
4.1
.

5.
Ruled surfaces

Let
S
be a surface and
C
a smooth, irreducible projective curve. Suppose there is a surjective morphism
f:S\to C
. Then
S
is said to be
ruled
over
C
if there is a point
x\in C
such that the (schematic) fibre
F_{x}=f^{*}(x)
of
f
over
x
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose that
f:S\to C
is ruled over
C
. Then there is a non–empty open subset
U
of
C
such that for all points
t\in U
the fibre
F_{t}=f^{*}(t)
of
f
over
t
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in C
be a point such that the fibre
F_{x}=f^{*}(x)
of
f
over
x
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Then
f
is smooth over
x
and we can find an open neighborhood
U
of
x
such that
f
is smooth over
U
. So for all
t\in U
, the fibre
F_{t}
is smooth and irreducible. Moreover,
f
is also flat over
U
, hence the genus of the fibres over the points of
U
is constant (see
[
12
, Chapt. III, Cor. 9.10]
), i.e., for all
t\in U
, the fibre
F_{t}
is smooth of genus 0, hence it is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. ∎

Theorem 5.2

(Noether–Enriques’ Theorem)
.

Suppose
f:S\to C
is ruled over
C
. Then there is an dense open subset
U
of
C
such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{f^{-1}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{U\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}}
\textstyle{U\,\,}

In particular
S
is birational to
C\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, and if
C\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, then
S
is
rational
, i.e., birational to
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

Proof.

If
F
is a general fibre of
f
, one has
F^{2}=0
, hence by adjunction formula, we have
K_{S}\cdot F=-2
, thus
p_{g}=0
.

Claim 5.3
.

There is a divisor
H
on
S
such that
H\cdot F=1
.

Proof of Claim
5.3
.

Since
p_{g}=0
, we have
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=0
. From the exponential sequence (
1
),
we have a surjective map
\operatorname{Pic}(S)=H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}^{*})\to H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. So it suffices to find a class
h\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
such that
h\cdot f=1
, where
f
is the class of
F
in
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.

Consider the map

a\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\to a\cdot f\in\mathbb{Z}

whose image is an ideal
(d)
in
\mathbb{Z}
, where
d>0
. Hence the map

a\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\to\frac{a\cdot f}{d}\in\mathbb{Z}

is a linear form on
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Poincaré duality implies that the cup product

H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\otimes H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\to H^{4}(X,\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}

induces a map

H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\to{\rm Hom}(H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})

that is surjective and its kernel is the torsion subgroup of
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Therefore there is
f^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
such that

a\cdot f^{\prime}=\frac{a\cdot f}{d},\quad\text{for all}\quad a\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}).

Hence

df^{\prime}=f,\quad\text{modulo torsion}.

Let
k
be the class of
K_{S}
in
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. We have

f^{2}=0,f\cdot k=-2,\quad\text{hence}\quad f^{\prime 2}=0,k\cdot f^{\prime}=\frac{k\cdot f}{d}=-\frac{2}{d},

and, since
(k+f^{\prime})\cdot f^{\prime}
has to be even, this implies
d=1
, proving the claim. ∎

Let now
U
be a dense open subset of
C
over which all fibres of
f
are isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
(see Lemma
5.1
). Set
f_{|U}=\phi
and consider
{\mathcal{E}}=\phi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{\phi^{-1}(U)}(H))
, which is a rank 2 vector bundle over
U
. Up to shrinking
U
we may assume that
{\mathcal{E}}
is trivial over
U
. Now, by applying Proposition
2.1
(with
Y=p^{-1}(U)
and
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\phi^{-1}(U)}(H)
), we have an epimorphism of sheaves
\phi^{*}({\mathcal{E}})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\phi^{-1}(U)}(H)
, which determines a map
p^{-1}(U)\to{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})=U\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
which is clearly an isomorphism. ∎

The surface
S
, endowed with a surjective morphism
f:S\to C
is said to be a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle
or a
scroll
over
C
, if every fibre of
f
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. These fibres are also called
rulings
of
S
. A
section
of the scroll
S
is an irreducible curve

\Gamma
on
S
which meets in one point the fibres of
f
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
f:S\to C
be a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle over
C
. Then there is a rank 2 vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
on
C
with an isomorphism
\psi:S\to{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E})
which makes the following diagram commutative

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm id}}
\textstyle{C\,\,}

where
p
is the obvious projection morphism.

Moreover two vector bundles
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
give rise to the same
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle as above if and only if there is a line bundle
L
such that
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{E}}\otimes L
.

Proof.

By Noether–Enriques’ theorem,
S
is locally trivial, so it is given by a 1–cocycle with values in
{\rm PGL}(2,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})
. From the exact sequence of groups

1\to\mathbb{C}^{*}\to{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{C})\to{\rm PGL}(2,\mathbb{C})\to 1

we deduce the exact sequence of sheaves in groups

1\to{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{*}\to{\rm GL}(2,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})\to{\rm PGL}(2,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})\to 1

hence the exact sequence

(7)

\operatorname{Pic}(C)\simeq H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{*})\to H^{1}({\rm GL}(2,{\mathcal{O}}_{C}))\to H^{1}({\rm PGL}(2,{\mathcal{O}}_{C}))\to H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{*})=1,

because
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{*})=1
. In fact we have the exact sequence

1\to{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{*}\to K_{C}^{*}\to{\rm Div}(C)\to 0

where
K_{C}^{*}
is the sheaf of non–zero rational functions on
C
and
{\rm Div}(C)
is the sheaf of germs of divisors on
C
: the sections of
{\rm Div}(C)
over an open subset
U
of
C
is the set of all maps
\nu:U\to\mathbb{Z}
such that
\nu(p)\neq 0
for only finitely many
p\in U
. The sheaves
K_{C}^{*}
and
{\rm Div}(C)
are fine sheaves, and this implies that
H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{*})=1
.

Finally the assertion follows right away from (
7
).∎

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface with a surjective morphism
f:S\to C
, with
C
a curve, such that the general fibre of
f
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Then
S
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle over
C
.

Proof.

Let
F
be a fibre of
f
. We have
F^{2}=0
,
K_{S}\cdot F=-2
. If
F
is irreducible it cannot be multiple. Indeed, if
F=mF^{\prime}
with
m>1
, then we have
-2=K_{S}\cdot F=m(K_{S}\cdot F^{\prime})
, hence
m=2
and
K_{S}\cdot F^{\prime}=-1
. On the other hand
F^{\prime 2}=0
which is incompatible with
K_{S}\cdot F^{\prime}=-1
because
(K+F^{\prime})\cdot F^{\prime}
has to be even. So for any irreducible fibre
F
we have that its genus is 0, hence
F\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. To finish the proof we have to show that no fibre
F
can be reducible. Assume by contradiction that
F=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}C_{i}
with
n>1
. Then, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we have

n_{i}C_{i}^{2}=C_{i}\cdot(F-\sum_{j\neq i}m_{j}C_{j})=-\sum_{j\neq i}m_{j}C_{i}\cdot C_{j}<0

because
F
is connected (cf.
[
12
, Chapt. III, Ex. 11.4: Principle of Connectedness]
). Hence
C^{2}_{i}<0
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Since
K_{S}\cdot F<0
, there is a
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
K_{S}\cdot C_{j}<0
. This implies that
K_{S}\cdot C_{j}=C_{j}^{2}=-1
, hence
C_{j}
is a
(-1)
–curve, a contradiction. ∎

We recall the following results (cf.
[
12
, Chapt. III, Prop. 2.6 and Corol. 2.14]
:

Proposition 5.6
.

Given a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}({\mathcal{E}})\to C
, there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between sections
\Gamma
of
f
and quotients
{\mathcal{E}}\twoheadrightarrow F
, with
F
a line bundle. Under this correspondence, if
G=\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{E}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{F}})
, then
G
is a line bundle on
C
and

{\mathcal{O}}_{\Gamma}(\Gamma)\simeq F\times G^{\vee}
.

Theorem 5.7

(Grothendieck–Bellatalla’s Theorem)
.

Every vector bundle on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is the direct sum of line bundles.

As a consequence we have:

Corollary 5.8
.

Any scroll on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is of the form

{\mathbb{F}}_{n}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-n)).

The quotient

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-n)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-n)\to 0

corresponds to a section
C
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
such that
C^{2}=-n
. This is the unique curve with non–positive self intersection of
{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
, except in the case
n=0
in which
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
: in this case there are no curves on
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
with negative self intersection, but the fibres of both projections onto the factors have self intersection 0.

Example 5.9

(Elementary transformations)
.

Let
f:S\to C
be a scroll.
Let
\alpha:T\to S
be the blow–up of
S
at a point
x
. The proper transform of the ruling through
x
is a (-1)–curve, which can be blown–down to a smooth point
y
, via
\beta:T\to S^{\prime}
. The surface

S^{\prime}
a new scroll over
C
, which is birational to
S
, linked to
S
by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm id}
\textstyle{C}

The map
\gamma:S\dasharrow S^{\prime}
is called and
elementary transformation
.

If
S={\mathbb{F}}_{n}
, then
S^{\prime}={\mathbb{F}}_{i}
where
i=n-1
is
x
does not belong to an irreducible section
C
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
with
C^{2}=-n
(this section is unique if
n>0
), whereas
i=n+1
if
x
belongs to such a curve. So
S
has two minimal models
{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
and
{\mathbb{F}}_{n\pm 1}
unless
n=0
. In this case
{\mathbb{F}}_{1}
is not minimal, and to get a minimal model we have to blow–down its (-1)–curve, thus obtaining
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. So if
n=0
,
S
has the two minimal models
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

6.
Surfaces with non–nef canonical bundle

Recall that a line bundle
L
on a surface
S
is said to be
nef
if
L\cdot C\geqslant 0
, for all curves
C
on
S
.
In this section we will prove the following:

Theorem 6.1

(Extremal Contraction Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a smooth, irreducible, projective surface such that
K_{S}
is not nef. Then there is a morphism
\varphi:S\to V
, called an
extremal contraction
, such that:

\bullet

\varphi
is not an isomorphism;

\bullet

if
C
is any irreducible curve on
S
which is contracted to a point by
\varphi
, then
K_{S}\cdot C<0
;

\bullet

if
C,D
are irreducible curves on
S
both contracted to points by
\varphi
, then the classes of
C
and
C
in
NS(S)
are equal, in particular
C\equiv D
;

\bullet

conversely, if
C,D
are irreducible curves on
S
, if
C
is contracted to a point by
\varphi
and if
C\equiv D
, then also
D
is contracted to a point by
\varphi
;

\bullet

\varphi
has connected fibres and
V
is smooth and projective.

The proof is based on the following two results:

Theorem 6.2

(Rationality Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a smooth, irreducible, projective surface such that
K_{S}
is not nef and let
A
be an ample line bundle on
S
. Define

r_{A}:={\rm sup}\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}:A+tK_{S}\quad\mbox{is nef}\,\,\}

(called the
canonical threshold
of
A
, sometimes simply denoted by
r
). Then
r_{A}\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{+}
.

Theorem 6.3

(Base Point Freeness Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a smooth, irreducible, projective surface and let
A
be an ample divisor on
S
. Consider

L=A+rK_{S}\in\operatorname{\rm Div}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Q}}

with
r\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{+}
and
L
nef. Then the linear system
\ell L
is base point free for
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\ell\gg 0
and
\ell
sufficiently divisible so that
\ell r\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

6.1.
Proof of the rationality theorem

We keep the notation introduced above.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose there is a rational number
r_{0}\geqslant r_{A}
such that
k(A+r_{0}K_{S})
is
effective
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, i.e.,
h^{0}(k(A+r_{0}K_{S}))>0
. Then
r_{A}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Proof.

Write
k(A+r_{0}K_{S})\sim\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}D_{i}
, where the
D_{i}
are distinct irreducible curves and
d_{i}
positive integers, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Then we have the following identity in
\operatorname{Pic}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Q}}

K_{S}=-\frac{1}{r_{0}}A+\frac{1}{kr_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}D_{i}

thus

A+tK_{S}=\frac{r_{0}-t}{r_{0}}A+\frac{t}{kr_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}D_{i},\quad\mbox{for any}\quad t<r_{0}.

For all irreducible curves
C
on
S
different from the
D_{i}
s and for all positive
t<r_{0}
one has

(A+tK_{S})\cdot C\geqslant\frac{r_{0}-t}{r_{0}}A\cdot C+\frac{t}{kr_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}D_{i}\cdot C\geqslant 0.

Then for
t\in(0,r_{0})
one has that
A+tK_{S}
is nef if and only if
(A+tK_{S})\cdot D_{j}\geqslant 0
, for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
. This is the case if and only if

\frac{r_{0}-t}{r_{0}}A\cdot D_{j}+\frac{t}{kr_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}D_{i}\cdot D_{j}\geqslant 0,\quad\mbox{for all }\quad 1\leqslant j\leqslant n.

Thus

r_{A}=\operatorname{min}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\{t_{j}:\frac{r_{0}-t_{j}}{r_{0}}A\cdot D_{j}+\frac{t_{j}}{kr_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}D_{i}\cdot D_{j}=0\}

and the minimum is taken over a set of rational numbers, so is rational. ∎

Lemma 6.5
.

If
r
is a positive irrational number, then there are infinitely many pairs
(u,v)
of positive integers such that

0<\frac{v}{u}-r<\frac{1}{3u}

Proof.

Write
r
as an infinite continuous fraction
r=[a_{0};a_{1},\ldots]
so that

r=\lim_{n}c_{n}

where
c_{n}=\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}
are the convergents of the continuous fraction, for which

c_{1}<c_{3}<\cdots<c_{2h+1}<\cdots r\dots<\cdots<c_{2h}<\cdots<c_{4}<c_{2},

see
[
1
, §1.5]
. It is well known that

|c_{n}-c_{n-1}|=\frac{1}{q_{n}q_{n-1}}\quad\mbox{for all }\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

If
n
is even we have

0<c_{n}-r<\frac{1}{q_{n}q_{n-1}}<\frac{1}{3q_{n}}\quad\mbox{if}\quad n\gg 0.

This proves the assertion. ∎

Proof of the Rationality Theorem.

We argue by contradiction: we assume
r_{A}
non–rational and we will prove that there is a rational number
r_{0}
verifying the hypoteses of Lemma
6.4
, leading to a contradiction.

For any pair
(x,y)
of integers, we set

P(x,y):=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(xA+yK_{S}))=\chi+\frac{1}{2}(xA+yK_{S})\cdot(xA+(y-1)K_{S}).

We can interpret
P(x,y)
as a polynomial of degree 2 in
x,y
. This polynomial is clearly not identically 0.

Now choose infinitely many pairs
(u,v)
of positive integers as in Lemma
6.5
, with
r=r_{A}
. For any such a pair,
P(ku,kv)
is a quadratic polynomial in
k
and
P(ku,kv)
is identically zero if and only if the line
vx-uy=0
is contained in the curve
P(x,y)=0
. Since we have infinitely many pairs
(u,v)
as above at our disposal, we can choose
(u_{0},v_{0})
in such a way that
P(ku_{0},kv_{0})
is not identically 0 in
k
. Then there is a
k_{0}\in\{1,2,3\}
such that
P(k_{0}u_{0},k_{0}v_{0})\neq 0
. Set

M=k_{0}(u_{0}A+v_{0}K_{S})=K_{S}+k_{0}u_{0}\Big(A+\frac{k_{0}v_{0}-1}{k_{0}u_{0}}K_{S}\Big).

Note that
A+\frac{k_{0}v_{0}-1}{k_{0}u_{0}}K_{S}
is ample, because so is
A
and moreover

0<\frac{k_{0}v_{0}-1}{k_{0}u_{0}}=\frac{v_{0}}{u_{0}}-\frac{1}{k_{0}u_{0}}\leqslant\frac{v_{0}}{u_{0}}-\frac{1}{3u_{0}}<r_{A}.

Hence, by Kodaira Vanishing Theorem, we have
h^{i}(M)=0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 2
. Therefore we find

h^{0}(M)=\chi(M)=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(k_{0}u_{0}A+k_{0}v_{0}K_{S}))=P(ku_{0},kv_{0})\neq 0.

This implies that we found

r_{0}:=\frac{v_{0}}{u_{0}}>r_{A}\quad\mbox{such that}\quad h^{0}(k_{0}u_{0}(A+r_{0}K_{S})),

as needed. ∎

6.2.
Zariski’s Lemma

Before the proof of the Base Point Freeness Theorem, we need an important preliminary.

Suppose
S
is a surface,
C
is a smooth, irreducible curve and
f:S\to C
a morphism with connected fibres. This is a called a
fibration
or a
pencil
on
S
and the fibres of
f
are called the
curves of the fibration or pencil
,
C
is the
base
of the fibration, and the fibration or pencil is said to be
over

C
. If
C\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, the pencil is
rational
and the curves of the pencil are
linearly equivalent
. Otherwise the pencil is called
irrational
. If the general curve of the fibration is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
[resp. a curve of genus 1], then the fibration is called
rational
[resp.
elliptic
].

If
F
is a curve of a pencil one has
F^{2}=0
. If
F
is a general curve of a pencil, then
F
is smooth and irreducible. There could be however singular and even reducible curves of a pencil.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
F=\sum_{i=1}^{h}n_{i}F_{i}
be a curve of a pencil, with
F_{i}
irreducible and distinct curves and
n_{i}
positive integers, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. If
h>1
then one has
F_{i}^{2}<0
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
.

Proof.

One has

n_{i}^{2}F_{i}^{2}=n_{i}F_{i}\cdot(F-\sum_{j\neq i}^{h}n_{j}F_{j})=-n_{i}F_{i}\cdot\sum_{j\neq i}^{h}n_{j}F_{j}

and the intersection product on the right hand side is positive because
F
is connected.
∎

Theorem 6.7

(Zariski’s Lemma)
.

Suppose
D
is a non–zero curve in
\operatorname{\rm Div}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
{\rm Supp}(D)
is contained in a curve
F
of a pencil with connected fibres. Then
D^{2}\leqslant 0
and equality holds if and only if
D=kF
with
k\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

For the proof, we need the following lemma of linear algebra:

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
Q
be a symmetric bilinear form on
{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}
, determined by the matrix
M_{Q}=(q_{ij})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
, and such that:

(1)

the annihilator of
Q
contains a vector
{\bf z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
, with
z_{i}>0
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
;

(2)

q_{ij}\geqslant 0
for all
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
.

Then
Q
is negative semidefinite and
\Phi:=\{{\bf x}:Q({\bf x},{\bf x})=0\}
is a subspace with dimension equal to the number of connected components of the graph with vertices
\{1,\ldots,n\}
and edges
(i,j)
if
q_{ij}>0
.

Proof.

Set
{\bf x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. One has

(8)

\begin{split}Q({\bf x},{\bf x})&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{ii}x_{i}^{2}+2\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}q_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{ii}x_{i}^{2}+\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}q_{ij}(x_{i}^{2}+x_{j}^{2})=\\
&=\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}q_{ij}x_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{ij}.\end{split}

Up to a base change we may assume that
{\bf z}=(1,\ldots,1)
, which means that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{ij}=0
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Thus we have
Q({\bf x},{\bf x})\leqslant 0
for any
{\bf x}\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}
. Moreover by (
8
) one has

Q({\bf x},{\bf x})=0
if and only if
x_{i}=x_{j}
for all
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n
such that
q_{ij}>0
. The assertion follows.∎

Proof of Zariski’s Lemma.

We can write

F=\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}F_{i}
and
D=\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}F_{i}
, with
F_{1}\ldots,F_{h}
irreducible and distinct curves and with
h_{i}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
and
k_{i}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Consider the subvector space
W
of
\operatorname{Num}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Q}}
generated by the classes of the curves
F_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. The intersection product induces a symmetric bilinear form
Q
on
W
. Then
F
belongs to the annihilator of
W
and
F_{i}\cdot F_{j}\geqslant 0
for
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
. We can apply Lemma
6.8
to this situation. We deduce that
\Phi:=\{E:E^{2}=0\}
is a subspace of dimension equal to the number of connected components of the graph with vertices
\{1,\ldots,n\}
and edges
(i,j)
if
F_{i}\cdot F_{j}>0
. Since
F
is connected, the number of such connected components is 1. Therefore
\Phi
is generated by
F
. So we deduce that
D^{2}\leqslant 0
and that if
D^{2}=0
then
D=kF
with
k\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. ∎

6.3.
Proof of the Base Point Freeness Theorem

In this section we give the:

Proof of the Base Point Freeness Theorem.

Since
L
is nef, we have
L^{2}\geqslant 0
.

Case (a):

L^{2}>0
.

If
L
is ample the assertion is clear. So we may assume
L
is nef but not ample, hence there is an irreducible curve
E
such that

0=L\cdot E=A\cdot E+rK_{S}\cdot E>rK_{S}\cdot E

hence
K_{S}\cdot E<0
. By the Hodge Index Theorem we also have
E^{2}=-1
, hence
E
is a
(-1)
–curve, and we can contract it via a birational morphism
\mu:S\to S^{\prime}
which maps
E
to a point. Since
L\cdot E=0
, we have that
L^{\prime}:=\mu_{*}(L)
is a line bundle. Moreover
L=\mu^{*}(L^{\prime})
and
L^{\prime}
is nef: indeed, for every curve
C^{\prime}
on
S^{\prime}
, we have

L^{\prime}\cdot C^{\prime}=\mu^{*}(L^{\prime})\cdot\mu^{*}(C^{\prime})=L\cdot\mu^{*}(C^{\prime})\geqslant 0.

By considering
L,L^{\prime},A,K_{S},K_{S^{\prime}}
as divisors, we have

L^{\prime}=\mu_{*}(L)=\mu_{*}(A)+rK_{S^{\prime}}

because
K_{S}=\mu^{*}(K_{S^{\prime}})+E
. Moreover
A^{\prime}:=\mu_{*}(A)
is ample. This can be seen by applying Nakai–Moishezon Theorem. In fact
A^{\prime 2}=\mu_{*}(A)^{2}=A^{2}>0
. Moreover, for every curve
C^{\prime}
on
S^{\prime}
, we have

A^{\prime}\cdot C^{\prime}=\mu^{*}(A^{\prime})\cdot\mu^{*}(C^{\prime})=A\cdot\mu^{*}(C^{\prime})>0.

If
L^{\prime}
is ample, then there is a positive integer
\ell
such that
\ell L^{\prime}
is base point free, hence so is
\ell L
. Otherwise
L^{\prime}
is nef but not ample and
L^{\prime 2}=L^{2}>0
. So we can repeat the argument. After finitely many steps we arrive at a birational morphism
f:S\to V
, where
V
is a surface and
S
is obtained from
V
with finitely many blowing–ups of points. Moreover we will have an ample line bundle
M
on
V
such that
L=f^{*}(M)
. Then the same argument as above proves the assertion.

Case (b):

L\equiv 0
.

We have

A+rK_{S}=L\equiv 0\quad\mbox{hence}\quad-K_{S}\equiv\frac{1}{r}A\quad\mbox{is ample}.

Similarly, for all integers
\ell
, we have

\ell L-K_{S}\equiv\frac{1}{r}A\quad\mbox{is ample}.

Hence, by Kodaira Vanishing Theorem we have

h^{i}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=h^{i}(K_{S}-K_{S})=0,\quad\mbox{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant 2

and, for all integers
\ell
for which
\ell L
is a line bundle, we have

h^{i}(\ell L)=h^{i}(\ell L-K_{S}+K_{S})=0,\quad\mbox{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant 2.

Then

h^{0}(\ell L)=\chi(\ell L)=\chi+\frac{1}{2}\ell L\cdot(\ell L-K_{S})=\chi=h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=1

so
\ell L
is effective, but also numerically equivalent to 0. This implies that it is 0, and the assertion follows.

Case (c):

L^{2}=0
and
L\not\equiv 0
.

So there is an irreducible curve
C
such that
L\cdot C>0
. We claim that
L\cdot K_{S}<0
. Indeed, take
h\gg 0
so that
hA-C
is effective. Then

L\cdot(hA)=L\cdot(hA-C)+L\cdot C\geqslant L\cdot C>0

so that
L\cdot A>0
. But then

0=L^{2}=L\cdot(A+rK_{S})=L\cdot A+rL\cdot K_{S}>rL\cdot K_{S}

proving the claim.

For all integers
\ell
we have

\ell L-K_{S}=\ell L-\frac{1}{r}(A-L)=\frac{1}{r}A+\frac{\ell r-1}{r}L

and, if
\ell\gg 0
this is ample, because is the sum of an ample and a nef divisor class. Therefore, for each
\ell\gg 0
such that
\ell L
is a line bundle, we again have
h^{1}(\ell L)=h^{2}(\ell L)=0
. Hence

h^{0}(\ell L)=\chi(\ell L)=\chi+\frac{1}{2}\ell L\cdot(\ell L-K_{S})=\chi-\frac{1}{2}\ell L\cdot K_{S}>0.

Finally we write
|\ell L|=|M|+F
, where
|M|
is the movable part (which is nef) and
F
is the fixed part. We have

0\leqslant M^{2}\leqslant M\cdot(M+F)=\ell L\cdot M\leqslant\ell L\cdot(M+F)=(\ell L)^{2}=0

which implies

M^{2}=M\cdot F=F^{2}=0.

This implies that
|M|
is
composed with
a pencil, i.e., there is a curve
D
and a morphism
f:S\to D

and there is a line bundle
N
on
C
such that
M=f^{*}(N)
. Moreover
M\cdot F=0
means that
F
is contained in a union of fibres of
f
. By Zariski’s Lemma,
F^{2}=0
implies that
F
is proportional, over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, to the sum of full fibres. Then, by taking
\ell
larger and more divisible, one may achieve the situation in which
\ell L
is base points free, as wanted. ∎

Remark 6.9
.

In Case (c) of the above proof we eventually have that the system determined by a high multiple of
L
is composed with a pencil whose general curve we denote by
F
. One has
L\cdot F=0
Moreover, since
K_{S}\cdot L<0
, we have
K_{S}\cdot F<0
and
F^{2}=0
, therefore the general curve
F
of the pencil is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

6.4.
Boundedness of denominators

In this section we prove the following:

Corollary 6.10
.

Same hypotheses as in the Rationality Theorem and set
r=r_{A}
. Then
r=\frac{p}{q}
, with
{\rm GCD}(p,q)=1
and
q\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Proof.

We consider the three cases as in the proof of the Base Point Freeness Theorem.

Case (a):

L^{2}>0
.

In this case there is a
(-1)
–curve
E
such that

0=L\cdot E=(A+rK_{S})\cdot E=A\cdot E-r

hence
q=1
in this case.

Case (c):

L^{2}=0
and
L\not\equiv 0
.

In this case by Remark
6.9
there is an irreducible curve
F
such that
F^{2}=0
,
K_{S}\cdot F=-2
and

0=L\cdot F=(A+rK_{S})\cdot E=A\cdot E-2r

hence
q=2
in this case.

Case (b):

L\equiv 0
.

Assume first that
\rho>1
. Then we can find an ample divisor
A^{\prime}\not\equiv A
and we have
L^{\prime}=A^{\prime}+r^{\prime}K_{S}
as in the Rationality Theorem. If
L^{\prime}\equiv 0
, then we have
rA^{\prime}\equiv-rr^{\prime}K_{S}\equiv r^{\prime}A
, a contradiction. Hence for
L^{\prime}
either Case (a) or Case (c) occur. In the former case there is on
S
a
(-1)
–curve
E
, in the latter a curve
F
such that
F\cdot K_{S}=-2
. Since
L\equiv 0
, hence
L\cdot E=0
or
L\cdot F=0
, we find again
q=1
in the former case,
q=2
in the latter.

Next we assume
\rho=1
. We can choose an ample generator
H
of
\operatorname{Num}(S)
, and we have
-K_{S}\equiv hH
. We claim that
h\in\{1,2,3\}
. Indeed, if
h>3
, for
x=1,2,3
we have

h^{0}(K_{S}+xH)=\chi(K_{S}+xH)=0
, because
h^{i}(K_{S}+xH)=0
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 2
by Kodaira Vanishing Theorem and
K_{S}+xH=(x-h)H
with
x-h<0
. This implies that
P(x):=\chi(K_{S}+xH)
is a polynomial of degree 2 which is identically 0, having the three roots
x=1,2,3
. This is clearly a contradiction. Finally, we have
A=kH
with
k>0
and for any curve
C
we have

0=C\cdot L=A\cdot C+rK_{S}\cdot C=(k-rh)H\cdot C

hence
r=\frac{k}{h}
, proving the assertion. ∎

Remark 6.11
.

In Case (b) of the above proof we have
-K_{S}\equiv\frac{1}{r}A
is ample. Therefore, by Kodaira Vanishing Theorem we have
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=h^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=0
. In particular we have
b_{1}=0
. If
\rho=1
we have also
b_{2}=1
. Moreover, being
-K_{S}
ample, we have also
P_{2}=0
. Then
S={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
by the characterization of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
(see Theorem
3.1
).

6.5.
Proof of the Extremal Contraction Theorem

Now we can give the:

Proof of the Extremal Contraction Theorem.

By keeping the above notation, we have that the linear system
|\ell L|
, with
\ell\gg 0
and highly divisible, is base point free. Hence it defines a morphism
\varphi_{|\ell L|}:S\to V
, where
V
is a projective variety of dimension
\dim(V)\leqslant 2
.

Case
\dim(V)=2
.
We have
L^{2}>0
, so we are in Case (a) of the proof of the Base Point Freeness Theorem. As we saw, in this case there is a
(-1)
–curve
E
such that
L\cdot E=0
. The contraction of
E
is an extremal contraction, as required.

Case
\dim(V)=1
.
We can assume that
V
is smooth. By Stein Factorization we can also assume that the fibres
F
of
S\to V
are connected. We have
F\cdot L=0
and this implies, as we saw above, that
F\cdot K_{S}<0
. Hence
F\cdot K_{S}=-2
and the general fibre
F
is smooth and rational. If all fibres are irreducible, then
S\to V
is the required extremal contraction. Otherwise there is a fibre
F=\sum_{i=1}^{h}n_{i}F_{i}
. If
h=1
, then
n_{1}>1
and we have
F_{1}^{2}=0
and
-2=n_{1}K_{S}\cdot F_{1}
, so that
n_{1}=2
and
K_{S}\cdot F_{1}=-1
, a contradiction, since by Adjunction Formula
F_{1}\cdot(K_{S}+F_{1})
is even. If
h\geqslant 2
, then
F_{i}^{2}<0
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. Moreover, since
K_{S}\cdot F<0
, there is an
i
with
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
such that
K_{S}\cdot F_{i}<0
. Then
F_{i}
is a
(-1)
–curve and its contraction is an extremal contraction.

Case
\dim(V)=0
.
In this case we are in Case (b) of the proof of the Base Point Freeness Theorem. As we saw in the proof of the Boundedness of Denominators, then:

\bullet

either there is a
(-1)
–curve
E
on
S
, and its contraction is an extremal contraction;

\bullet

or there is no
(-1)
–curve but there is a morphism
f:S\to C
, with
C
a smooth curve, with irreducible, smooth and rational fibres, and
f:S\to C
is an extremal contraction;

\bullet

or
\rho=1
, in which case
S\to V
is an extremal contraction as required.

∎

7.
The Cone Theorem

If
S
is a surface, for any class
H\in\operatorname{NS}(S)
we set
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{H\geqslant 0}
for the cone of classes
Z\in\operatorname{NS}(S)
, such that
K_{S}\cdot H\geqslant 0
. Similar notations
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{H>0}
,
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{H\leqslant 0}
,
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{H<0}
have similar meaning.

Theorem 7.1

(The Cone Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a surface. Then

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}\geqslant 0}+\sum_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}R_{\ell}

where:

\bullet

\ell
varies in a countable set
\mathfrak{L}
;

\bullet

for each
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
,
R_{\ell}
is a ray in
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}
, generated by the class of a smooth rational curve
C_{\ell}
such that
0<-C\cdot K_{S}\leqslant 3
;

\bullet

for each
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
, there is a nef line bundle
L_{\ell}
such that
R_{\ell}=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)\cap L_{\ell}^{\perp}
, hence
R_{\ell}
is an
extremal ray
;

\bullet

the rays
R_{\ell}
are
discrete
in
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}>0}
, i.e., for any ample divisor
H
and for all
\varepsilon>0
there are only finitely many
R_{\ell}
s,
R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}
in the cone
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{(K_{S}+\varepsilon H)<0}
and

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{(K_{S}+\varepsilon H)\geqslant 0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}

hence
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)\cap\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{(K_{S}+\varepsilon H)<0}
is polyhedral;

\bullet

for each
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
there is an extremal contraction contracting the curves with class in
R_{\ell}
and viceversa, any extremal contraction is of this type.

Proof.

The proof will be divided in various steps.

7.1.
Step 1 of the proof

In this step we prove the following:

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
M
be a nef divisor class which is not ample, so that
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)\cap M^{\perp}\neq\{0\}
. Assume that there is a face

F:=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M^{\perp}\neq\{0\}

of
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}
. Then there is some nef divisor class
N
such that

R:=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap N^{\perp}\subseteq\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M^{\perp}

is an extremal ray.

Before proving this lemma, we need a lemma of linear algebra:

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
W
be a
{\mathbb{R}}
–vector space of dimension
\rho
. Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{\rho}
be a basis of
W
, let
v\in W
and
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\rho}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then

\dim(\langle v_{i}+\lambda_{i}v\rangle_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\rho})\geqslant\rho-1.

Proof.

Suppose we have a relation of the form

(9)

\sum_{i=1}^{\rho}a_{i}(v_{i}+\lambda_{i}v)=0

Write
v=\sum_{i=1}^{\rho}\alpha_{i}v_{i}
, so that (
9
) becomes

\sum_{i=1}^{\rho}\Big(a_{i}+(\sum_{j=1}^{\rho}a_{j}\lambda_{j})\alpha_{i}\Big)v_{i}=0,

hence we have

a_{i}+(\sum_{j=1}^{\rho}a_{j}\lambda_{j})\alpha_{i}=0,\quad\mbox{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant\rho.

This is a linear system in the
a_{i}
s with matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1+\lambda_{1}\alpha_{1}&\lambda_{2}\alpha_{2}&\ldots&\lambda_{\rho}\alpha_{\rho}\\
\lambda_{1}\alpha_{1}&1+\lambda_{2}\alpha_{2}&\ldots&\lambda_{\rho}\alpha_{\rho}\\
\dots&\ldots&\dots&\ldots\\
\lambda_{1}\alpha_{1}&\lambda_{2}\alpha_{2}&\ldots&1+\lambda_{\rho}\alpha_{\rho}\end{pmatrix}

By subtracting the last row from the others, we find the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\ldots&0&-1\\
0&1&\ldots&0&-1\\
\dots&\ldots&\dots&\ldots&\ldots\\
0&0&\ldots&1&-1\\
\lambda_{1}\alpha_{1}&\lambda_{2}\alpha_{2}&\ldots&\lambda_{\rho-1}\alpha_{\rho-1}&1+\lambda_{\rho}\alpha_{\rho}\end{pmatrix}

whose rank is at least
\rho-1
. The assertion follows.∎

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

The assertion is trivial if
\dim(F)=1
, so we assume
\dim(F)>1
.

Fix an ample divisor class
B
, a non–negative rational number
\nu
, and consider
r_{\nu M+B}
, so that

\nu M+B+r_{\nu M+B}K_{S}
is nef and not ample. We have:

\bullet

r_{\nu M+B}
is non–decreasing in
\nu
. Indeed, if
\nu^{\prime}>\nu
we have

(\nu^{\prime}M+B)+r_{\nu M+B}K_{S}=(\nu^{\prime}-\nu)M+\Big(\nu M+B+r_{\nu M+B}K_{S}\Big)

which is nef, because so are
(\nu^{\prime}-\nu)M
and
\nu M+B+r_{\nu M+B}K_{S}
. This proves that
r_{\nu^{\prime}M+B}\geqslant r_{\nu M+B}
;

\bullet

r_{\nu M+B}
is bounded. Indeed, if we take
Z\in F-\{0\}
, we have

0\leqslant Z\cdot(\nu M+B+r_{\nu M+B}K_{S})=Z\cdot(B+r_{\nu M+B}K_{S})

hence

-(K_{S}\cdot Z)r_{\nu M+B}\leqslant B\cdot Z,\quad\mbox{thus}\quad r_{\nu M+B}\leqslant-\frac{B\cdot Z}{K_{S}\cdot Z}

because
-K_{S}\cdot Z>0
;

\bullet

the denominators of
r_{\nu M+B}
are bounded.

This implies that there is a
\nu_{B}
such that
r_{\nu M+B}
stabilizes to a fixed
r_{M,B}
for
\nu\geqslant\nu_{B}
. Set

M_{B,\nu}:=\nu M+B+r_{M,B}K_{S}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\nu\geqslant\nu_{B}

which is nef but not ample. Then:

\bullet

we have

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M_{B,\nu}^{\perp}\neq\{0\}\quad\mbox{ for}\quad\nu\geqslant\nu_{B}.

Indeed, since
\nu M+B
is ample and
M_{B,\nu}
is nef but not ample, there is
Z\in\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)-\{0\}
such that

0=Z\cdot M_{B,\nu}=Z\cdot(\nu M+B)+r_{M,B}(K_{S}\cdot Z)>r_{M,B}(K_{S}\cdot Z)

hence
K_{S}\cdot Z<0
;

\bullet

for
\nu\geqslant\nu_{B}
one has

\{0\}\neq\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M_{B,\nu}^{\perp}\subseteq\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M^{\perp}=F.

In fact, if
Z\in\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M_{B,\nu}^{\perp}
and since
M_{B,\nu}+B+r_{M,B}K_{S}
is nef, one has

0=Z\cdot M_{B,\nu}=Z\cdot(\nu_{B}M+B+r_{M,B}K_{S})+(\nu-\nu_{B})Z\cdot M\geqslant(\nu-\nu_{B})Z\cdot M\geqslant 0

thus
M\cdot Z=0
;

\bullet

if
\dim(F)\geqslant 2
, we can find an ample divisor class
B
such that the face
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M_{B,\nu_{B}+1}^{\perp}
has smaller dimension. In fact we can choose
B_{1},\ldots,B_{\rho}
ample divisor classes which form a basis of
\operatorname{NS}(S)
. Suppose that

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M_{B_{i},\nu_{B_{i}}+1}^{\perp}=F\quad\mbox{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant\rho.

This implies that all hyperplanes
(B_{i}+r_{M,B_{i}}K_{S})^{\perp}
contain the face
F
. Then apply Lemma
7.2
with
W=\operatorname{NS}(S)^{\vee}
,
v_{i}
the linear map

v_{i}:D\in\operatorname{NS}(S)\to B_{i}\cdot D\in{\mathbb{R}},

\lambda_{i}=r_{M,B_{i}}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant\rho
, and
v
is the linear map

v:D\in\operatorname{NS}(S)\to K_{S}\cdot D\in{\mathbb{R}},

to get a contradiction. In conclusion, there is an
i
such that
1\leqslant i\leqslant\rho
and

\{0\}\neq\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M_{B_{i},\nu}^{\perp}\varsubsetneq F

as wanted.

By iterating this argument the assertion follows.∎

7.2.
Step 2 of the proof

In this step we prove the:

Lemma 7.4
.

Consider the family
\{R_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}
of extremal rays such that for all
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
there is a nef divisor class
L_{\ell}
such that

(10)

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap L_{\ell}^{\perp}=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)\cap L_{\ell}^{\perp}=R_{\ell}

Then

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}\geqslant 0}+\overline{\sum_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}R_{\ell}}.

Proof.

One has
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}\geqslant 0}+\overline{\sum_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}R_{\ell}}\subseteq\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)
. Suppose the inclusion is strict, so that we can find a
Z\in\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)-\Big(\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}\geqslant 0}+\overline{\sum_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}R_{\ell}}\Big)
. Then we can find a divisor class
D
such that
D\cdot Z<0
whereas

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}\geqslant 0}+\overline{\sum_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}R_{\ell}}\subseteq\operatorname{NS}(S)_{D>0}
.

Note that
D^{\perp}\cap K_{S}^{\perp}\cap\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)=\emptyset
. This implies that we can find some negative rational number
a
such that
A:=D+aK_{S}
is an ample divisor class. By the Rationality Theorem we find a nef, not ample, divisor class

M:=A+rK_{S}=D+(a+r)K_{S},\quad\mbox{with}\quad r\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}

such that
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)\cap M^{\perp}\neq\{0\}
. Actually, by an argument we already made, we have that

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M^{\perp}\neq\{0\}
. Then by Step 1, there is some extremal ray
R\subseteq\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap M^{\perp}
. Now notice that
a+r<0
, because

0\leqslant M\cdot Z=D\cdot Z+(a+r)K_{S}\cdot Z<(a+r)K_{S}\cdot Z

and
K_{S}\cdot Z<0
. Next we denote by
C
a generator of the ray
R
, so that

0=M\cdot C=D\cdot C+(a+r)K_{S}\cdot C

hence, being
a+r<0
and
K_{S}\cdot C<0
, we deduce that also
D\cdot C<0
. This is a contradiction, since

\overline{\sum_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}R_{\ell}}\subseteq\operatorname{NS}(S)_{D>0}
. ∎

7.3.
Step 3 of the proof

Next we prove the:

Lemma 7.5
.

Consider the family
\{R_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}
of extremal rays as in the statement of Lemma
7.4
. Then
\{R_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}
is discrete in the half–space

\operatorname{NS}(S)_{K_{S}<0}
. Therefore

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}\geqslant 0}+{\sum_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}R_{\ell}}.

Proof.

For all
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
there is a nef divisor class
L_{\ell}
such that (
10
) holds. Then, as we saw in the proof of Lemma
7.2
, for every ample divisor class
A
one has

\{0\}\neq\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap(L_{\ell})_{A,\nu_{A}+1}^{\perp}\subseteq\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap(L_{\ell})^{\perp}

hence

R_{\ell}=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap(L_{\ell})_{A,\nu_{A}+1}^{\perp}.

Let
C_{\ell}
a generator of
R_{\ell}
. The
C_{\ell}\cdot(L_{\ell})_{A,\nu_{A}+1}=0
implies that

(A+r_{L_{\ell},A}K_{S})\cdot C_{\ell}=0\quad\mbox{hence}\quad r_{L_{\ell},A}=-\frac{A\cdot C_{\ell}}{K_{S}\cdot C_{\ell}}.

Now fix an ample divisor class
H
and an
\varepsilon>0
and look at the rays
R_{\ell}
such that
(K_{S}+\varepsilon H)\cdot C_{\ell}<0
. This means that

-\frac{H\cdot C_{\ell}}{r_{L_{\ell},H}}=K_{S}\cdot C_{\ell}<-\varepsilon H\cdot C_{\ell}\quad\mbox{hence}\quad r_{L_{\ell},H}<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}.

Since the denominators of
r_{L_{\ell},H}
are bounded, we have only finitely many choices for
r_{L_{\ell},H}
, so for
L_{\ell}
, thus for
R_{\ell}
.∎

7.4.
Step 4 of the proof: the Contraction Theorem

In this section we prove the:

Theorem 7.6

(The Contraction Theorem)
.

Consider the family
\{R_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}
of extremal rays as in the statement of Lemma
7.4
. For each
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
, there is an extremal contraction (the
contraction of

R_{\ell}
)

\varphi_{\ell}:S\to V_{\ell}

such that for any curve
C
contracted by
\phi_{\ell}
to a point, one has that
C\in R_{\ell}
.

Conversely, any extremal contraction is a contraction of an extremal ray as above.

Proof.

For all
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
there is a nef divisor class
L_{\ell}
such that (
10
) holds. Then for each
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
we have
L_{\ell}^{\perp}\cap K_{S}^{\perp}\cap\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)=\emptyset
. Hence, if for a given
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
we set
R=R_{\ell}
and
L=L_{\ell}
, we have that
A=L-rK_{S}
is ample, if
r\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>0}
is small enough, and then
r=r_{A}
. So, the assertion follows by the Extremal Contraction Theorem.

Conversely, let
\varphi:S\to V
be an extremal contraction. Let
H
be an ample divisor on
V
. Then
L:=\phi^{*}(H)
is nef, but not ample, and, by the very definition of an extremal contraction, one has

\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)\cap L^{\perp}=\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)_{K_{S}<0}\cap L^{\perp}=R

where
R
is generated by any curve
C
contracted to a point by
\varphi
.∎

7.5.
Step 5: of the proof

In this step we prove that:

Lemma 7.7
.

Consider the family
\{R_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}
of extremal rays as in the statement of Lemma
7.4
. For each
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
, there is a smooth rational curve
C
in
R_{\ell}

such that

1\leqslant-C\cdot K_{S}\leqslant 3.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:S\to V
be the extremal contraction which contracts the curves in
R
. If
\dim(V)=2
we saw in the proof of the Extremal Contraction Theorem that the only irreducible curve contracted by
\varphi
is a
(-1)
–curve
E
, for which
E\cdot K_{S}=-1
. If
\dim(V)=1
we saw that the only irreducible curves contracted by
\varphi
are the fibres
F
of
\varphi
which are smooth rational with
F^{2}=0
and
F\cdot K_{S}=-2
. Finally, if
\dim(V)=0
then
\rho=1
. Then by the argument in Case (b) if the proof of the Boundedness of Denominators and by Remark
6.11
, we see that
S\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and, if
C
is a line one has
L\cdot K_{S}=-3
and
R
is generated by
C
. ∎

The previous steps prove the Cone Theorem. ∎

8.
The minimal model programme

The minimal model programme is an algorithm which, given a surface, returns one of the two items: either a surface
S
birational to the given one with
K_{S}
nef which we will call a
strong minimal model
of the given surface, or a
Mori fibre space
, to be shortly defined, again birational to the given surface.

Given a surface
S
, the algorithm works as follows:

Step 1
: if
K_{S}
is nef, the algorithm stops and we say that
S
is
strongly minimal
. Note that
S
is in particular minimal, because a
(-1)
–curve
E
would be such that
K_{S}\cdot E=-1
, impossible if
K_{S}
is nef.

Step 2
: if
K_{S}
is not nef, then there is an extremal contraction
\varphi:S\to V
. If
\dim(V)<2
, then we say that
\varphi:S\to V
(or simply
S
if no confusion arises) is a
Mori fibre space
and the algorithm stops. If
\dim(V)=2
, then we go to Step 1.

After a finite number of steps the algorithm produces:

\bullet

either a birational morphism
f:S\to S^{\prime}
, composed of blow–downs of
(-1)
