–curves, with
K_{S^{\prime}}
nef, so that
S^{\prime}
is a strong minimal model of
S
;

\bullet

or a birational morphism
f:S\to S^{\prime}
, composed of blow–downs of
(-1)
–curves, and a morphism
\varphi:S^{\prime}\to V
which is a Mori fibre space. Precisely, if
\dim(V)=1
, then
S^{\prime}
is a scroll over the curve
V
, if
\dim(V)=0
, then
S^{\prime}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

Note that the algorithm has a certain amount of freedom in its application, so that its outcome could be not unique. However the outcome is uniquely determined up to birational transformation. We will soon see that different outcomes applied to the same surface cannot be a strong minimal model and a Mori fibre space. However, if the algorithm produces a Mori fibre space, this could be not unique, as the Example
5.9
shows.
By contrast, one has the:

Theorem 8.1

(Uniqueness of the Strong Minimal Model)
.

Let
S
be a strong minimal model. Then if
S^{\prime}
is a smooth, projective surface and
f:S^{\prime}\dasharrow S
a birational map, then it is a morphism.

In particular, if
S,S^{\prime}
are both strong minimal models and
f:S^{\prime}\dasharrow S
is a birational map, then it is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We resolve the indeterminacies of
f

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S}

where
p,q
are sequences of blow–ups. We may assume that in the above diagram the number of blow–ups occurring in
p
is minimal.

If
p
is an isomorphism, we are done. If not, by the ramification formula we have

K_{X}=q^{*}(K_{S})+R=p^{*}(K_{S^{\prime}})+R^{\prime}

with
R
and
R^{\prime}
effective divisors. There is a
(-1)
–curve
E\leqslant R^{\prime}
, i.e., the exceptional divisor of the last blow–up occurring in
p
, and

-1=E\cdot K_{X}=E\cdot(q^{*}(K_{S})+R)\geqslant E\cdot R

because,
K_{S}
being nef, so is
q^{*}(K_{S})
. Hence
E\leqslant R
, hence
E
is contracted by both
p
and
q
. This means that, if
\pi:X\to X^{\prime}
is the blow–down of
E
,
p
and
q
both factor through
\pi
, i.e., there is a diagram

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S}

with
p=p^{\prime}\circ p
and
q=q^{\prime}\circ\pi
. This is a contradiction, since
p^{\prime}
is the composition of one blow–up less than
p
.∎

Remark 8.2
.

If the result of the minimal model programme applied to
S
is a Mori fibre space
\varphi:S^{\prime}\to V
, then we have
\kappa(S)=-\infty
. Indeed,
S
and
S^{\prime}
are birationally equivalent, so that
\kappa(S)=\kappa(S^{\prime})
. Moreover either
S^{\prime}={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
or
S^{\prime}\to V
is a scroll. In either case there are moving curves
C
on
S^{\prime}
such that
C\cdot K_{S^{\prime}}<0
, which yields
P_{n}(S^{\prime})=0
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, so
\kappa(S^{\prime})=-\infty
.

9.
Castelnuovo’s Rationaly Criterion

In this section we prove the fundamental:

Theorem 9.1

(Castelnuovo’s Rationaly Criterion)
.

A surface
S
is rational if and only if
q=P_{2}=0
.

Proof.

Recall that
q
and
P_{2}
are birational invariants. They are both zero for
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. Indeed, if
L
is the class of a line, one has
K_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}}=-3L
and
L
is very ample. Hence all plurigenera vanish and moreover
q({\mathbb{P}}^{2})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}})=h^{1}(3L+K_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}})=0
by Kodaira Vanishing Thoerem. Hence
q
and
P_{2}
are zero for all rational surfaces.

To prove the converse, we will prove that

(11)

q=P_{2}=0\quad\mbox{implies}\quad K_{S}\quad\mbox{not nef}.

Taking this for granted, the theorem follows. Indeed, by applying the minimal model programme to
S
, since
q
and
P_{2}
are birational invariants, we never reach a strong minimal model, so we eventually reach a Mori fibre space
f:S^{\prime}\to V
, with
q(S^{\prime})=P_{2}(S^{\prime})=0
. If
\dim(V)=0
we know that
S^{\prime}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
proving the theorem. If
V
is a smooth curve, one has
g(V)=0
. Indeed, one has an injection
f^{*}:H^{0}(\omega_{V}^{1})\to H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{S})=\{0\}
, so that
g(V)=h^{0}(\omega_{V}^{1})=0
. Then
S^{\prime}
is a scroll over
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, hence it is birational to
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, so that the assertion follows.

To prove (
11
), we note that
P_{2}=0
implies
p_{g}=0
and, since
q=0
, then
p_{a}=0
, so that
\chi=1
. Hence, using Serre Duality and Riemann–Roch Theorem, we get

\begin{split}h^{0}(-K_{S})&=h^{0}(-K_{S})+h^{0}(2K_{S})=h^{0}(-K_{S})+h^{2}(-K_{S})\geqslant\\
&\geqslant h^{0}(-K_{S})-h^{1}(-K_{S})+h^{2}(-K_{S})=\chi(-K_{S})=\\
&=\chi+\frac{(-K_{S})(-2K_{S})}{2}=1+K_{S}^{2}.\end{split}

We now argue by contradiction and assume
K_{S}
is nef. Then we have
K_{S}^{2}\geqslant 0
, hence
h^{0}(-K_{S})>0
. If
H
is an ample divisor on
S
we have then
-K_{S}\cdot H>0
, hence
K_{S}\cdot H<0
, a contradiction. This proves (
11
) and the theorem.∎

Corollary 9.2
.

Any unirational surface is rational.

Proof.

If
S
is unirational there is a rational surface
S^{\prime}
and a dominant rational map
f:S^{\prime}\dasharrow S
. By elimination of indeterminacies, we may assume
f
is a morphism. Then we claim that
q(S)=P_{2}(S)=0
. Indeed, since we have an injection
f^{*}:H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{S})\to H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{S^{\prime}})=\{0\}
, we see that
q(S)=h^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{S})=0
. The proof that
P_{2}(S)=0
is similar. Hence
S
is rational. ∎

10.
The Fundamental Theorem of the Classification

The main result of this section is the following:

Theorem 10.1

(Fundamental Theorem of the Classification)
.

Let
S
be a surface. Then:

(a)

the end result of the minimal model programme applied to
S
is a strong minimal model if and only if
\kappa(S)\geqslant 0
;

(b)

the end result of the minimal model programme applied to
S
is a Mori fibre space if and only if
\kappa(S)=-\infty
.

Note that (a) and (b) are equivalent. We already observed that if the end result of the minimal model programme applied to
S
is a Mori fibre space then
\kappa(S)=-\infty
(see Remark
8.2
). This is the same as proving that if
\kappa(S)\geqslant 0
then the end result of the minimal model programme applied to
S
is a strong minimal model. So we are left to prove the:

Theorem 10.2
.

If the end result of the minimal model programme applied to
S
is a strong minimal model then
\kappa(S)\geqslant 0
.

To prove this, we need a few preliminary results of independent interest.

10.1.
Castelnuovo–De Franchis’ Theorem

Theorem 10.3

(Castelnuovo–De Franchis’ Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a surface. Suppose there are two linearly independent holomorphic 1–forms
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
on
S
such that
\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}\equiv 0
. Then there is a smooth projective curve
C
, with
g(C)\geqslant 2
, a surjective morphism
f:S\to C
with connected fibres, and two linearly independent holomorphic 1–forms on
C
such that
\omega_{i}=f^{*}(\alpha_{i})
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 2
.

Proof.

Since
\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}\equiv 0
, there is a non–zero rational function
g
on
S
such that
\omega_{2}=g\omega_{1}
. Since
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
are closed forms, we deduce that
\omega_{1}\wedge dg\equiv 0
. Hence there is a non–zero rational function
h
on
S
such that
\omega_{1}=hdg
. Hence
dg\wedge dh\equiv 0
. Consider now the rational map

\phi:S\dasharrow{\mathbb{P}}^{2},\quad p\in S\to(g(p),h(p)).

Since
dg\wedge dh\equiv 0
, the image of
\phi
is a curve
\Gamma
, with affine equation
P(x,y)=0
. If
\nu:D\to\Gamma
is the normalization of
\Gamma
, we have a rational map
\psi:S\dasharrow D
. Now, eliminating the indeterminacies and using Stein factorization we can find a commutative diagram

\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\bar{q}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{\Gamma\,\,}

where
\pi:S^{\prime}\to S
is a birational morphism,
C
is a smooth curve,
q:C\to D
is a finite map and
f:S^{\prime}\to C
is surjective with connected fibres. Consider the meromorphic 1–forms on
C
given by
\alpha_{1}=\bar{q}^{*}(xdy)
and
\alpha_{2}=\bar{q}^{*}(xydy)
. We have
\pi^{*}(\omega_{i})=f^{*}(\alpha_{i})
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 2
, hence
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are holomorphic as well as
\pi^{*}(\omega_{1})
and
\pi^{*}(\omega_{2})
. This proves that
g(C)\geqslant 2
. Then the rational map
\pi^{-1}\circ f:S\dasharrow C
has no indeterminacies (see §
2.8
), so
S=S^{\prime}
,
\pi={\rm id}
, and the assertion follows. ∎

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
C
be a reduced curve on a surface
S
. Then

(12)

e(C)\geqslant 2\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C})

with equality if and only if
C
is smooth.

Proof.

First of all if
C
is a smooth curve then (
12
) holds with equality. In fact,
C
is the disjoint union of
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
its irreducible components. Then

g(C_{i})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{i}})
, and

e(C_{i})=2-2g(C_{i})=2\Big(h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{i}})-h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{i}})\Big)=2\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{i}}),\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n.

Then

e(C)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c(C_{i})=2\Big(n-\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(C_{i})\Big)=2\Big(h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})-h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})\Big)=2\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C}).

Consider now the general case. Let
\nu:D\to C
be the normalization of
C
. Consider the diagram

(13)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{C}}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nu_{*}{\mathbb{C}}_{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\epsilon\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nu_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
denotes the constant sheaf with stalk
{\mathbb{C}}
on a variety
X
and the torsion sheaves
\epsilon,\delta
are defined in such a way that the horizontal sequences are exact.

The map
f
is injective. Indeed, this amounts to prove that local sections of
\nu_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D}
which come from both

{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
and from
\nu_{*}{\mathbb{C}}_{D}
, in fact come from
{\mathbb{C}}_{C}
. This is immediate. In fact, the sections in question are local regular functions on
C
which, when pulled back to
D
, become constant which means they were constant to start with.

Hence we have
h^{0}(\epsilon)\leqslant h^{0}(\delta)
. From diagram (
13
) we deduce

e(D)=e(C)+h^{0}(\epsilon),\quad\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{D})=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C})+h^{0}(\delta)

hence

e(C)=e(D)-h^{0}(\epsilon)=2\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{D})-h^{0}(\epsilon)=2\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{C})+2h^{0}(\delta)-h^{0}(\epsilon)

which implies (
12
). Moreover the equality holds if and only if

0=2h^{0}(\delta)-h^{0}(\epsilon)=h^{0}(\delta)+\Big(h^{0}(\delta)-h^{0}(\epsilon)\Big)

which holds if and only if
h^{0}(\delta)=0
, since
h^{0}(\delta)\geqslant h^{0}(\epsilon)
. On the other hand
h^{0}(\delta)=0
if and only if
C
is smooth. ∎

Proposition 10.5
.

Let
S
be a surface and
C
a smooth, projective curve and
f:S\to C
a surjective morphism, with connected general fibre
F
(a smooth curve) and
F_{1},\ldots,F_{h}
the only singular fibres of
f
. Then:

(i)

one has

e(F_{i})\geqslant e(F),\quad\mbox{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant h;

(ii)

one has

e(S)=e(C)\cdot e(F)+\sum_{i=1}^{h}\Big(e(F_{i})-e(F)\Big)\geqslant e(C)\cdot e(F).

Proof.

(i) Since
f:S\to C
is flat, then
\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{i}})=\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{F})
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. Since the fibres of
f
are connected, so that
h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{i}})=h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{F})=1
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
, we have
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{i}})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{F})=g(F)
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. Since
e(F)=2-2g(F)
, we have to prove that

e(F_{i})\geqslant 2-2h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{i}})
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
.

Now notice that for any curve
D
on a surface, if we denote by
D_{\rm red}
its (reduced) support we have
e(D)=e(D_{\rm red})
. If
D
is connected, by taking into account Lemma
10.4
, we have

e(D)=e(D_{\rm red})\geqslant 2\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{D_{\rm red}})=2h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{D_{\rm red}})-2h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{D_{\rm red}})=2-2h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{D_{\rm red}}).

Moreover, we have
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{D})\geqslant h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{D_{\rm red}})
, because we have a surjection of sheaves, i.e., the restriction map,
{\mathcal{O}}_{D}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{D|D_{\rm red}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{D_{\rm red}}
. Hence

e(D)\geqslant 2-2h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{D_{\rm red}})\geqslant 2-2h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{D}).

Applying this to
D=F_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
, ends the proof of (i).

(ii) Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{h}
be the points such that
F_{i}
is the fibre over
p_{i}
, with
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. Then
f:S-\cup_{i=1}^{h}F_{i}\to C-\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{h}\}
is locally topologically a product with fibre
F
. Hence we have

e(S)=e(S-\sum_{i=1}^{h}F_{i})+e(\sum_{i=1}^{h}F_{i})=(e(C)-h)\cdot e(F)+\sum_{i=1}^{h}e(F_{i})

whence the assertion follows. ∎

Corollary 10.6
.

Same hypotheses as in Theorem
10.3
. In addition, assume
K_{S}
nef. Then
e(S)\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

If
K_{S}
is nef, then the generic fibre
F
of
f
does not have genus 0, hence
e(F)\leqslant 0
. Then, applying Proposition
10.5
, we have

e(S)\geqslant e(F)\cdot e(C)\geqslant 0

since
e(C)=2-2g(C)\leqslant-2
, being
g(C)\geqslant 2
.
∎

Lemma 10.7

(Hopf’s Lemma)
.

Let
V,W
be vector spaces of finite dimension
n,m
respectively over a field
\mathbb{K}
. Assume we have a linear map

\phi:\wedge^{2}V\to W.

If

m\leqslant 2n-4

then there are non–zero indecomposable tensors
\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}
in
{\rm Ker}(\phi)
.

Proof.

Consider the projective transformation induced by
\phi

\widetilde{\phi}:{\mathbb{P}}(\wedge^{2}V)\dasharrow{\mathbb{P}}(W)

whose indeterminacy locus is
{\mathbb{P}}({\rm Ker}(\phi))
. In
{\mathbb{P}}(\wedge^{2}V)
there is the
(2n-4)
–dimensional
Grassmann variety

\mathbb{G}
of lines in
{\mathbb{P}}(V)
, which is the locus of equivalence classes of non–zero indecomposable tensors. If
m\leqslant 2n-4
, then

\dim({\mathbb{P}}(W))=m-1\leqslant 2n-5=\dim(\mathbb{G})-1

so that
\widetilde{\phi}_{|\mathbb{G}}
is not finite. This implies that
\mathbb{G}
has to intersect the indeterminacy locus
{\mathbb{P}}({\rm Ker}(\phi))
of
\widetilde{\phi}
. This proves the assertion.
∎

Corollary 10.8
.

Let
S
be a surface such that

p_{g}\leqslant 2q-4.

Then there is a morphism
f:S\to C
as in Castelnuovo–De Franchis’ Theorem.

Proof.

Consider the obvious linear map

\wedge^{2}H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{S})\to H^{0}(\Omega^{2}_{S})

which sends
\sum_{ij}\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{j}
to the 2–form which is denoted in the same way. An application of Hopf’s Lemma implies that there is a non–zero indecomposable tensor
\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}
such that
\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}\equiv 0
as a 2–form. The assertion then follows by Castelnuovo–De Franchis’ Theorem. ∎

Theorem 10.9

(Castelnuovo–De Franchis–Enriques’ Theorem)
.

If
S
is a surface with
K_{S}
nef, then:

(i)

\chi\geqslant 0
and
e\geqslant 0
;

(ii)

if
\kappa=2
, then
\chi>0
and
K_{S}^{2}>0
;

(iii)

if
K_{S}^{2}>0
then
\kappa=2
;

(iv)

if
\chi>0
then
\kappa\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

(i) First we prove that
e\geqslant 0
. Argue by contradiction, and assume
e<0
. Then

0>e=2-2b_{1}+b_{2}
, hence
2b_{1}\geqslant 2+b_{2}\geqslant 3
, which implies
b_{1}\geqslant 2
. Therefore

H_{1}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
is a free abelian group of rank at least 2. Choose a subgroup
G
of
H_{1}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
of index
m\geqslant 6
. This lifts to a subgroup
G^{\prime}
of the same index of
\pi_{1}(S)
, so it gives rise to an étale cover

f:S^{\prime}\to S
of order
m
, such that

-6\geqslant me(S)=e(S^{\prime})=2-2b_{1}(S^{\prime})+b_{2}(S^{\prime})\geqslant 2-4q(S^{\prime})+2p_{g}(S^{\prime}),

hence
p_{g}(S^{\prime})\leqslant 2q(S^{\prime})-4
. Since
K_{S^{\prime}}=f^{*}(K_{S})
, then
K_{S^{\prime}}
is nef. Then by Corollary
10.8
and Corollary
10.6
we have
e(S^{\prime})\geqslant 0
, which implies
e(S)\geqslant 0
, a contradiction.

Then
\chi\geqslant 0
follows by Noether’s Formula, since also
K_{S}^{2}\geqslant 0
because
K_{S}
is nef. This proves (i).

(ii) To prove the assertion it suffices to prove that
K_{S}^{2}>0
, because then
\chi>0
by Noether’s Formula. If
\kappa=2
, then there is an
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the image of the map
\varphi_{|nK_{S}|}
is a surface. Set

|nK_{S}|=|M|+F

where
F
is the fixed part and
|M|
the movable part, which is nef. One has
M^{2}>0
, hence

(nK_{S})^{2}=nK_{S}\cdot F+nK_{S}\cdot M\geqslant nK_{S}\cdot M=(F+M)\cdot M\geqslant M^{2}>0

as wanted.

(iii) Assume
K_{S}^{2}>0
. Then for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

(14)

h^{0}(nK_{S})+h^{2}(nK_{S})\geqslant\chi(nK_{S})=\frac{m(m-1)}{2}K^{2}_{S}+\chi.

If
n\geqslant 2
, one has
h^{2}(nK_{S})=h^{0}((1-n)K_{S})=0
. Indeed, if
A
is ample and if
n\geqslant 2
, then
A\cdot((1-n)K_{S})<0
because
K_{S}
is nef, so
h^{0}((1-n)K_{S})=0
. Then (
14
) implies that
h^{0}(nK_{S})
grows as
n^{2}
proving that
\kappa=2
(see Theorem
2.2
).

(iv) The proof is similar to the one of (iii). If
\chi>0
, then from (
14
) and
h^{0}((1-n)K_{S})=0
one has
P_{n}>0
as soon as
n\geqslant 2
. ∎

10.2.
The canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations

Consider the following situation:
S
is a surface,
C
is a smooth projective curve, and we have a surjective morphism
f:S\to C
, such that the general fibre
F
of
f
is a smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
1
, and
S
is minimal. Then
K_{S}\cdot F=0
hence
\kappa(S)<2
and, by Castelnuovo–De Franchis–Enriques’ theorem, we have
K_{S}^{2}=0
.
One has:

Theorem 10.10
.

In the above setting, one has

K_{S}=f^{*}\Big(K_{C}\otimes(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})^{\vee}\Big)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{h}(m_{i}-1)F_{i}\Big),

where
R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
is a line bundle of degree
\chi
on
C
, and
m_{i}F_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
, are the multiple fibres of
f
.

As a consequence, there is an
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a line bundle
\mathcal{K}
on
C
such that
nK_{S}=f^{*}(\mathcal{K})
.

Proof.

Let
x\in C
be a point. We will denote by
F_{x}
the fibre of
f
over
x
and, as usual, by
F
the general fibre of
f
. Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be general points of
C
. Consider the exact sequence

0\to K_{S}\to K_{S}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{x_{i}})\to\oplus_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{x_{i}}}\to 0.

The image of
H^{0}(K_{S}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{x_{i}}))
in
H^{0}(\oplus_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{x_{i}}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
has codimension at most
q=h^{1}(K_{S})
, hence

h^{0}(K_{S}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{x_{i}}))\geqslant n+p_{g}-q

which is positive when
n\gg 0
. If
D\in|K_{S}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{x_{i}}|
, we have
D\cdot F=0
. This implies that we can write

K_{S}=f^{*}(L)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(B)

with
L
a line bundle on
C
and
B
a curve contained in a union of fibres of
f
, but not containing any fibre of
f
. Let
B_{0}
be any connected component of
B
and let
F_{0}
be the fibre containing it.

Claim 10.11
.

F_{0}
is a multiple fibre and
B_{0}
is a rational submultiple of
F_{0}
. i.e., there is an effective divisor
P
such that

F_{0}=mP\quad\text{and}\quad B_{0}=aP

with
0<a<m
.

Proof of Claim
10.11
.

If
B_{0},\ldots,B_{h}
are the connected components of
B
, we have
B=\sum_{j=1}^{h}B_{j}
and

0=K_{S}^{2}=B^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{h}B^{2}_{j}.

On the other hand we have
B_{j}^{2}\leqslant 0
, hence we have

B_{j}^{2}=0
for all
j\in\{0,\ldots,h\}
and the claim follows by Zariski’s Lemma. ∎

So we have

K_{S}=f^{*}(L)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{h}a_{i}F_{i}\Big)

with
m_{i}F_{i}
the multiple fibres of
f
and
0\leqslant a_{i}<m_{i}
. By the adjunction formula we have

{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{i}}=K_{F_{i}}={\mathcal{O}}_{S}(K_{S}+F_{i})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{i}}={\mathcal{O}}_{S}((a_{i}+1)F_{i})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{i}}.

But
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(F_{i})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{i}}
is torsion, of order
m_{i}
, therefore we must have
a_{i}=m_{i}-1
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
.

Note that, by the projection formula and relative duality (see Theorem
2.18
), we have

L=L\otimes f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(\sum_{i=1}^{h}(m_{i}-1)F_{i}))=f_{*}(K_{S})=K_{C}\otimes(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})^{\vee}.

Finally, by the spectral sequence associated to the map
f
, we have

\begin{split}&\chi=h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})-h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})+h^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=\\
&=h^{0}(f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})-h^{1}(f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})-h^{0}(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})+h^{1}(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})=\\
&=h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})-h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})-h^{0}(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})+h^{1}(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})=\\
&=1-g(C)-\chi(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})=1-g(C)-(\deg(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})-g(C)+1)=\\
&=-\deg(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})=\deg(R^{1}f_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})^{\vee}\end{split}

as desired. ∎

10.3.
Basic lemmas

Now we prove a couple of lemmas which will be crucial in what follows. Let
S
be a surface with
K_{S}
nef,
q=1
,
p_{g}=0
and
\kappa\leqslant 0
. Consider the Albanese map
\alpha:S\to E
, where
E=\operatorname{\rm Alb}(S)
is an elliptic curve. Consider the Stein factorization

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{E}

By the universal property of the Albanese map,
\operatorname{\rm Alb}(C)
and
E=\operatorname{\rm Alb}(S)
are isomorphic, hence
C=E
, hence
\alpha
has connected fibres. We denote by
g
the genus of a general fibre of
\alpha
.

Lemma 10.12
.

In the above setting the Albanese morphism
\alpha:S\to E
is smooth.

Proof.

First we remark that
\chi=0
and that, by Castelnuovo–De Franchis–Enriques’ Theorem, we have
K_{S}^{2}=0
. Then

(15)

e=12\chi-K_{S}^{2}=0=2(b_{0}-b_{1})+b_{2}=2-4q+b_{2}=b_{2}-2,\quad\mbox{hence}\quad b_{2}=2.

First we prove that for each fibre
F
of
\alpha
,
F_{\rm red}
is irreducible. Indeed, assume the contrary and suppose that a fibre contains two distinct irreducible components
F_{1},F_{2}
. Take
H
an ample divisor. We claim that the classes of
H,F_{1},F_{2}
are linearly independent in
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
, contradicting
(
15
). Indeed, assume
hH+f_{1}F_{1}+f_{2}F_{2}=0
in
H^{2}(S,{\mathbb{Z}})
. Then, intersecting with
F
, we find

0=(hH+f_{1}F_{1}+f_{2}F_{2})\cdot F=hH\cdot F

whence
h=0
. On the other hand Zariski’s Lemma implies that
f_{1}F_{1}+f_{2}F_{2}\equiv 0
if and only if
f_{1}=f_{2}=0
. This proves the claim.

Next, for every
x\in E
we consider the fibre
F_{x}
, which we write as
F_{x}=n_{x}G_{x}
, with
n_{x}\geqslant 1
and
G_{x}
irreducible and reduced.
If
F
is the general fibre of
\alpha
, by Lemma
10.4
we have

\begin{split}e(G_{x})&\geqslant 2\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{G_{x}})=-G_{x}\cdot(G_{x}+K_{S})=\\
&=-\frac{1}{n_{x}}F\cdot(K_{S}+\frac{1}{n_{x}}F)=-\frac{1}{n_{x}}F\cdot K_{S}=\\
&=-\frac{1}{n_{x}}F\cdot(K_{S}+F)=\frac{2}{n_{x}}\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{F})=\frac{1}{n_{x}}e(F).\end{split}

Since
g\geqslant 1
, we have
e(F)\leqslant 0
, hence
e(G_{x})\geqslant e(F)
for all
x\in E
, and the equality holds if and only if

e(G_{x})=2\chi({\mathcal{O}}_{G_{x}})\quad\mbox{hence $G_{x}$ is smooth, and}\quad\frac{1}{n_{x}}e(F)=e(F).

By part (ii) of Proposition
10.5
we have

0=e(S)=e(E)\cdot e(F)+\sum_{x\in E}(e(F_{x})-e(F))=\sum_{x\in E}(e(G_{x})-e(F))

since
e(E)=0
. Thus we have
e(G_{x})=e(F)
for all
x\in E
. As we saw, this implies that
G_{x}
is smooth for all
x\in E
. Moreover, if
e(F)\neq 0
, i.e., if
g>1
, then we must have
n_{x}=1
for all
x\in E
, proving the lemma. If
g=1
and
n_{x}>1
for some
x\in E
, then the canonical bundle formula for
\alpha
implies that there is an
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a line bundle
\mathcal{K}
of positive degree on
E
such that
nK_{S}=\alpha^{*}(\mathcal{K})
, which implies that that there is a positive integer
m
such that
P_{nm}\geqslant 2
, so that
\kappa>0
, a contradiction. So again
n_{x}=1
for all
x\in E
, and the assertion holds. ∎

Next we assume that the general fibre of
\alpha
has genus
g=1
.

Lemma 10.13
.

In the above set up, there is a morphism
\beta:S\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with connected fibres of genus 1.

Proof.

Recall that
\chi=0
,
K_{S}^{2}=0
,
q=1
.

Claim 10.14
.

There is a smooth elliptic curve
G
on
S
such that
G^{2}=K_{S}\cdot G=0
and
\alpha(G)=E
.

Proof of the Claim.

Consider a very ample divisor
H
, then
H_{|F}
is an effective divisor of degree
m:=F\cdot H>0
. For each fibre
F
there are
m^{2}
points
p_{F,i}
such that
mp_{F,i}\sim H_{|F}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m^{2}
. As
F
moves among the fibres, these points describe a curve
G^{\prime}
, and the map

\alpha_{|G^{\prime}}:G^{\prime}\to E
is étale. Hence any irreducible component
G
of
G^{\prime}
is a smooth elliptic curve. The canonical bundle formula for
\alpha
implies that
K_{S}\equiv 0
, hence
K_{S}\cdot G=0
, thus also
G^{2}=0
, as desired. ∎

Claim 10.15
.

There is another smooth elliptic curve
G^{\prime}\neq G
on
S
such that
G\cdot G^{\prime}=G^{\prime 2}=K_{S}\cdot G^{\prime}=0
and
\alpha(G^{\prime})=E
.

Proof of the Claim.

For every integer
n\geqslant 2
, consider the exact sequence

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+(n-1)G)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+nG)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{G}(nK_{S}+nG)=nK_{G}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{G}\to 0

and the related exact cohomology sequence

H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+nG))\to H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{G})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\to H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+(n-1)G)).

By Serre Duality we have

(16)

h^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+(n-1)G))=h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(-(n-1)K_{S}-(n-1)G))=0

because
-(n-1)K_{S}-(n-1)G\equiv-(n-1)G
and
G
is effective. Hence we have
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+nG))\geqslant 1
. Similarly to (
16
) we see that
h^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+nG))=0
.
Therefore

h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+nG))=\chi+\frac{1}{2}(nK_{S}+nG)\cdot((n-1)K_{S}+nG)+h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(nK_{S}+nG))\geqslant 1.

Hence, for every
n\geqslant 2
we find a curve
G_{n}\in|nK_{S}+nG|
.

Note that
G_{n}
cannot be a multiple of
G
for all
n\geqslant 2
. Otherwise we would have

G_{n}=mG\sim n(K_{S}+G),\quad G_{n+1}=m^{\prime}G\sim(n+1)(K_{S}+G).

If
A
is any ample divisor, since
K_{S}\equiv 0
, we clearly have
A\cdot G_{n+1}>A\cdot G_{n}
, hence
m^{\prime}>m
. But then

K_{S}\sim G_{n+1}-G_{n}-G\sim(m^{\prime}-m-1)G

hence we would have
h^{0}(K_{S})>0
, a contradiction.

So we can take an integer
n\geqslant 2
such that

G_{n}=mG+\sum_{i=1}^{h}d_{i}D_{i}

with
h>0
,
D_{1},\ldots,D_{h}
irreducible distinct curves distinct from
G
,
d_{1},\ldots,d_{h}
positive integers. Since

0=G\cdot(nK_{S}+nG)=G\cdot G_{n}=G\cdot(mG+\sum_{i=1}^{h}d_{i}D_{i})=G\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{h}d_{i}D_{i}

then we must have
G\cdot D_{i}=0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. By Hodge Index Theorem this yields
D_{i}^{2}\leqslant 0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. On the other hand

0=K_{S}\cdot(nK_{S}+nG)=K_{S}\cdot G_{n}=K_{S}\cdot(mG+\sum_{i=1}^{h}d_{i}D_{i})=K_{S}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{h}d_{i}D_{i}

and since
K_{S}
is nef we have
K_{S}\cdot D_{i}=0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. This implies that each
D_{i}
is either rational or it is smooth of genus 1. However, there is no rational curve on
S
, because a rational curve cannot dominate
E
, so it should be contained in a fibre of
\alpha
, which is impossible, since all fibres of
\alpha
are smooth elliptic curves. So
D_{i}
is smooth, elliptic, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. So we can take
G^{\prime}
equal to one of the
D_{i}
s. Furthermore,
\alpha(G^{\prime})=E
, otherwise
G^{\prime}
would be a fibre of
\alpha
, and this is impossible, because the fibre of
\alpha
intersect
G
positively. ∎

Now we can finish the proof of the Lemma. Consider the exact sequence

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+G+G^{\prime})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{G\cup G^{\prime}}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{G}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{G^{\prime}}\to 0

which gives rise to

H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}))\to H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{G\cup C^{\prime}}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\to H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+G+G^{\prime})).

We have

h^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+G+G^{\prime}))\simeq h^{0}(-K_{S}-G-G^{\prime})=0

and similarly
h^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}))=0
. Then

h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}))\geqslant h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{G\cup C^{\prime}}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}))=2.

Hence

\begin{split}h^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}))&=\chi+\frac{1}{2}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\cdot(K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})+\\
&+h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S}(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}))\geqslant 2.\end{split}

Write
|2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}|=|M|+\Phi
, where
|M|
is the movable part and
\Phi
the fixed part and note that
\dim(|M|)\geqslant 1
. We have that
2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime}
is nef, because so if
K_{S}
and moreover
(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\cdot G=(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\cdot G^{\prime}=0
. So, since
M
is also nef, we have

\begin{split}&0=(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})^{2}=(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\cdot(M+\Phi)\geqslant\\
&\geqslant(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\cdot M=M\cdot(M+\Phi)\geqslant M^{2}\geqslant 0\end{split}

hence
M^{2}=0
and
|M|
is base point free. Moreover

\begin{split}&0=(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})^{2}\geqslant(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\cdot(M+\Phi)\geqslant\\
&\geqslant(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})\cdot M\geqslant 2K_{S}\cdot M\geqslant 0,\end{split}

thus
K_{S}\cdot M=0
, hence
|M|
is composed with a pencil of elliptic curves. Hence there is a smooth curve
B
, a morphism
\beta:S\to B
with connected fibres of arithmetic genus 1, and a linear series
\mathcal{M}
on
B
such that
|M|=\beta^{*}(\mathcal{M})
. Note that
\beta:S\to B
cannot coincide with
\alpha:S\to E
, since otherwise we have

0<G\cdot F\leqslant G\cdot(M+\Phi)=G\cdot(2K_{S}+2G+2G^{\prime})=0,

a contradiction. Finally we observe that the genus of
B
cannot be positive, otherwise by the universal property of the Albanese variety, we would have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{S}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{B}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{J(B)\,\,}

and
\alpha_{S}
would coincide with
\beta
, a contradiction. Hence
B\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. ∎

10.4.
The proof of Theorem
10.2

Now we can turn to the:

Proof of Theorem
10.2
.

We argue by contradiction, assuming
\kappa=-\infty
. In particular
P_{2}=0
. Then we must have
q>0
otherwise, by Castelnuovo’s Criterion,
S
would be rational, i.e.,
we would have a birational map
f:S\dasharrow{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, which leads to a contradiction.
Indeed, if
f
is an isomorphism, then
K_{S}
is not nef, a contradiction. If
f
is a morphism which is not an isomorphism, then
S
has some
(-1)
–curve, and
K_{S}
is not nef, a contradiction. Then assume
f
is not a morphism. Then we have a resolution of the indeterminacies

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}}

and assume that
\pi
is composed with the minimal number of blow–ups. Then, by an argument we already made,
X
contains a
(-1)
–curve
C
, the exceptional curve of the last blow–up composing
\pi
, which is not contracted by
p
. We have

K_{X}\sim p^{*}(K_{S})+E\sim\pi^{*}(K_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}})+E^{\prime},

where
E
and
E^{\prime}
are divisors contracted to points by
p
and
\pi
respectively. As we said,
C
is not contained in
E
. Hence

-1=K_{X}\cdot C=p^{*}(K_{S})\cdot C+E\cdot C\geqslant p^{*}(K_{S})\cdot C=K_{S}\cdot p_{*}(C),

thus
K_{S}
is not nef, a contradiction.

Now we claim that
q=1
. In fact

0\leqslant\chi=1-q+p_{g}=1-q,\quad\mbox{hence}\quad 0<q\leqslant 1.

Hence the Albanese variety of
S
is an elliptic curve
E
and the Albanese map is a morphism
\alpha:S\to E
which, as we saw at the beginning of §
10.3
, has
connected fibres. We denote by
g
the genus of the general fibre of
\alpha
. Since we may assume
K_{S}
is nef, we have
g>0
.

Step 1.
First we assume
g=1
. Then we can apply Theorem
10.10
. Note that
K_{E}
is trivial,
\chi=0
, so that
R^{1}\alpha_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
has degree 0. Moreover, by Lemma
10.12
, the Albanese morphism is smooth. So we conclude that
K_{S}\equiv 0
.

By Lemma
10.13
, we have a morphism
\beta:S\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with connected fibres of genus 1, and, again by Theorem
10.10
, we have an
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a line bundle
\mathcal{K}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
such that
nK_{S}=\beta^{*}(\mathcal{K})
. Since
K_{S}\equiv 0
, then
\mathcal{K}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
. This implies that
nK_{S}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
, so
P_{n}=1
, proving that
\kappa\geqslant 0
, thus contradicting the hypothesis
\kappa=-\infty
.

Step 2.

Next we assume that the fibres of the Albanese map
\alpha:S\to E
have genus
g>1
. Let us apply Theorem
2.11
to
\alpha:S\to E
, noting that
\omega_{S|E}\simeq K_{S}
in this case, since
K_{E}
is trivial. If
\deg(\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|E})>0
, by Riemann–Roch theorem for vector bundles on curves, we have

\chi(\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|E})=h^{0}(\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|E})-h^{1}(\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|E})=\deg(\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|E})>0,

hence
h^{0}(\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|E})>0
. But
\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|E}=\alpha_{*}(K_{S})
, hence we get
p_{g}>0
, a contradiction. So it does not happen that
\deg(\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|E})>0
, then by Theorem
2.11
all fibres of
\alpha:S\to E
are isomorphic. Let
F
be such a fibre. Then
\operatorname{Aut}(F)
is finite and
\pi_{1}(E)
acts on
F
, i.e., we have a homomorphism

\rho:\pi_{1}(E)\to\operatorname{Aut}(F).

Hence
\operatorname{ker}(\rho)
has finite index, so it corresponds to an étale cover
f:C\to E
, with
C
an elliptic curve. Consider the cartesian square

\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{E\,\,}

with
\alpha^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\to C
isotrivial as well, with fibres isomorphic to
F
. By definition now
\pi_{1}(C)
acts trivially on the fibres of
\alpha^{\prime}
, hence
S^{\prime}=C\times F
. Then the canonical bundle of
S^{\prime}
is the pull–back of the canonical bundle
K_{F}
on
F
via the obvious projection, hence
\kappa(S^{\prime})=1
.

Finally we prove that
\kappa(S)\geqslant 0
. Since
g
is étale, we have
nK_{S^{\prime}}=g^{*}(nK_{S})
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The surface
S
is the quotient of
S^{\prime}
via the action of a finite group
G
of order
m
which acts freely on
S^{\prime}
. Hence, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

H^{0}(nK_{S})=H^{0}(nK_{S^{\prime}})^{G}.

Take any non–zero section
s\in H^{0}(nK_{S^{\prime}})
and consider the section
s^{G}\in H^{0}(mnK_{S^{\prime}})
defined as

s^{G}(x)=\prod_{g\in G}s(g(x)),\quad\mbox{for all}\quad x\in S^{\prime}.

The section
s^{G}
is clearly non–zero and
s^{G}\in H^{0}(mnK_{S^{\prime}})^{G}\simeq H^{0}(mnK_{S})
. Hence
P_{mn}(S)>0
, proving that
\kappa(S)\geqslant 0
.

This ends the proof of Theorem
10.2
. ∎

11.
The classification and the Abundance Theorem

By the Fundamental Theorem of the Classification and by Remark
8.2
, we have that:

\bullet

the result of the minimal model programme applied to a surface
S
is a Mori fibre space if and only if
\kappa(S)=-\infty
;

\bullet

the result of the minimal model programme applied to a surface
S
is a strong minimal model if and only if
0\leqslant\kappa(S)\leqslant 2
.

11.1.
Surfaces with
\kappa=-\infty

If
\kappa(S)=-\infty
, then either
S
is rational, or it is birational to an irrational scroll.
In the former case the birational equivalence class of
S
is represented by
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, thus
q=0
, in the latter case, since
S
is birational to
{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E})
with
\mathcal{E}
a rank 2 vector bundle on a curve
C
, with
g(C)>0
, the birational equivalence class of
S
is represented by
C\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, hence
q=g(C)
, because the only holomorphic 1–forms on
C\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
are pull–back of the 1–forms on
C
via the projection to the first factor. More precisely, one has
\operatorname{\rm Alb}(S)=J(C)
in this case.

It is useful to record the following:

Theorem 11.1

(Vaccaro’s Theorem)
.

If
S
is a minimal surface with
\kappa(S)=-\infty
, then:

(i)

if
S
is rational then either
S={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
or
S={\mathbb{F}}_{n}
, with
n\in{\mathbb{N}}-\{1\}
;

(ii)

if
S
is not rational, then
S={\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E})
with
\mathcal{E}
a rank 2 vector bundle on a curve
C
of genus
g=q>0
.

Proof.

(i) First it is clear that
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is minimal. Also
{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
with
n\in{\mathbb{N}}-\{1\}
, is minimal. Indeed on

{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
there is no curve with negative self–intersection and on
{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
with
n\geqslant 2
, there is only one irreducible curve with negative self–intersection equal to
-n
. Moreover all
{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
are rational.

Conversely, let
S
be rational and minimal. Then, by the structure of the minimal model programme,
S
is a Mori fibre space, hence it is a scroll over
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, and the assertion follows.

(ii) Let
S
be not rational, minimal and
\kappa(S)=-\infty
. Then the minimal model programme applied to
S
is a Mori fibre space, hence
S
is a scroll over a curve
C
of genus
g=q>0
. Hence
S={\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E})
with
\mathcal{E}
a rank 2 vector bundle on
C
(see Theorem
5.4
). ∎

11.2.
The Abundance Theorem: statement

In this section we will prove the following result:

Theorem 11.2

(The Abundance Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a strong minimal model. Then there is an
m\gg 0
and highly divisible such that
|mK_{S}|
is base point free.

Actually we will prove a finer result. The proof will consist in separately considering the cases
\kappa=0,1,2
.

11.3.
Surfaces with
\kappa=2

Surfaces with
\kappa=2
are called
surfaces of general type
. Recall that for a minimal such surface
S
one has
K_{S}^{2}>0
. For any positive integer
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have the
n–canonical map

\varphi_{|nK_{S}|}
, often denoted by
\varphi_{n}
, whose image we denote by
S_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{P}}^{P_{n}-1}
, and call the
n–canonical image
of
S
. If
S
is of general type,
S_{n}
is a surface for infinitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

For surfaces of general type we have the following result which is much stronger than the Abundance Theorem:

Theorem 11.3

(Bombieri’s Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface of general type. Then:

\bullet

\varphi_{n}
is a birational morphism onto its image
S_{n}
, for all
n\geqslant 3
, except for

n=3,4
,
p_{g}=2
,
K_{S}^{2}=1
and
n=3
,
p_{g}=3
,
K_{S}^{2}=2
;

\bullet

\varphi_{2}
is a birational morphism onto
S_{2}
if
K_{S}^{2}\geqslant 10
, unless
S
has a pencil of genus 2 curves;

\bullet

\varphi_{n}
is an isomorphism onto
S_{n}
, for all
n\geqslant 5
, off finitely many rational curves
C
with
C^{2}=-2
.

More precise results are known for canonical and bicanonical maps, but we do not dwell on this here. We will not prove Theorem
11.3
in its full generality here. However, in §
12
, using some of the main ideas of Bombieri’s, we will prove a weaker result which implies the Abundance Theorem in this case.

11.4.
Surfaces with
\kappa=1

Surfaces with
\kappa=1
are called
properly elliptic surfaces
. By Castelnuovo–De Franchis–Enriques’ Theorem, we have
K_{S}^{2}=0
for a minimal such surface.

Theorem 11.4
.

Let
S
be a minimal properly elliptic surface. Then there is a unique elliptic pencil
f:S\to C
and there are infinitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that there is a base point free complete linear series
\mathcal{K}_{n}
on
C
such that
|nK_{S}|=f^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{n})
.

Proof.

Remember that there is a sequence
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of positive integers such that
P_{h_{n}}
grows like
n
as
n\to\infty
(see Theorem
2.2
). For such an
h_{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}
we write

|h_{n}K_{S}|=F+|M|

with
F
the fixed part and
|M|
the movable part. We have

0=(h_{n}K_{S})^{2}=h_{n}K_{S}\cdot(M+F)\geqslant h_{n}K_{S}\cdot M\geqslant(M+F)\cdot M\geqslant M^{2}+M\cdot F\geqslant M^{2}\geqslant 0,

hence
M^{2}=M\cdot F=F^{2}=0
. This implies that
|M|
is a composed with a pencil, i.e., there
is a pencil
f:S\to C
and a complete, base point free linear series
\mathcal{M}
on
C
such that
|M|=f^{*}(\mathcal{M})
. Since
K_{S}\cdot M=\frac{1}{h_{n}}M\cdot(F+M)=0
, we have that the arithmetic genus of the curves of the pencil is 1. Moreover, since
F^{2}=M\cdot F=0
, by Zariski’s lemma we have that
F=F_{1}+\cdots+F_{k}
, where each
F_{i}
is a submultiple of a mupltiple of a curve of the pencil, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
. It is then clear that by taking a sufficiently large and highly divisible
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the linear system
|mh_{n}K_{S}|
has the required property.

The uniqueness of the pencil
f:S\to C
is immediate. ∎

11.5.
Surfaces with
\kappa=0

If
S
is a minimal surface with
\kappa=0
, by Castelnuovo–De Franchis–Enriques’ Theorem we have
K_{S}^{2}=0
,
e\geqslant 0
and
\chi\geqslant 0
. Moreover, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
P_{n}\in\{0,1\}
and there is an
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
P_{n}=1
. Furthermore

0\leqslant\chi=1-q+p_{g},\quad\mbox{hence}\quad 1\geqslant p_{g}\geqslant q-1,\quad\mbox{thus}\quad q\leqslant 2,

so we have only the following possibilities:

(i)

q=0,p_{g}=1
, these surfaces are called
K3 surfaces
;

(ii)

q=0,p_{g}=0
, these surface are called
Enriques surfaces
;

(iii)

q=2,p_{g}=1
, these are
abelian surfaces
;

(iv)

q=1,p_{g}=0
, these surfaces are called
bielliptic surfaces
;

(v)

q=1,p_{g}=1
, as we will see there are no surfaces with
\kappa=0
with these invariants.

Case 11.5
.

q=0,p_{g}=1

Theorem 11.6
.

If
S
is a minimal surface with
\kappa=0
and
q=0,p_{g}=1
, then
K_{S}
is trivial, hence all pluricanonical systems are trivial, and therefore are base point free.

Proof.

One has

h^{0}(-K_{S})+h^{0}(2K_{S})\geqslant h^{0}(-K_{S})-h^{1}(-K_{S})+h^{2}(-K_{S})=\chi(-K_{S})=\chi=2

but
h^{0}(2K_{S})=P_{2}\leqslant 1
, hence
h^{0}(-K_{S})\geqslant 1
. On the other hand also
h^{0}(K_{S})=p_{g}=1
, hence
K_{S}
is trivial.
∎

Example 11.7
.

K3 surfaces do exist. For instance, by §
2.11
and §
2.15
, a smooth surface of degree
4 in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
, a smooth complete intersection of type
(2,3)
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{4}
, a smooth complete intersection of type
(2,2,2)
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
, the double cover of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
branched along a smooth sextic curve, are K3 surfaces. More generally, for any integer
g\geqslant 3
, there are smooth K3 surfaces of degree
2g-2
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{g}
, and they depend on 19 parameters (see
[
2
, Chapt. VIII]
).

Case 11.8
.

q=0,p_{g}=0

Theorem 11.9
.

If
S
is a minimal surface with
\kappa=0
and
q=0,p_{g}=0
, then
2K_{S}
is trivial, hence all even pluricanonical systems are trivial, and therefore are base point free, whereas
P_{n}=0
for all odd
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

First we observe that
P_{2}=1
otherwise by Castelnuovo’s criterion the surface is rational. One has

(17)

h^{0}(-2K_{S})+h^{0}(3K_{S})\geqslant h^{0}(-2K_{S})-h^{1}(-2K_{S})+h^{2}(-2K_{S})=\chi(-2K_{S})=\chi=1.

Next we claim that
P_{3}=0
. Otherwise one has
P_{3}=1
and there is a unique curve
D\in|3K_{S}|
. On the other hand there is a unique curve
D^{\prime}\in|2K_{S}|
. We can write
D=\sum_{i=1}^{h}a_{i}D_{i}
and
D^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}b_{i}D_{i}
, with
D_{1},\ldots,D_{h}
distinct irreducible curves and
a_{i},b_{i}\geqslant 0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. Then we have
P_{6}=1
and the unique curve in
|6K_{S}|
is
2D=3D^{\prime}
. Hence we have

2\sum_{i=1}^{h}a_{i}D_{i}=3\sum_{i=1}^{h}b_{i}D_{i}

which implies that

2a_{i}=3b_{i},\quad\mbox{for all}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant h.

Then there are non–negative integers
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{h}
such that

a_{i}=2\lambda_{i},\quad b_{i}=3\lambda_{i},\quad\mbox{for all}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant h.

Hence

D-D^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}(a_{i}-b_{i})D_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}\lambda_{i}D_{i}\in|K_{S}|

which implies
p_{g}=1
, a contradiction.

Since
P_{3}=0
, then (
17
) implies that
h^{0}(-2K_{S})>0
. Since also
h^{0}(2K_{S})=P_{2}=1
, we have that
2K_{S}
is trivial, and the assertion follows.
∎

Remark 11.10
.

For Enriques surfaces
K_{S}
is a non–trivial element of order 2 in
\operatorname{Pic}(S)
. This determines an étale double cover
\pi:S^{\prime}\to S
(see Example
2.15
), such that
K_{S^{\prime}}=\pi^{*}(K_{S})={\mathcal{O}}_{S^{\prime}}
, so that
\kappa(S^{\prime})=0
. Moreover, by (
5
), we have

q(S^{\prime})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{\prime}})=h^{1}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}^{\prime})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})+h^{1}(K_{S})=2h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=0.

Thus
S^{\prime}
is a K3 surface. Enriques surfaces can also be defined as the quotient of a K3 surface via a fixed point free involution.

Enriques surfaces do exist. Here we give three examples.

Example 11.11
.

(i)
Classical Enriques sextic surfaces.
Let
T
be a tetrahedron in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
, which, up to change of coordinates, we may assume to be the
coordinate tetrahedron
, whose four vertices are the
coordinate points
with all coordinates but one equal to zero. Consider the linear system
\mathcal{S}
of surfaces of degree 6 which are singular along the 6 edges of
T
. The linear system
\mathcal{S}
has equation

c_{3}(x_{0}x_{1}x_{2})^{2}+c_{2}(x_{0}x_{1}x_{3})^{2}+c_{1}(x_{0}x_{2}x_{3})^{2}+c_{0}(x_{1}x_{2}x_{3})^{2}+Qx_{0}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}=0,

where
c_{0},\ldots,c_{3}\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
Q=\sum_{i\leqslant j}q_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}
is a quadratic form.
This shows that
\dim(\mathcal{S})=13
and we may identify
\mathcal{S}
with the
{\mathbb{P}}^{13}
with homogeneous coordinates

q=(c_{0}:c_{1}:c_{2}:c_{3}:q_{00}:q_{01}:q_{02}:q_{03}:q_{11}:q_{12}:q_{13}:q_{22}:q_{23}:q_{33}).

An open dense set of
\mathcal{S}
correspond to irreducible surfaces which have ordinary singularities along the edges of
T
and no further singularity. For any such surfaces
\Sigma
its normalization
\nu:S\to\Sigma
is smooth and
it is an Enriques surface. Indeed, by §
2.11
,
K_{S}
is the pull–back via
\nu
of the linear system of quadrics passing through the edges of
T
, off the pull–back of these edges. Since there is no such a quadric, then
|K_{S}|=\emptyset
and
p_{g}=0
. The bicanonical system
2K_{S}
is the pull–back via
\nu
(off the pull–back of the edges of
T
) of the linear system of surfaces of degree 4 which are singular along the edges of
T
. There is only one such surface, namely
T
itself, and the mentioned pull–back is trivial. Thus
2K_{S}\sim{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
is trivial,
P_{2}=1
,
\kappa=0
and
S
is an Enriques surface, because
q=0
. To see this one can argue as follows. First blow–up the vertices of
T
, then the edges of
T
, so to obtain a smooth threefold
X
. The take the strict transform
S^{\prime}
of an element
S\in\mathcal{S}
with ordinary singularities to
X
. Then
S^{\prime}
moves in a linear system
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, the proper transform of
\mathcal{S}
, which is base point free, hence
S^{\prime}
is nef. Moreover it is not difficult to see that
S^{\prime 3}>0
. So
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(S^{\prime})
is big and nef. Then the same argument we made in §
2.11
, using the Kawamata–Viehweg Vanishing Theorem, tells us that
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{\prime}})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. But
S^{\prime}
is birational to
S
and
X
to
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
, hence
h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{S^{\prime}})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})=h^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{3}})=0
.

It is known that
all
Enriques surfaces appear as the desingularization of some surface
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}
. Since
\dim(\mathcal{S})=13
but there is the 3–dimensional group of projective transformations fixing
T
which acts on
\mathcal{S}
, one has that Enriques surfaces depend on
10
parameters.

(ii) Consider the complete intersection surface
S^{\prime}
of type
(2,2,2)
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
defined by equations of the form

Q_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})+Q^{\prime}_{i}(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})=0\quad\mbox{with}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant 3,

where
Q_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})
and
Q^{\prime}_{i}(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})
are quadratic forms, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 3
. It is not difficult to see that for a sufficiently general choice of
Q_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})
and
Q^{\prime}_{i}(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 3
, the surface
S^{\prime}
is smooth, hence it is a K3 surface (see Example
11.7
). Consider the projective transformation

\sigma:[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{5}\to[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},-x_{3},-x_{4},-x_{5}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{5}

which clearly fixes
S^{\prime}
and acts on it freely for a sufficiently general choice of
Q_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})
and
Q^{\prime}_{i}(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 3
. The quotient
S
of
S^{\prime}
by the action of
\sigma
is an Enriques surface.

(iii)
Reye congruences.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a linear system of dimension 3 of quadrics in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
such that :

\bullet

\mathcal{P}
is base point free;

\bullet

if
\ell
is a double line for
Q\in\mathcal{P}
, then
Q
is the unique quadric in
\mathcal{P}
containing
\ell
.

These conditions are verified if
\mathcal{P}
is sufficiently general.

Next, denote by
\mathbb{G}
the grassmannian variety of lines in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
and consider

S=\{\ell\in\mathbb{G}:\mbox{$\ell$ is contained in a pencil contained in $\mathcal{P}$}\}.

We claim that
S
is an Enriques surface. Surfaces obtained in this way are called
Reye congruences
.

To prove our claim, consider
S^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
thus defined

S^{\prime}=\{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{3}:\mbox{$x\neq y$ and $x$ and $y$ are conjugated with respect to all $Q\in\mathcal{P}$}\}.

One remarks that
\ell=\langle x,y\rangle\in S
if and only if
(x,y)\in S^{\prime}
and moreover the involution

\sigma:(x,y)\in S^{\prime}\to(y,x)\in S^{\prime}

has no fixed points. Then
S
is the quotient of
S^{\prime}
via the action of
\sigma
and, to prove that
S
is an Enriques surface, one has to prove that
S^{\prime}
is a K3 surface. To see this, suppose
\mathcal{P}
is spanned by the four independent quadrics
Q_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=0
, with
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
. Consider the bilinear forms
\phi_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3};y_{0},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
associated to
Q_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
. Then
S^{\prime}
is clearly defined in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
by the four equations
\phi_{i}=0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
. The canonical bundle of
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
is the sum of the pull–backs of
K_{{\mathbb{P}}^{3}}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{3}}(-4)
via the projections to the two factors. Then the adjunction formula (see §
2.11
) tells us that
K_{S^{\prime}}
is trivial. Moreover one can prove that
q(S^{\prime})=0
(see §
2.11
for a similar argument), thus
S^{\prime}
is a K3 surface.

For more information on Enriques surfaces, see
[
9
]
and
[
2
, Chapt. VII]
.

Case 11.12
.

q=2
,
p_{g}=1

We need the following special case of Poincaré Complete Reducibility Theorem
2.13
:

Lemma 11.13
.

Let
A
be an abelian surface and let
C
be a smooth elliptic curve contained in
A
. Then there is a smooth elliptic curve
E
and a morphism
f:A\to E
with connected fibres such that
C
is a fibre of
f
.

Proof.

Since
K_{A}
is trivial, we have
C^{2}=C\cdot(C+K_{A})=0
. We also assume that, up to translations,
C
contains the point
0
of
A
.

For each
x\in A
consider the curve
C_{x}=x+C
, which is isomorphic to
C
. Note that
x\in C_{x}
.
For each
x\in A
, the curve
C_{x}
is homologically equivalent to
C
, hence
C_{x}\cdot C=0
and
C_{x}^{2}=0
.
Then given
x,y\in A
, either
C_{x}=C_{y}
or
C_{x}\cap C_{y}=\emptyset
. Moreover, for every point
z\in A
,
C_{z}
is the unique curve in the family
\mathcal{C}:=\{C_{x}\}_{x\in A}
passing through
z
. Furthermore, if
y\in C_{x}
, then
C_{x}=C_{y}
, because both
C_{x}
and
C_{y}
contain
y
.

Consider now the map

f:x\in A\to[C_{x}-C]\in\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(A).

From the above remarks it follows that all curves in the family
\mathcal{C}
are contained in fibres of
f
.
Note that
f
cannot be constant. Otherwise the curves in
\mathcal{C}
would be linearly equivalent and we would have a morphism
\phi:A\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
such that the curves in
\mathcal{C}
are the fibres of
\phi
. By applying the canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations, we find a contradiction to
K_{A}
being trivial.

So the image of
f
is a curve
E
which we can assume to be smooth and, up to Stein factorization, we can assume that
\mathcal{C}
is the family of all fibres of
f
. By applying again the canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations we se that
E
has to be an elliptic curve, as desired.
∎

Theorem 11.14

(Enriques’ Theorem)
.

If
S
is a minimal surface wirth
\kappa=0
and
q=2
,
p_{g}=1
, then
S
is an abelian surface. In particular
K_{S}
is trivial, so that the Abundance Theorem holds.

Proof.

Consider the Albanese map

\alpha:S\to A:=\operatorname{\rm Alb}(S).

There are two cases to be discussed:

(i)

\dim(\alpha(S))=1
;

(ii)

\alpha(S)=A
.

Case (i).
We prove that this case does not occur. Indeed, consider the Stein factorization

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{A}

Since
\alpha(S)
spans
A
, then
g(C)\geqslant 2
. On the other hand the map

\beta^{*}:H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{C})\to H^{0}(\Omega^{1}_{S})

is an injection, hence
2=q(S)\geqslant g(C)
, thus
g(C)=2
. Now take a non–trivial point of order 2 in
\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(C)
, and consider the corresponding étale double cover
f:\widetilde{C}\to C
, with
g(\widetilde{C})=3
. Consider the cartesian diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\widetilde{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C\,\,}

We claim that
\kappa(\widetilde{S})=\kappa(S)=0
. Since
K_{\widetilde{S}}=g^{*}(K_{S})
, it is clear that

\kappa(\widetilde{S})\geqslant\kappa(S)
. To see the converse, we make an argument similar to the one at the end of the proof of Theorem
10.2
. Take an
n\gg 0
such that

\dim({\rm im}(\varphi_{|nK_{\widetilde{S}}|}))=\kappa(\widetilde{S}).

The surface
S
is the quotient of
\widetilde{S}
via the action of a group
G\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
which acts freely on
\widetilde{S}
. Hence we have

H^{0}(nK_{S})=H^{0}(nK_{\widetilde{S}})^{G}.

Take any non–zero section
s\in H^{0}(nK_{\widetilde{S}})
and consider the section
s^{G}\in H^{0}(2nK_{\widetilde{S}})
defined as

s^{G}(x)=\prod_{g\in G}s(g(x)),\quad\mbox{for all}\quad x\in\widetilde{S}.

The section
s^{G}
is non–zero and
s^{G}\in H^{0}(2nK_{\widetilde{S}})^{G}\simeq H^{0}(2nK_{S})
. Consider now the map

\rho:[(s)]\in{\mathbb{P}}(H^{0}(nK_{\widetilde{S}}))\to[(s^{G})]\in{\mathbb{P}}(H^{0}(2nK_{\widetilde{S}})^{G})={\mathbb{P}}(H^{0}(2nK_{S})).

The map
\rho
is clearly finite, since it maps the general
D\in|nK_{\widetilde{S}}|
to

D^{G}:=\sum_{\gamma\in G}\gamma(D)\in|2nK_{\widetilde{S}}|.

This proves that
\dim({\rm im}(\varphi_{2nK_{S}}))=\dim({\rm im}(\varphi_{nK_{\widetilde{S}}}))=\kappa(\widetilde{S})
, which proves the claim.

Hence we proved that
\kappa(\widetilde{S})=0
. Moreover
q(\widetilde{S})\geqslant 3
, because we have the map
\tilde{\alpha}:\widetilde{S}\to C
and
g(\widetilde{C})=3
. This is a contradiction, since, as we saw, there are no surfaces with
\kappa=0
and
q>2
. This proves that case (i) does not occur.

Case (ii).
We prove that
K_{S}
is trivial in this case. Assume the contrary holds and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{h}a_{i}D_{i}
be the unique element in
|K_{S}|
, where
D_{1},\ldots,D_{h}
are irreducible and distinct and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{h}
are positive integers. For every
i\in\{1,\dots,h\}
one has

(18)

0=K_{S}^{2}=K_{S}\cdot D\geqslant K_{S}\cdot D_{i}=D\cdot D_{i}=\sum_{j\neq i}^{h}a_{j}D_{j}\cdot D_{i}+a_{i}D_{i}^{2}\geqslant a_{i}D_{i}^{2}

and

p_{a}(D_{i})=\frac{1}{2}(D_{i}+K_{S})\cdot D_{i}+1\leqslant 1,

hence:

(a)

either
D_{i}
is a smooth elliptic curve;

(b)

or
D_{i}
is rational which could either be smooth or singular with a node or a cusp.

Assume that case (b) occurs for all
i\in\{1,\dots,h\}
. Then each divisor
D_{i}
is contracted to a point by
\alpha
, because there are no rational curves on an abelian variety. Thus
D
is contracted to a union of points, hence, by the Hodge Index Theorem, one has
K_{S}^{2}=D^{2}<0
, a contradiction. Hence there is an
i\in\{1,\dots,h\}
such that case (a) occurs. From (
18
) we have
D_{i}^{2}\leqslant 0
and
K_{S}\cdot D_{i}\leqslant 0
. On the other hand one has
D_{i}\cdot(K_{S}+D_{i})=0
, so that
D_{i}^{2}=0
and
K_{S}\cdot D_{i}=0
. Therefore
D_{i}
is not contracted to a point by
\alpha
. Its image is a smooth elliptic curve
E
in
A
. By Lemma
11.13
, there is an elliptic curve
B
and a morphism
f:A\to B
with connected fibres such that
E
is a fibre of
f
. Consider
\beta=f\circ\alpha:S\to B
. Then
D_{i}
is contained in a fibre of
\beta
and, being
D_{i}^{2}=0
, one has that
D_{i}
is the support of a fibre of
\beta
. If
nD_{i}
is the full fibre, then for all
h\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

h^{0}(hnK_{S})\geqslant h^{0}(hnD_{i})=h

which is not possible. This proves that
K_{S}
is trivial.

Let now
R
be the ramification divisor of the Albanese map
\alpha:S\to A
. By the ramification formula we have

{\mathcal{O}}_{S}=K_{S}=\alpha^{*}(K_{A})+R=R

which proves that
R
is trivial, hence
\alpha
is unramified. This implies that
S
is an abelian surface and
\alpha
is an isomorphism (see Proposition
2.12
).∎

Case 11.15
.

q=1
,
p_{g}=0

Theorem 11.16
.

If
S
is a surface with
\kappa=0
,
q=1
and
p_{g}=0
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
P_{n}=1
, one has that
nK_{S}
is trivial.

Proof.

By an argument we already made, the Albanese map
\alpha:S\to A:=\operatorname{\rm Alb}(S)
, where
A
is an elliptic curve, has connected fibres. Let
F
be the general such fibre.
Of course
g(F)\geqslant 1
.

We claim that
g(F)=1
. Indeed, assume by contradiction that
g(F)\geq 2
. Then we can argue as in Step 2 of the proof of Theorem
10.2
. Namely, if
\deg(\alpha_{*}\omega_{S|A})>0
, we have
p_{g}>0
, a contradiction. Otherwise we have a cartesian square

\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\,\,}

with
C
an elliptic curve and
S^{\prime}=C\times F
, hence
\kappa(S^{\prime})=1
. On the other hand, the same argument we made in the proof of case (i) of Enriques’ Theorem
11.14
, on p.
11.5
, proves that
\kappa(S)=\kappa(S^{\prime})=1
, a contradiction. This proves that
g(F)=1
.

Let now
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
be such that
P_{n}=1
and consider the unique divisor
D\in|nK_{S}|
.
We claim that
D=0
. We argue by contradiction and assume
D\neq 0
.
Since
K_{S}\cdot F=0
, we have
D\cdot F=0
, hence
D
consists of parts of fibres of
\alpha
. Moreover
D^{2}=(nK_{S})^{2}=0
and Zariski’s Lemma, imply that the support of
D
consists of the support of fibres of
\alpha
. But in this case for
m\gg 0
and highly divisible we would have

P_{nm}=h^{0}(nmK_{S})=h^{0}(mD)>1

a contradiction. Hence
nK_{S}
is trivial, proving the theorem.∎

The surfaces in this case, called bielliptic surfaces, have been classified by Bagnera–De Franchis, and we will explain their classification in §
13
below.

Case 11.17
.

q=p_{g}=1

This case does not occur. Indeed, let
\eta\in\operatorname{Pic}(S)
be a non–trivial order two element. Then

h^{0}(\eta)+h^{0}(K_{S}-\eta)\geqslant\chi(\eta)=\chi=1.

Since
h^{0}(\eta)=0
, we have
h^{0}(K_{S}-\eta)\geqslant 1
. Take
D\in|K_{S}-\eta|
and
D^{\prime}\in|K_{S}|
. Then
2D,2D^{\prime}\in|2K_{S}|
, hence
2D=2D^{\prime}
, thus
D=D^{\prime}
and
\eta=0
, a contradiction.

12.
Surfaces of general type

In this section we give a sketch of the proof of the following weaker version of Bombieri’s Theorem
11.3
:

Theorem 12.1
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface of general type. Then
|nK_{S}|
is base point free as soon as
n\geqslant 5
. Moreover
\varphi_{nK_{S}}
is birational onto its image
S_{n}
as soon as
n\geqslant 6
.

The proof of Bombieri’s Theorem
11.3
, though more complicated, relies on the same basic ideas. For the proof we need a few essential preliminaries.

12.1.
Some vanishing theorems

Let
S
be a surface and
C
a curve on
S
. One says that
C
is
numerically
m
–connected
(or simply
m
–connected
), if for every decomposition
C=C_{1}+C_{2}
with
C_{1},C_{2}
effective, non–zero, one has
C_{1}\cdot C_{2}\geqslant m
.

The following are fundamental results which widely extend Kodaira vanishing theorem:

Theorem 12.2

(Franchetta–Ramanujam’s Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a surface and
C
an effective divisor on
S
. If
C
is 1–connected and
C^{2}>0
, then
h^{1}(K_{S}+C)=0
.

For the proof, see
[
4
, p. 179–180]
.

The following is the surface version of Kawamata–Viehweg Vanishing Theorem:

Theorem 12.3

(Ramanujam–Mumford’s Vanishing Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a surface and
C
an effective divisor on
S
. If
C
is nef and
C^{2}>0
, then
h^{1}(K_{S}+C)=0
.

For the proof, see
[
2
, Theorem (12.1)]
.

Corollary 12.4
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface of general type. Then

(19)

P_{n}=\chi+\frac{n(n-1)}{2}K_{S}^{2}\quad\text{for all}\quad n\geqslant 2.

Proof.

By Serre duality we have
h^{2}(nK_{S})=h^{0}((1-n)K_{S})=0
, for all
n\geqslant 2
. Moreover all pluricanonical divisors are nef, with positive self–intersection. Hence, by Ramanujam–Mumford’s Vanishing Theorem, we have
h^{1}(nK_{S})=0
, for all
n\geqslant 2
. The assertion follows by Riemann–Roch theorem.∎

12.2.
Connectedness of pluricanonical divisors

We consider here a minimal surface
S
of general type, so that it is also a strong minimal model and therefore
K_{S}
is nef.

Lemma 12.5

(Franchetta–Bombieri’s Lemma)
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface of general type and let
C\equiv nK_{S}
with
n\geqslant 1
a curve. Then
C
is 1–connected.

For the proof, see
[
4
, p. 181]
.

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface of general type, let
x
be a point on
S
, let
p:S^{\prime}\to S
the blow–up of
S
at
x
, with exceptional divisor
E
. Let
C
be a curve on
S^{\prime}
with
C\in|p^{*}(nK_{S})-2E|
, with
n\geqslant 3
. Then
C
is 1–connected.

For the proof, see
[
4
, p. 183]
.

Lemma 12.7
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface of general type, let
x,y
be distinct points on
S
, let
p:S^{\prime}\to S
be the blow–up of
S
at
x,y
, with exceptional divisor
E_{x},E_{y}
. Let
C
be a curve on
S^{\prime}
with
C\in|p^{*}(nK_{S})-2E_{x}-2E_{y}|
, with
n\geqslant 4
. Then
C
is 1–connected.

For the proof, see again
[
4
, p. 183]
.

12.3.
Base point freeness

In this section we prove the:

Theorem 12.8
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface of general type. Then
|nK_{S}|
is base point free as soon as
n\geqslant 5
.

Proof.

Let
x\in S
and let
p:S^{\prime}\to S
be the blow–up of
S
at
x
, with exceptional divisor
E
. Consider the exact sequence

0\to p^{*}(nK_{S})(-E)\to p^{*}(nK_{S})\to p^{*}(nK_{S})_{|E}={\mathcal{O}}_{E}\to 0.

From this it follows that
x
cannot be a base point for
|nK_{S}|
if
h^{1}(p^{*}(nK_{S})(-E))=0
. Suppose there exists a divisor
C\in|p^{*}((n-1)K_{S})(-2E))|
. Then
C
is 1–connected as soon as
n\geqslant 4
by Lemma
12.6
. Moreover
C^{2}=(n-1)^{2}K_{S}^{2}-4\geqslant 5>0
if
n\geqslant 4
. Hence, by Theorem

12.2
, we have that
h^{1}(K_{S^{\prime}}+C)=0
. Since
K_{S^{\prime}}=p^{*}(K_{S})+E
, then

K_{S^{\prime}}+C\sim p^{*}(K_{S})+E+p^{*}((n-1)K_{S})(-2E))=p^{*}(nK_{S})(-E)
, hence

h^{1}(p^{*}(nK_{S})(-E))=0
, which proves that
x
is not a base point for
|nK_{S}|
.

Finally the existence of
C\in|p^{*}((n-1)K_{S})(-2E))|
is ensured by the fact that, if
n\geqslant 5
one has
\dim(|(n-1)K_{S}|)\geqslant\chi+\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}-1\geqslant 6
, hence certainly there is
a curve in
|(n-1)K_{S}|
which is singular at
x
, because this imposes at most 3 conditions to
|(n-1)K_{S}|
. ∎

12.4.
Birationality

In this section we prove the:

Theorem 12.9
.

Let
S
be a minimal surface of general type. Then the map
\phi_{nK_{S}}
is birational onto its image as soon as
n\geqslant 6
.

Proof.

Let
x\in S
be a general point. Suppose
\phi_{nK_{S}}
non–birational. Then there is another point
y\in S
different from
x
, such that
\phi_{nK_{S}}(x)=\phi_{nK_{S}}(y)
, i.e.,
x
and
y
are not separated by
\phi_{nK_{S}}
.

Let
p:S^{\prime}\to S
be the blow–up of
S
at
x
and
y
with exceptional divisors
E_{x}
and
E_{y}
. Consider the exact sequence

0\to p^{*}(nK_{S})(-E_{x}-E_{y})\to p^{*}(nK_{S})\to p^{*}(nK_{S})_{|E_{x}\cup E_{y}}={\mathcal{O}}_{E_{x}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{y}}\to 0.

Since
x
and
y
are not separated by
\phi_{nK_{S}}
, then the map

H^{0}(p^{*}(nK_{S}))\to H^{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{E_{x}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{E_{y}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{2}

is not surjective, and this implies that
h^{1}(p^{*}(nK_{S})(-E_{x}-E_{y}))>0
. We will see this is a contradiction.

Indeed, suppose there exists a divisor
C\in|p^{*}((n-1)K_{S})(-2E_{x}-2E_{y})|
. Then
C
is 1–connected as soon as
n\geqslant 5
by Lemma
12.7
. Moreover
C^{2}=(n-1)^{2}K_{S}^{2}-8\geqslant 1>0
if
n\geqslant 4
. Hence, by Theorem

12.2
, we have that
h^{1}(K_{S^{\prime}}+C)=0
. Since
K_{S^{\prime}}=p^{*}(K_{S})+E_{x}+E_{y}
, then

K_{S^{\prime}}+C\sim p^{*}(K_{S})+E_{x}+E_{y}+p^{*}((n-1)K_{S})(-2E_{x}-2E_{y})=p^{*}(nK_{S})(-E_{x}-E_{y})
, hence

h^{1}(p^{*}(nK_{S})(-E_{x}-E_{y}))=0
, a contradiction.

Finally the existence of
C\in|p^{*}((n-1)K_{S})(-2E_{x}-2E_{y})|
is ensured by the fact that, if
n\geqslant 6
, one has
\dim(|(n-1)K_{S}|)\geqslant\chi+\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}-1\geqslant 10
, hence certainly there is
a curve in
|(n-1)K_{S}|
which is singular at
x
and
y
, because this imposes at most 6 conditions to
|(n-1)K_{S}|
. ∎

13.
Bagnera–De Franchis’ classification of bielliptic surfaces

In this section we explain Bagnera–De Franchis’ classification of bielliptic surfaces, with
\kappa=0
,
q=1
,
p_{g}=0
. The main result is as follows:

Theorem 13.1

(Bagnera–De Franchis’ Theorem)
.

The bielliptic surfaces
S
are as follows:
E,F
are smooth elliptic curves,
G
is a group of translations of
E
acting on
F
and
S=E\times F/G
, with:

(i)

G={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
acting on
F
as
x\in F\to-x\in F
;

(ii)

G={\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{2}
acting on
F
as
x\in F\to-x\in F
and
x\in F\to x+\epsilon\in F
, where
\epsilon
is a non–trivial order 2 point on
F
;

(iii)

F=F_{i}={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus i{\mathbb{Z}}
and
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
, acting on
F
as
x\in F\to ix\in F
;

(iv)

F=F_{i}
and
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
acting on
F
as
x\in F\to ix\in F
and
x\in F\to x+\frac{1+i}{2}\in F
;

(v)

F=F_{\rho}={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Z}}+\rho{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
\rho
a non–trivial cubic root of 1,
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
acting on
F
as

x\in F\to\rho x\in F
;

(vi)

F=F_{\rho}
,
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{3}^{2}
, acting on
F
as
x\in F\to\rho x\in F
and
x\in F\to x+\frac{1-\rho}{3}\in F
;

(vii)

F=F_{\rho}
,
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{6}
, acting on
F
as
x\in F\to-\rho x\in F
.

The first trivial pluricanonical bundle for
S
is:

(a)

2K_{S}
in cases (i) and (ii);

(b)

4K_{S}
in cases (iii) and (iv);

(c)

3K_{S}
in cases (v) and (vi);

(d)

6K_{S}
in case (vii).

We start with the:

Lemma 13.2
.

A minimal bielliptic surface
S
is the quotient of the product of two elliptic curves via a free group action. More precisely there is a commutative diagram

(20)

\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A\,\,}

where
\alpha:S\to A=\operatorname{\rm Alb}(S)
is the Albanese elliptic isotrivial fibration with elliptic base
A
,
S^{\prime}=E\times F
, is the product of two smooth elliptic curves,
\alpha^{\prime}
is the projection onto the first factor, and
f,g
are étale, induced by a free group
G
action.

Proof.

We saw in the proof of Theorem
11.16
that a bielliptic surface
S
has an elliptic fibration
\alpha:S\to A
, with
A
elliptic base. This clearly coincides with the Albanese map. Moreover
p_{g}=0
whereas there is a multiple of
K_{S}
which is trivial. Hence
K_{S}
is a non–trivial torsion point of
\operatorname{Num}(S)
. By the canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations there are no multiple fibres of
\alpha
and
(R^{1}\alpha_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S})^{\vee}
is a torsion line bundle on
A
. Since
e(S)=0
, by Proposition
10.5
, (ii), there are no singular fibres of
\alpha
. Then we have a morphism
\mu:A\to\mathcal{M}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{1}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the moduli space of curves of genus 1, which assignes to each point
x
the modulus of the fibre of
\alpha
over
x
. Since
A
is projective,
\mu
is constant, hence the elliptic fibration
\alpha:S\to A
is isotrivial with fibre
F
. Then,
by an argument we already made in Step 2 of the proof of Theorem
10.2
,
there is a diagram as (
20
)
where
f,g
are étale, induced by the action of a finite group
G
acting freely on
S^{\prime}
and
E
, so that
E
is an elliptic curve and
S^{\prime}=E\times F
. ∎

Note that the group
G
in the statement of Lemma
13.2
is a finite group of translations of
E
, because
E/G
is the elliptic curve
A
. In fact, if
E={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda
and
A={\mathbb{C}}/\Lambda^{\prime}
, then
\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda^{\prime}
and
G=\Lambda^{\prime}/\Lambda
. Then
G
is abelian.

Lemma 13.3
.

In the above set up, there is a smooth elliptic curve
E^{\prime}
, an étale morphism
h:E^{\prime}\to E
, and a finite group
G^{\prime}
acting on
E^{\prime}
and on
F
(hence acting diagonally on the product
E^{\prime}\times F
), such that
E^{\prime}/G^{\prime}\simeq E/G=A
and
E^{\prime}\times F/G^{\prime}\simeq E\times F/G=S
.

Proof.

For every
g\in G
, every
x\in E
and every
y\in F
we set
g\cdot(x,y)=(g\cdot x,\phi_{g,x}(y))
, where
\phi_{g,x}
is an automorphism of
F
depending on
x
and
g
. Let us fix an origin on the elliptic curve
F
so that
F
has the structure of an abelian variety. Then
\phi_{g,x}
has the form

\phi_{g,x}:y\in F\to a_{g,x}\cdot y\oplus t_{g,x}\in F

where
a_{g,x}
is an automorphism of
F
preserving the group structure and
t_{g,x}\in F
(we denote by
\oplus
the addition in
F
and by
\ominus
the difference). The group
\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(F)
of automorphisms of
F
preserving the group structure is finite (see Theorem
13.7
below), hence
a_{g,x}=a_{g}
does not depend on
x
. Moreover

t_{g}:x\in E\to t_{g,x}\in F

is a morphism. Of course
a_{\rm id}={\rm id}
and
t_{\rm id}
is the zero map.

By imposing that
fg(x,y)=f(g(x,y))
we get
a_{gf}=a_{g}\circ a_{f}
and also

a_{f}\cdot t_{g,x}+t_{f,g\cdot x}=t_{fg,x},

in particular

(21)

a_{g^{-1}}\cdot t_{g,x}+t_{g^{-}1,g\cdot x}=0

Since
a_{gf}=a_{g}\circ a_{f}
the map

a:g\in G\to a_{g}\in\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(F)

is a homomorphism.

Claim 13.4
.

There is a morphism
r:E\to F
and a positive integer
n
such that, for all
x\in E
and
g\in G
, one has

(22)

r(g\cdot x)\ominus a_{g}\cdot r(x)=nt_{g,x}.

Proof of Claim
13.4
.

Note that we have a canonical isomorphism

\psi:y\in F\to[y-0]\in\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(F)

(we denote by
[D]
the class of a divisor
D
in
\operatorname{Pic}(F)
) and we denote the sum in
\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(F)
still by
\oplus
.
If we have
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in F
, then

\psi(y_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus y_{n})=[y_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus y_{n}-0]

and also

\psi(y_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus y_{n})=\psi(y_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\psi(y_{n})=[y_{1}-0]\oplus\cdots\oplus[y_{n}-0]=[y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n}-n0]

so that

y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n}-n0\sim y_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus y_{n}-0

hence

(23)

y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n}\sim y_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus y_{n}+(n-1)0.

Let
v
be an automorphism of
F
given by
v(y)=a\cdot y\oplus t
, with
a\in\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(F)
and
t\in F
. If
D=(n-1)0+y
, we have

v^{*}(D)=(n-1)v^{-1}(0)+v^{-1}(y)=(n-1)(\ominus a^{-1}\cdot t)+(a^{-1}\cdot y\ominus a^{-1}\cdot t).

By applying (
23
), we deduce that

(24)

\begin{split}v^{*}(D)&\sim(n-1)(\ominus a^{-1}\cdot t)+(a^{-1}\cdot y\ominus a^{-1}\cdot t)\\
&\sim(n-2)0+(\ominus(n-1)a^{-1}\cdot t)+(a^{-1}\cdot y\ominus a^{-1}\cdot t)\\
&\sim(n-2)0+0+(a^{-1}y\ominus na^{-1}\cdot t)\\
&\sim(n-1)0+(a^{-1}y\ominus na^{-1}\cdot t).\end{split}

Let
H
be a very ample line bundle on
E\times F
. The line bundle

L={\mathcal{O}}_{E\times F}(\sum_{g\in G}g^{*}(H))

is invariant by the
G
action. For a given
x\in E
, let us set

L_{x}=L_{|\{x\}\times F}

which is an invertible sheaf of degree
n>0
on
F
. The invariance of
L
by the
G
action implies that for any
x\in E
and
g\in G
one has

L_{x}=\phi_{g,x}^{*}L_{gx}.

We define the morphism
r:E\to F
in such a way that

L_{x}={\mathcal{O}}_{F}((n-1)0+r(x)).

Then, by applying (
24
), we see that (
22
) holds. ∎

Assume first that
n=1
in Claim
13.4
. Let
u
be the automorphism of
E\times F
defined by
u(x,y)=(x,y\ominus r(x))
. Then we have

\begin{split}ugu^{-1}(x,y)&=ug(x,y\oplus r(x))=u(gx,\phi_{g,x}(y\oplus r(x))=u(g\cdot x,a_{g}\cdot y\oplus a_{g}\cdot r(x)\oplus t_{g,x})\\
&=(gx,a_{g}\cdot y\oplus a_{g}\cdot r(g\cdot x)\oplus t_{g,x}\ominus r(x))=(g\cdot x,a_{g}\cdot y)\end{split}

the last equality following from (
22
) with
n=1
. So if we set
G^{\prime}=uGu^{-1}
, then
G^{\prime}
acts on
E
and
F
, and on
E\times F
with the diagonal action, and
E\times F/G=E\times F/G^{\prime}
, and the proof is completed in this case.

Consider now the case
n>1
. Consider the cartesian diagram

(25)

\textstyle{\widetilde{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{r}}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot n}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{F\,\,}

where
\pi:\widetilde{E}\to E
is a covering, not necessarily connected. We have that
\widetilde{E}
is stable for the
G
action. Indeed,
\widetilde{E}\subset E\cdot F
consists of all pairs
(x,y)
such that
r(x)=ny
. If we have such a pair, then

g\cdot(x,y)=(g\cdot x,a_{g}\cdot y\oplus t_{g,x})

and, by (
22
), we have

r(g\cdot x)=a_{g}\cdot r(x)\oplus nt_{g,x}=a_{g}\cdot(ny)\oplus nt_{g,x}=n(a_{g}\cdot y\oplus t_{g,x})

as wanted.

Moreover the group
F[n]
of points of
F
of order
n
also acts on
\widetilde{E}
via the action

\varepsilon\cdot(x,y)=(x,y\oplus\varepsilon).

Let
G^{\prime}
be the group of automorphism of
\widetilde{E}
generated by
G
and
F[n]
.
For any
g\in G
and
\varepsilon\in F[n]
, one has

\begin{split}g\varepsilon g^{-1}(x,y)&=g\varepsilon(g^{-1}\cdot x,a_{g}^{-1}\cdot y\oplus t_{g^{-1},x})\\
&=g(g^{-1}\cdot x,a_{g}^{-1}\cdot y\oplus t_{g^{-1},x}\oplus\varepsilon)\\
&=(x,y\oplus a_{g}\cdot\varepsilon\oplus a_{g}\cdot t_{g^{-1},x}\oplus t_{g,g^{-1}\cdot x})=(x,y\oplus a_{g}\cdot\varepsilon),\end{split}

the last equality holding because of (
21
). Hence we have
g\varepsilon g^{-1}=a_{g}\cdot\varepsilon
. This means that
G^{\prime}
is the semidirect product of
F[n]
and
G
, and one has an exact sequence

0\to F[n]\to G^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{u}}}{{\to}}G\to 1.

We define an operation of
G^{\prime}
on
\widetilde{E}\times F
as follows

\gamma(\tilde{x},y)=(\gamma\cdot\tilde{x},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma),\pi(\tilde{x})}(y))

with
\gamma\in G^{\prime}
,
\tilde{x}\in\widetilde{E}
and
y\in F
. Since
\widetilde{E}/F[n]\simeq E
, one has
E/G\simeq(\widetilde{E}/F[n])/G\simeq\widetilde{E}/G^{\prime}
and therefore
\widetilde{E}\times F/G^{\prime}\simeq E\times F/G
.

Consider now the automorphism
w
of
\widetilde{E}\times F
defined as

w(\tilde{x},y)=(\tilde{x},y\ominus\tilde{r}(\tilde{x}))

with
\tilde{x}\in\widetilde{E},y\in F
. One has

\begin{split}w\gamma w^{-1}(\tilde{x},y)&=w\gamma(\tilde{x},y\oplus\tilde{r}(\tilde{x}))\\
&=w(\gamma\cdot\tilde{x},\phi_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma),\pi(\tilde{x})}(y\oplus\tilde{r}(\tilde{x})))\\
&=w(\gamma\cdot\tilde{x},a_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma)}(y\oplus\tilde{r}(\tilde{x}))\oplus t_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma),\pi(\tilde{x})})\\
&=(\gamma\cdot\tilde{x},a_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma)}(y\oplus\tilde{r}(\tilde{x}))\oplus t_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma),\pi(\tilde{x})}\ominus\tilde{r}(\gamma(\tilde{x})))\\
&=(\gamma\cdot\tilde{x},a_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma)}\cdot y\oplus\eta_{\gamma,\tilde{x}})\end{split}

where

\eta_{\gamma,\tilde{x}}=a_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma)}\cdot\tilde{r}(\tilde{x})\oplus t_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma),\pi(\tilde{x})}\ominus\tilde{r}(\gamma(\tilde{x})).

By looking at diagram (
25
) and by (
22
), we have

\begin{split}n\eta_{\gamma,\tilde{x}}&=a_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma)}\cdot n\tilde{r}(\tilde{x})\oplus nt_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma),\pi(\tilde{x})}\ominus n\tilde{r}(\gamma(\tilde{x}))\\
&=a_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma)}\cdot r(\pi(\tilde{x}))\ominus r(\pi(\gamma\cdot\tilde{x}))\oplus nt_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma),\pi(\tilde{x})}=0,\end{split}

hence
\eta_{\gamma,\tilde{x}}\in F[n]
. Since
F[n]
is finite,
\eta_{\gamma,\tilde{x}}
does not depend on
\tilde{x}
, so we can denote it by
\eta_{\gamma}
. Thus we have

w\gamma w^{-1}(\tilde{x},y)=(\gamma\cdot\tilde{x},a_{\mathfrak{u}(\gamma)}\cdot y\oplus\eta_{\gamma})

which implies that the action of
G^{\prime}
on
\widetilde{E}\times F
, after conjugation by
u
, is of the required form.

If
\widetilde{E}
is not connected, one may replace
\widetilde{E}
by one of its connected components
E^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
with its subgroup fixing
E^{\prime}
. ∎

According to the previous lemma, up to replacing
E
with
E^{\prime}
, we may and will assume that
G
is a finite group of translations of
E
acting also on
F
and diagonally on
S^{\prime}=E\times F
.

Set
C=F/G
and note that
g(C)\leqslant 1
. We claim that
g(C)=0
. In fact, we have an obvious morphism
\phi:S\to C
. If
g(C)=1
,
\phi
would factor through the Albanese map, thus if would factor through
\alpha
, which is impossible.

Though Theorem
13.1
tells in detail which pluricanonical systems are trivial for bielliptic surfaces, we show a partial result in this direction in the following lemma:

Lemma 13.5
.

In the above setting one has that
either
4K_{S}
or
6K_{S}
is trivial,
hence
12K_{S}
is trivial and
P_{12}=1
.

Proof.

By Theorem
11.16
it suffices to prove that either
P_{4}=1
or
P_{6}=1
. Note that
H^{0}((\Omega^{1}_{E})^{\otimes n})
has dimension 1 and is invariant by
G
, since the non–zero 1–form on
E
is invariant by translations. Hence

\begin{split}H^{0}(nK_{S})&=H^{0}((\Omega^{2}_{S})^{\otimes n})=H^{0}((\Omega^{2}_{S^{\prime}})^{\otimes n})^{G}\simeq\\
