&\simeq\Big(H^{0}((\Omega^{1}_{E})^{\otimes n})\otimes H^{0}((\Omega^{1}_{F})^{\otimes n})\Big)^{G}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\otimes H^{0}((\Omega^{1}_{F})^{\otimes n})^{G}\end{split}

and

P_{n}=H^{0}((\Omega^{1}_{F})^{\otimes n})^{G}\simeq H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},L_{n})=\ell_{n}+1

where
L_{n}
is a line bundle of degree
\ell_{n}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Let
\nu
be the order of
G
and consider the
\nu:1
morphism
\pi:F\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=F/G
. Let
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
be a branch point. Then
G
acts transitively on
\pi^{-1}(p)=\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{s}\}
and the stabilizers of the points
q_{i}
are conjugated in
G
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
. Let
r_{p}
, or simply
r
, be their common order. This is the
ramification index
of
\pi
at each of the points
q_{1},\ldots,q_{s}
, also called the
branching index
of
\pi
at
p
, i.e., for
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
, there are local coordinates
y
at
p
and
x_{i}
at
q_{i}
such that
\pi^{*}y=x_{i}^{r}
. Note that
\nu=sr
.

Claim 13.6
.

The degree of
L_{n}
is

\ell_{n}=-2n+\sum_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\Big[n\Big(1-\frac{1}{r_{p}}\Big)\Big]

where
r_{p}
is the ramification index of
\pi:F\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
at
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Proof of Claim
13.6
.

We have to look at rational
n
–tensor forms
\sigma
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=F/G
such that
\pi^{*}\sigma\in H^{0}(\omega_{F}^{\otimes n})
. With the notation introduced above, we locally write around any branch point
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

\sigma=fy^{-m}dy^{\otimes n},\quad\mbox{with}\quad f\quad\mbox{non--zero and}\quad m\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Then around any point
q_{i}\in\pi^{-1}(p)
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
, we have

\pi^{*}\sigma=(\pi^{*}f)x_{i}^{-rm}(rx_{i}^{r-1}dx_{i})^{\otimes n}=f_{i}x_{i}^{-rm+(r-1)n}dx_{i}^{\otimes n}

where we set
r\pi^{*}f:=f_{i}
, which is a non–zero function. So
\pi^{*}\sigma
is holomorphic if and only if
-rm+(r-1)n\geqslant 0
, i.e., if and only if

m\leqslant n\Big(1-\frac{1}{r}\Big).

Hence
\sigma
is a
n
–tensor form with poles of order at most
n\Big(1-\frac{1}{r_{p}}\Big)
at
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. ∎

Now we can finish the proof of the lemma. We have to prove that either
\ell_{4}\geqslant 0
or
\ell_{6}\geqslant 0
. Let
h
be the number of branch points of
\pi
and let
r_{1},\ldots,r_{h}
be the corresponding branching indices, with
r_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant r_{h}
. By the Riemann–Hurwitz formula we have

0=2g(F)-2=-2\nu+\sum_{i=1}^{h}\nu\Big(1-\frac{1}{r_{i}}\Big),

hence

(26)

\sum_{i=1}^{h}\Big(1-\frac{1}{r_{i}}\Big)=2.

Moreover, since
r_{p}\geqslant 2
for any branch point
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, we have

2\Big(1-\frac{1}{r_{i}}\Big)\geqslant 1.

First we assume
h\geqslant 4
. Then

P_{2}=\ell_{2}+1=-4+\sum_{i=1}^{h}\Big[2\big(1-\frac{1}{r_{i}}\big)\Big]+1\geqslant 1,\quad\mbox{hence}\quad P_{4}=P_{6}=1,

proving the lemma in this case.

Next notice that
h\leqslant 2
is not possible because of (
26
), so we only have to discuss the case
h=3
. Then (
26
) reads

(27)

\frac{1}{r_{1}}+\frac{1}{r_{2}}+\frac{1}{r_{3}}=1.

If
r_{1}\geqslant 3
, we have

3\Big(1-\frac{1}{r_{i}}\Big)\geqslant 2,\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,2,3,

hence

P_{3}=\ell_{3}+1=-6+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Big[3\big(1-\frac{1}{r_{i}}\big)\Big]+1\geqslant 1,\quad\mbox{hence}\quad P_{6}=1,

again proving the lemma in this case.

If
r_{2}\geqslant 4
, by (
27
) we have
r_{1}=2,r_{2}=r_{4}=4
. This implies
\ell_{4}=0
hence
P_{4}=1
. Finally we can only have
r_{2}=3
, and by (
27
) only the following cases are possible

\begin{split}&(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})=(3,3,3)\quad\mbox{hence}\quad\ell_{3}=0\quad\mbox{thus}\quad P_{3}=1\\
&(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})=(2,3,6)\quad\mbox{hence}\quad\ell_{6}=0\quad\mbox{thus}\quad P_{6}=1\end{split}

and in both cases
P_{6}=1
. This ends the proof of the lemma. ∎

For the proof of Theorem
13.1
we need a well known preliminary result (see, e.g.,
[
12
, Chapt. IV, Cor. 4.7]
):

Theorem 13.7
.

Let
E
be a smooth elliptic curve, which we look at as an abelian variety of dimenion 1. Let
j
be the
j
–invariant of
E
. Denote by
\operatorname{Aut}(E)
the group of all automorphisms of
E
and by
\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(E)
the group of automorphism of
E
as an abelian variety. Then
\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(E)
is a finite group and precisely:

(i)

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, generated by the symmetry
x\to-x
if
j\neq 0,1728
;

(ii)

{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
, generated by the automorphism
x\to ix
, if
j=1728
, i.e., if
E={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Z}}+i{\mathbb{Z}}
;

(iii)

{\mathbb{Z}}_{6}
, generated by the automorphism
x\to-\rho x
, with
\rho
a non–trivial cubic root, if
j=0
, i.e., if
E={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Z}}+\rho{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Moreover
\operatorname{Aut}(E)=E\ltimes\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(E)
, where
E
is identified with the group of translations of
E
.

We can finally give the:

Proof of Theorem
13.1
.

By Lemma
13.2
and the subsequent discussion, a bielliptic surface
S
is a quotient
E\times F/G
, where
E,F
are elliptic curves,
G\subset E
is a finite (abelian) group of translations (so that
E/G
is an elliptic curve), and
G
is also a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(F)
, such that
F/G\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. By the last assertion of Theorem
13.7
,
G
is the direct product
T\times H
where
T
is a finite subgroup of translations of
F
and
H
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(F)
. Since

F/G\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, then
H\neq 0
, and therefore, by Theorem
13.7
, one has
H={\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, with
p=2,3,4,6
. Since
G
is abelian, the elements of
T
commute with those of
H
, i.e., they are translations by the fixed points of the action by
H
. It is immediate to find these fixed points:

(a)

if
H={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
generated by the symmetry
x\to-x
, the fixed points are the points of order 2 of
F
, whose set we denote by
F[2]
;

(b)

if
H={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
, generated by the automorphism
x\to ix
, and
F={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Z}}+i{\mathbb{Z}}
,
the fixed points are (the classes of)
0
and
\frac{1+i}{2}
;

(c)

if
H={\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
, generated by the automorphism
x\to\rho x
, with
\rho
a non–trivial cubic root, and
F={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Z}}+\rho{\mathbb{Z}}
, the fixed points are (the classes of)
0
and
\pm\frac{1-\rho}{3}
;

(d)

if
H={\mathbb{Z}}_{6}
, generated by the automorphism
x\to-\rho x
, with
\rho
a non–trivial cubic root, and
E={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Z}}+\rho{\mathbb{Z}}
, then the only fixed point is (the class of)
0
.

Finally
G=T\times H
is a subgroup of
E
, which excludes the possibility that
G=F[2]\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. The cases (i)–(viii) in the statement are now an easy consequence of the above considerations.

As for the final assertion of the theorem, let
\omega
be a non–zero holomorphic
2
–form on
E\times F
. Then the minimum
n
such that
nK_{S}
is trivial, is the minimum
n
such that
G
acts trivially on
\omega^{n}
. It is then clear that the assertion follows.
∎

14.
The
P_{12}
–Theorem

In this section we prove the:

Theorem 14.1

(The
P_{12}
–Theorem)
.

Let
S
be a surface. Then:

(i)

\kappa=-\infty
if and only if
P_{12}=0
;

(ii)

\kappa=0
if and only if
P_{12}=1
;

(iii)

\kappa=1
if and only if
P_{12}\geqslant 2
and
K_{S}^{2}=0
for
S
minimal;

(iv)

\kappa=2
if and only if
P_{12}\geqslant 2
and
K_{S}^{2}>0
for
S
minimal.

More precisely:

(a)

P_{12}=0
if and only if
S
is ruled, i.e., birationally equivalent to
C\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, where
C
is a smooth curve of genus
g(C)=q(C)
; if
S
is minimal, then either
S={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
or
S
is a scroll over a curve;

(b)

P_{12}=1
if and only if
12K_{S}
is trivial, with the following subcases for
S
minimal:

(I)

p_{g}=1
,
q=2
if and only if
S
is an abelian surfaces, in which case
K_{S}
is trivial;

(II)

p_{g}=1
,
q=0
if and only if
S
is a K3 surface, in which case
K_{S}
is trivial;

(III)

p_{g}=0
,
q=0
if and only if
S
is an Enriques surface, in which case
K_{S}
is non–trivial, but
2K_{S}
is trivial;

(IV)

p_{g}=0
,
q=1
if and only if
S
is a bielliptic surface, in which case
K_{S}
is non–trivial, but
nK_{S}
is trivial, for
n\in\{2,3,4,6\}
.

(c)

P_{12}\geq 2
and
K_{S}^{2}=0
for
S
minimal if and only if
S
is properly elliptic and
\varphi_{|12K_{S}|}
provides an elliptic fibration over a curve (up to eliminating fixed components and up to Stein factorization);

(d)

P_{12}\geq 2
and
K_{S}^{2}>0
for
S
minimal if and only if
S
of general type, in which case
\varphi_{|nK_{S}|}
is a morphism which is birational onto its image, as soon as
n\geqslant 6
.

Proof.

The second part of the theorem follows from the classification we already made. So it suffices to prove (i)–(iv).
The proof follows the one by Catanese–Li in
[
7
]
and by Francia
[
10
]
and it is a case by case analysis which follows the classification.

If
\kappa=-\infty
then
P_{n}=0
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, in particular
P_{12}=0
. Then (i) follows as soon as we prove (ii)–(iv). If
\kappa=0
, following the classification, we have the cases (I)–(IV) listed in the statement of the Theorem. For all of them we have
P_{12}=1
. Then (ii) follows once we prove (iii) and (iv). On the other hand, if
\kappa=2
and if
S
is minimal we have (
19
) and
K^{2}_{S}>0
, hence
P_{12}\geqslant 2
. Conversely, if
S
is minimal,
P_{12}\geqslant 2
and
K^{2}_{S}>0
, then
\kappa=2
. Hence we only have to consider case (iii), i.e., the
\kappa=1
case.

If
P_{12}\geqslant 2
, then
\kappa\geqslant 1
. Moreover, if
K_{S}^{2}=0
on a minimal model
S
, then
\kappa<2
, hence we have
\kappa=1
. Thus the theorem follows from the:

Proposition 14.2
.

If
\kappa=1
one has:

(1)

P_{12}\geqslant 2
;

(2)

there a positive integer
n\leqslant 4
such that
P_{n}\neq 0
;

(3)

there a positive integer
n\leqslant 8
such that
P_{n}\geqslant 2
;

(4)

for all integers
n\geqslant 14
one has
P_{n}\geqslant 2
.

Example 14.3
.

The bounds in Proposition
14.2
are sharp, as the following examples show.

(i) Cases (2) and (4).
Consider the group
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}_{6}
, which is a subgroup of translations of any elliptic curve
C_{1}
, in such a way that
C_{1}/G
is still elliptic.

By Riemann’s Existence Theorem
2.17
there is a curve
C_{2}
with a
G
action, such that
C_{2}/G={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and the quotient morphism
f:C_{2}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
of degree
m=12
is branched at three points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
with local monodromies

g_{1}=(1,0),\quad g_{2}=(0,1),\quad g_{3}=(1,5)

with respective orders
m_{1}=2
,
m_{2}=6
,
m_{3}=6
. Thus over
p_{1}
there are 6 points with simple ramification, and over
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
there are 2 points with ramification index 6. By Riemann–Hurwitz formula one has

22+2g(C_{2})=2m+2g(C_{2})-2=26\quad\mbox{hence}\quad g(C_{2})=2.

Now
G
acts diagonally on
C_{1}\times C_{2}
and let
S=C_{1}\times C_{2}/G
. Then we have the quotient map

S\to C_{2}/G={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with elliptic fibres and with exactly 3 multiple fibres with multiplicities
2,6,6
.
Moreover
K^{2}_{S}=e(S)=0
. Hence, according to the canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations, we have

P_{n}(S)=\operatorname{max}\{0,-2n+1+\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big]+2\Big[\frac{5n}{6}\Big]\}

thus

P_{1}=P_{2}=P_{3}=0,P_{4}=P_{5}=1,P_{6}=2,P_{13}=1.

(ii) Cases (2) and (3).
Here we set
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{10}
which acts on any elliptic curve
C_{1}
with elliptic quotient
C_{1}/G
. By Riemann’s Existence Thoerem, we find a curve
C_{2}
with a
G
action such that
C_{2}/G={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and the degree 10 quotient map
C_{2}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is ramified at 3 points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
with local monodromies

g_{1}=5,\quad g_{2}=4,\quad g_{3}=1

with respective orders
m_{1}=2
,
m_{2}=5
,
m_{3}=10
. Thus over
p_{1}
there are 5 points with simple ramification, over
p_{2}
there are 2 points with ramification index 5 and over
p_{3}
there is one point with total ramification index. Riemann–Hurwitz formula
says

18+2g(C_{2})=2m+2g(C_{2})-2=22\quad\mbox{hence}\quad g(C_{2})=2.

As above,
G
acts diagonally on
C_{1}\times C_{2}
and we set
S=C_{1}\times C_{2}/G
. Then we have an obvious map

S\to C_{2}/G={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with elliptic fibres and with exactly 3 multiple fibres with multiplicities
2,5,10
.
Again
K^{2}_{S}=e(S)=0
and therefore

P_{n}(S)=\operatorname{max}\{0,-2n+1+\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big]+\Big[\frac{4n}{5}\Big]+\Big[\frac{9n}{10}\Big]\}

thus

P_{1}=P_{2}=P_{3}=0,P_{4}=P_{5}=P_{6}=P_{7}=1,P_{8}=P_{9}=2,P_{10}=3,P_{11}=1,P_{12}=P_{13}=2.

Proof of Proposition
14.2
.

By Castelnuovo–De Franchis–Enriques’ Theorem, we have
\chi\geqslant 0
and
K_{S}^{2}=0
if
S
is a minimal model. Suppose we have an elliptic fibration
f:S\to C
, with
g=g(C)
.

First suppose
\chi\geqslant 1
and
g\geqslant 1
. Then the canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations implies that

P_{n}\geqslant n(2g-2+\chi)-g+1=n\chi+(g-1)(2n-1)\geqslant n

proving the assertion. Next assume
\chi=0
and
g\geqslant 2
. By the same computation we get
P_{n}\geqslant 2n-1
, again proving the assertion. If
\chi=0,g=1
, then there are multiple fibres of the elliptic fibration, otherwise the canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations would imply that
K_{S}\equiv 0
, which is not possible. Then we have

(28)

nK_{S}\sim\eta+n\sum_{i=1}^{h}(m_{i}-1)F_{i}\equiv\eta+\sum_{i=1}^{h}\Big[n\frac{m_{i}-1}{m_{i}}\Big]\cdot F+D

where
F
is a general fibre of
f
,
h>0
,
\eta
is the pull–back to
S
via
f
of a degree 0 line bundle on
C
and
D
is an effective, integral divisor. Hence

(29)

P_{n}\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{h}\Big[n\frac{m_{i}-1}{m_{i}}\Big]\geqslant\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big]

whence the assertion follows.

So we are left with the case
g=0
. If
\chi\geqslant 3
, then again

P_{n}\geqslant n(\chi-2)+1\geqslant n+1

which proves the assertion. If
\chi=2
the same argument as above tells us that (
28
) and (
29
) hold, proving the assertion again. So we are left with
g=0
and
0\leqslant\chi\leqslant 1
. In both cases the
canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations implies that
p_{g}=0
.

Case 14.4
.

g=0
,
\chi=1

We have

K_{S}\sim-F+\sum_{i=1}^{h}(m_{i}-1)F_{i}.

Since
\kappa=1
, then some multiple of
K_{S}
is equivalent to an effective divisor. Hence for any ample divisor
A
on
S
, we have

(30)

0<A\cdot K_{S}=\Big(-1+\sum_{i=1}^{h}\frac{m_{i}-1}{m_{i}}\Big)F\cdot A\quad\mbox{hence}\quad h-1>\sum_{i=1}^{h}\frac{1}{m_{i}}.

This implies
h\geqslant 2
. If
h\geqslant 3
we have

nK_{S}\sim-nF+\sum_{i=1}^{h}\Big[n\frac{m_{i}-1}{m_{i}}\Big]F+D

where
D
is an effective, integral divisor. On the right hand side the coefficient of
F
is

\begin{split}&-n+\Big[n\Big(1-\frac{1}{m_{1}}\Big)\Big]+\cdots+\Big[n\Big(1-\frac{1}{m_{h}}\Big)\Big]\geqslant\\
&\geqslant-n+h\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big]\geqslant\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big]\end{split}

proving the assertion. If
h=2
, we must have
m_{i}\geqslant 3
for at least an
i\in\{1,2\}
. Hence

P_{n}\geqslant\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big]+\Big[\frac{2n}{3}\Big]-n+1

hence
P_{n}\geqslant 1
for
n\geqslant 3
and
P_{n}\geqslant 2
for
n\geqslant 8
, proving again the assertion.

Case 14.5
.

g=0
,
\chi=0

Now we have

(31)

K_{S}\sim-2F+\sum_{i=1}^{h}(m_{i}-1)F_{i}

where we assume
m_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant m_{h}
. Recall that

(32)

P_{n}\geqslant\operatorname{max}\Big\{0,1-2n+\sum_{i=1}^{h}\Big[n\frac{m_{i}-1}{m_{i}}\Big]\Big\}.

If
h\geqslant 5
, we have

P_{n}\geqslant 1-2n+5\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big].

If
n=2m+\epsilon
, with
0\leqslant\epsilon\leqslant 1
, we get

P_{n}\geqslant 1-4m+5m-2\epsilon=1+m-2\epsilon\geqslant\begin{cases}1&\quad\mbox{for}\quad n\geqslant 4\\
2&\quad\mbox{for}\quad n\geqslant 6\end{cases}

whence the assertion follows.

Next we assume
h\leqslant 4
. Similarly to (
30
), we get

(33)

h-2>\sum_{i=1}^{h}\frac{1}{m_{i}},

which implies
h\geqslant 3
. Furthermore we make the following:

Claim 14.6
.

For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,h\}
,
m_{i}
divides the least common multiple of
m_{1},\ldots
,
m_{i-1},m_{i+1},\dots,m_{h}
.

Let us take this claim for granted for the time being. Assume first
h=4
. Observe that the right hand side of (
32
) is increasing in the
m_{i}
s. The worst case for the sequence of the
m_{i}
s is
(2,2,3,3)
, because
(2,2,2,2)
is not a solution of (
33
) and
(2,2,2,3)
does not verify Claim
14.6
. For
(2,2,3,3)
we obtain

P_{n}\geqslant 1-2n+2\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big]+2\Big[\frac{2n}{3}\Big]=1+\Big(2\Big[\frac{n}{2}\Big]-n\Big)+\Big(2\Big[\frac{2n}{3}\Big]-n\Big).

If
n=2k
we get

P_{n}=1+2\Big[\frac{k}{3}\Big]\geqslant\begin{cases}1\quad\mbox{if}\quad k\geqslant 1\\
3\quad\mbox{if}\quad k\geqslant 3\end{cases}

If
n=2k+1
we get

P_{n}=2\Big[\frac{4k+2}{3}\Big]-2k-1=2\Big[\frac{k+2}{3}\Big]-1\geqslant\begin{cases}1\quad\mbox{if}\quad k\geqslant 1\\
3\quad\mbox{if}\quad k\geqslant 4\end{cases}

proving the assertion.

Finally we assume
h=3
. Suppose that
m_{1}\geqslant 4
. Then, as above, the worst case for the sequence of the
m_{i}
s is
(4,4,4)
. Then set
n=4k+\epsilon
, with
0\leqslant\epsilon\leqslant 3
. Hence

\begin{split}&P_{n}\geqslant 3\Big[\frac{3n}{4}\Big]-2n+1=3\Big(3k+\Big[\frac{3\epsilon}{4}\Big]\Big)-8k-2\epsilon+1=\\
&=1+k+3\Big[\frac{3\epsilon}{4}\Big]-2\epsilon=\begin{cases}k+1\quad\mbox{for}\quad\epsilon=0,3\\
k\quad\quad\,\,\,\,\mbox{for}\quad\epsilon=2\\
k-1\quad\mbox{for}\quad\epsilon=1.\end{cases}\end{split}

Thus
P_{3}=1,P_{4}=2,P_{n}\geqslant 2
for
n\geqslant 10
, proving the assertion.

Assume next
m_{1}=3
. Since
3
divides the least common multiple of
m_{2}
and
m_{3}
, then either
3
divides both
m_{2}
and
m_{3}
or it divides only one of the two. So we have the following possibilities for
m_{2},m_{3}
:

\bullet

(m_{2},m_{3})=(3a,3b)
: then
a
divides
b
and
b
divides
a
, thus
a=b
and the worst case is
(3,6,6)
;

\bullet

\{m_{2},m_{3}\}=\{c,3b\}
, with
c
not divisible by
3
: then
3b
divides
3c
, hence
b
divides
c
and moreover
c
divides
b
, thus
b=c
and the worst case is
(3,4,12)
.

Then we have:

\mbox{in the case}\quad(3,6,6):P_{n}=\operatorname{max}\{0,F(n)\},\quad\mbox{with}\quad F(n):=1-2n+\Big[\frac{2n}{3}\Big]+2\Big[\frac{5n}{6}\Big]

and

\mbox{in the case}\quad(3,4,12):P_{n}=\operatorname{max}\{0,G(n)\},\quad\mbox{with}\quad G(n):=1-2n+\Big[\frac{2n}{3}\Big]+\Big[\frac{3n}{4}\Big]+\Big[\frac{11n}{12}\Big].

In the former case, write
n=6k+\epsilon
, with
0\leqslant\epsilon\leqslant 5
. We get

F(n)=2k+F(\epsilon),\quad\mbox{and}\quad F(\epsilon)=1,-1,0,1,1,2\quad\mbox{for}\quad\epsilon=0,\ldots,5,

thus
P_{3}\geqslant 1
,
P_{5}\geqslant 2
and
P_{n}\geqslant 2
for
n\geqslant 8
, proving the assertion.

In the latter case, write
n=12k+\epsilon
, with
0\leqslant\epsilon\leqslant 11
. We get

G(n)=4k+G(\epsilon),\quad\mbox{and}\quad G(\epsilon)=1,-1,0,1,1,1,2,2,3,3,3,4\quad\mbox{for}\quad\epsilon=0,\ldots,11,

thus
P_{n}\geqslant 1
for
n\geqslant 3
and
P_{n}\geqslant 2
for
n\geqslant 6
, proving the assertion.

Finally, we assume
m_{1}=2
. Then one of
m_{2},m_{3}
is even. In case
m_{2}=2a,m_{3}=2b
, we have
a=b
and the worst case is
(2,6,6)
which was considered in Example
14.3
, (i). If
\{m_{2},m_{3}\}=\{c,2b\}
, with
c
odd, then
c
has to divide
b
and
2b
divides
2c
, hence
b
divides
c
, thus
b=c
. The worst case is then
(2,5,10)
which was considered in Example
14.3
, (ii).

In conclusion we have to give the:

Proof of Claim
14.6
.

First, note that by (
31
), we have
p_{g}=0
. Since
\chi=0
, we have
q=1
, hence the Albanese map is a morphism
\alpha:S\to E
, with
E
an elliptic curve, with fibres of genus
g>1
, because,
S
having
\kappa=1
, it cannot have two different elliptic fibrations. Note also that, since all fibres of the elliptic pencil
f:S\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
map to
E
via
\alpha
, no fibre of
f
can be rational, hence all fibres of
f
are smooth, except the multiple fibres.
We denote by
D
the general fibre of the Albanese map and, as usual, by
F
the general fibre of
f
. More specifically, if
y\in E
[resp.,
x\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
] , we denote by
D_{y}
[resp., by
F_{x}
] the fibre of
\alpha
[resp., of
f
] over
y
[resp., over
x
].

Note that the general Albanese fibre
D
maps to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
via
f_{|D}
.
For each point
p\in D
we have that
p
is a ramification point of order
\nu
for
f_{|D}:D\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
if and only if the intersection multiplicity of
D
and
F_{x}
is
\nu
, with
x=f(p)
. Let now
F_{x}
be reduced.
The morphism
\alpha_{|F_{x}}:F_{x}\to E
cannot be ramified at
p
, because it is the map between two elliptic curves. Thus the only ramification points of
f_{|D}:D\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
are the intersections of
D
with the multiple fibres
m_{i}F_{i}
of
f
, and their ramification order is exactly
m_{i}
, with
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
the points such that
F_{x_{i}}=m_{i}F_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
.

Claim 14.7
.

The cover
f_{|D}:D\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is Galois, with an abelian monodromy group.

Proof of Claim
14.7
.

For
x\neq x_{i}
, with
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
, consider the smooth elliptic curve
F_{x}
and the étale cover
\alpha_{|F_{x}}:F_{x}\to E
, which has degree
n=D\cdot F
and is Galois with abelian monodromy group, because
\pi_{1}(E)
is abelian. We abuse notation and denote
\alpha_{|F_{x}}
simply by
\alpha
.

First we show that the subgroup
\alpha_{*}(\pi_{1}(F_{x}))
of
\pi_{1}(E)
does not depend on
x
. Indeed, if
x^{\prime}\neq x
, choose a path
\gamma:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}-\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\}
such that
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=x^{\prime}
. Consider the map

q\in f^{-1}(\gamma)\to(\alpha(q),f(q))\in E\times\gamma.

If
c
is a loop in
\alpha_{*}(\pi_{1}(F_{x}))
, let
\sigma(c\times\gamma)
be a lifting on
f^{-1}(\gamma)
of the cycle
c\times\gamma
. Since
\sigma(c\times\gamma(0))
is a closed path, the same happens for its homotopic path
\sigma(c\times\gamma(1))
, and this gives us a map

\alpha_{*}(\pi_{1}(F_{x}))\to\alpha_{*}(\pi_{1}(F_{x^{\prime}}))
which is clearly an identification.

Consider then the group

G=\frac{\pi_{1}(E)}{\alpha_{*}(\pi_{1}(F_{x}))}

which does not depend on
x\neq x_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
. This group acts on the fibres
F_{x}
in the following way

(g,q)\in G\times F_{x}\mapsto\sigma_{x}(g(1))\in F_{x}.

where
g:[0,1]\to E
is a loop on
E
and
\sigma_{x}(g)
is the lifting on
F_{x}
of the loop
g
such that
\sigma_{x}(g(0))=q
. In particular we obtain an action of
G
on the fibres of the étale cover

f:D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}-\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\}.

Since
G
acts freely, because of the way we defined the action, we get an injective homomorphism

(34)

G\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}\Big(D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\Big).

Since the curves
F_{x}
are irreducible,
G
acts transitively on the fibres, hence also

\operatorname{Aut}\Big(D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\Big)
acts transitively, i.e.,

f:D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}-\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\}

is Galois with group
G
.∎

As a consequence of the claim, the homorphism in (
34
) is an isomorphism. Moreover we have

\operatorname{Aut}\Big(D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\Big)\simeq\frac{\pi_{1}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}-\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\})}{f_{*}\pi_{1}\Big(D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\Big)}.

Hence finally

G\simeq\frac{\pi_{1}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}-\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\})}{f_{*}\pi_{1}\Big(D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\Big)}

and since
G
is abelian, the same happens for the group on the right hand side.

Let us look at this group more closely. First of all,
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}-\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\})
is the free group generated by
h
loops
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{h}
around the points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}
respectively, with the only relation
\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{h}={\rm id}
.
Moreover, since the ramification order at
x_{i}
is
m_{i}
, we have that
\gamma_{i}^{r}\in f_{*}\pi_{1}\Big(D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\Big)
if and only if
r\equiv 0
mod.
m_{i}
. Hence in the group

\frac{\pi_{1}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}-\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{h}\})}{f_{*}\pi_{1}\Big(D-\{f^{-1}(x_{1}),\ldots,f^{-1}(x_{h})\}\Big)}

we have the relations

\gamma_{1}\dots\gamma_{h}={\rm id},\quad\gamma_{i}^{m_{i}}={\rm id},\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant h.

Since the group in question is abelian, we have

\gamma_{i}^{-1}=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{i-1}\gamma_{i+1}\cdots\gamma_{h},\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant h.

If
M_{i}
is the least common multiple of
m_{1},\ldots m_{i-1},m_{i+1}\cdots m_{h}
we have

(\gamma_{i}^{-1})^{M_{i}}=(\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{i-1}\gamma_{i+1}\cdots\gamma_{h})^{M_{i}}=\text{id},

hence
m_{i}
has to divide
M_{i}
, for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant h
.∎

This ends the proof of Proposition
14.2
. ∎

Hence also the proof of Theorem
14.1
is finished.∎

15.
The Sarkisov’s Programme

This section is devoted to describe the birational maps between Mori fibre spaces in the rational case. This goes under the name of Sarkisov’s Programme. It is an essential complement of the minimal model programme, since it clarifies the relation between the various outcomes of the programme when they are Mori fibre spaces.

15.1.
Sarkisov’s links

The Sarkisov’s links are diagrams of maps, which are the basic bricks for the construction of birational maps between Mori fibre spaces. They are as follows:

Type I
:

\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}

where
p
is a point,
\mathbb{F}_{1}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is the blow–up of a point and
\mathbb{F}_{1}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is the obvious map which makes
\mathbb{F}_{1}
a scroll over
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Type III
(inverse of type I):

\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p}

with the same meaning of the maps as above.

Type II
:

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{C}

where
S\to C
and
S^{\prime}\to C
are
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundles, and
S\leftarrow Z\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is an elementary transformation (see Example
5.9
).

Type IV
:

\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{0}={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=\mathbb{F}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}

where
p
is a point, and
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are the projections onto the first and second factor of the product.

15.2.
Noether–Castelnuovo’s Theorem: statement

The main objective of this section is to prove the following:

Theorem 15.1

(Noether–Castelnuovo’s Theorem)
.

Suppose we have

(35)

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}}

where
S\to C
and
S^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}
are Mori fibre spaces and
\phi:S\dasharrow S^{\prime}
is a birational map. Then
\phi
can be factored through a finite sequence of Sarkisov’s links.

The rest of the section is devoted to prove this theorem.

15.3.
The Sarkisov’s degree

Consider a situation as in (
35
).

Lemma 15.2
.

There is an ample divisor
A^{\prime}
on
C^{\prime}
such that
-K_{S^{\prime}}+p^{\prime*}(A^{\prime})
is ample on
S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If
C^{\prime}
is a point, then
S^{\prime}={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and the assertion is clear. Suppose that
C^{\prime}
is a curve, so that
S^{\prime}
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle over
C^{\prime}
. Then
\operatorname{Num}(S^{\prime})\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, generated by the classes of a section
\Gamma
of
S^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}
and of a fibre
F
of
S^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}
. The class of
F
generates an extremal ray of
\overline{\rm NE}(S^{\prime})
, and the other extremal ray of
\overline{\rm NE}(S^{\prime})
is generated by the class of
a\Gamma-hF
, with
a,h
suitable positive integers.

We have
K_{S^{\prime}}\equiv-2\Gamma+xF
, with
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, and therefore the assertion is proved if we prove that there is a
y\gg 0
such that
yF+2\Gamma
is ample. Now

F\cdot(yF+2\Gamma)=2
and
(yF+2\Gamma)\cdot(a\Gamma-hF)=ay-2a\Gamma^{2}-2h
, which is positive for
y\gg 0
. Hence
yF+2\Gamma
is positive on
\overline{\rm NE}(S^{\prime})
for
y\gg 0
, which proves the assertion.
∎

We fix once and for all a very ample line bundle
H^{\prime}
of the form

H^{\prime}=-\mu^{\prime}K_{S^{\prime}}+p^{\prime*}(A^{\prime}),\quad\text{ with}\quad\mu^{\prime}\gg 0

and
A^{\prime}
very ample on
S^{\prime}
(see Lemma
15.2
), and we set
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}=|H^{\prime}|
. Next, consider a resolution of indeterminacies

(36)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}}

We may assume that
\sigma^{\prime}
is composed with the minimal number of blow–ups. In particular, if
\sigma^{\prime}
is not the identity there is a curve on
X
contracted by
\sigma^{\prime}
but not by
\sigma
.

For each divisor
D^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
we define its
transformed
via
\phi
to be
D:=\sigma_{*}\sigma^{\prime*}(D^{\prime})
. As
D^{\prime}
varies in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
,
D
varies in a linear system
{\mathcal{L}}
, called the
transformed
linear system of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
. Note that:

\bullet

{\mathcal{L}}
can be incomplete;

\bullet

{\mathcal{L}}
depends only on
\phi
and not on the resolution of the indeterminacies (
36
);

\bullet

{\mathcal{L}}
has no base curve, hence it is nef.

Next we define the
quasi–effective threshold
of
\phi
to be the real number
\mu
such that for every curve
F
contracted by
p
one has

(\mu K_{S}+D)\cdot F=0.

Note that:

\bullet

\mu
is rational and it is positive because
K_{S}\cdot F<0
and
D
is nef;

\bullet

\mu
depends only on
{\mathcal{L}}
and not on the curve
D\in{\mathcal{L}}
;

\bullet

since
F
generates an extremal ray which is also generated by the class of a smooth rational curve
\ell
such that
-3\leqslant\ell\cdot K_{S}\leqslant-2
,
\mu
is defined by the relation

(\mu K_{S}+D)\cdot\ell=0\quad\text{hence}\quad\mu=-\frac{D\cdot\ell}{K_{S}\cdot\ell}\in\frac{1}{6}\mathbb{Z}.

Then we define the
maximal multiplicity

\lambda
of
{\mathcal{L}}
as follows. We have

(37)

K_{X}=\sigma^{*}(K_{S})+\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}E_{k},

where
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
are the distinct curves contracted by
\sigma
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
are positive integers. Moreover

(38)

{\mathcal{L}}_{X}:=\sigma^{\prime*}({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})=\sigma^{*}({\mathcal{L}})-\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{k}E_{k},

where
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
are non–negative integers. We define

\lambda:=\operatorname{max}\Big\{\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}}\Big\}_{k=1,\ldots,n}.

One has:

\bullet

\lambda
is attained at
proper points
,
which implies that if
\lambda=\frac{b_{i}}{a_{i}}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we may assume that
a_{i}=1
and therefore
\lambda
can be also defined as the maximum multiplicity of a base point of
{\mathcal{L}}
.
In fact, suppose we have a proper point
p_{1}
and a sequence
p_{2},\ldots,p_{j}
of infinitely near points to
p_{1}
, each infinitely near to the other (see §
16.1
below). Let
E_{1},\ldots,E_{j}
be the proper transform on
X
of the exceptional divisors of the blow–ups at
p_{1},\ldots,p_{j}
. Then the total transforms of the exceptional divisors are of the form

E_{1}+\ldots+r_{1}E_{j},\quad E_{2}+\ldots+r_{2}E_{j},\quad\ldots,\quad E_{j},\quad\text{with}\quad r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,\quad\text{positive integers}.

Hence
a_{1}=1
and

a_{j}=r_{1}+r_{2}+\cdots+r_{j-1}+1.

If
{\mathcal{L}}
has multiplicities
m_{1},\ldots,m_{j}
at
p_{1},\ldots,p_{j}
, one has
m_{1}\geqslant m_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant m_{j}
,
b_{1}=m_{1}
and

b_{j}=r_{1}m_{1}+r_{2}m_{2}+\cdots+r_{j-1}m_{j-1}+m_{j}.

Hence
\frac{b_{1}}{a_{1}}=m_{1}
, whereas

\frac{b_{j}}{a_{j}}=\frac{r_{1}m_{1}+r_{2}m_{2}+\cdots+r_{j-1}m_{j-1}+m_{j}}{r_{1}+r_{2}+\cdots+r_{j-1}+1}\leqslant\frac{r_{1}m_{1}+r_{2}m_{1}+\cdots+r_{j-1}m_{1}+m_{1}}{r_{1}+r_{2}+\cdots+r_{j-1}+1}=m_{1}.

\bullet

in particular
\lambda=0
if and only if
{\mathcal{L}}
is base point free;

\bullet

if
\lambda>0
, one has

K_{X}+\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathcal{L}}_{X}=\sigma^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathcal{L}})+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\big(a_{k}-\frac{1}{\lambda}b_{k}\big)E_{k}

and

a_{k}-\frac{1}{\lambda}b_{k}\geqslant 0,\quad\text{and the equality holds for some}\quad k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

Finally we introduce the number
\ell
of
crepant exceptional divisors
. When
\lambda=0
then
\ell
is undetermined. If
\lambda>0
then we set

\ell:=\quad\text{the order of the set}\quad\Big\{k:\lambda=\frac{b_{k}}{a_{k}}\Big\}.

The triple
(\mu,\lambda,\ell)
is, by definition, the
Sarkisov’s degree
of
\phi
.

15.4.
The Noether–Fano–Iskovkikh Theorem

We keep the above notation and prove the:

Theorem 15.3

(Noether–Fano–Iskovkikh’s Theorem)
.

Suppose that
\lambda\leqslant\mu
and that
K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}
is nef. Then
\phi
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The proof will be divided in various steps.

15.5.
Step 1: one has
\mu=\mu^{\prime}

Recall that, as in Lemma
15.2
, we have
H^{\prime}+\mu^{\prime}K_{S^{\prime}}=p^{\prime*}(A^{\prime})
, with
H^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}
very ample. Hence, for all
\epsilon>0

(1+\epsilon)H^{\prime}+\mu^{\prime}K_{S^{\prime}}=\epsilon H^{\prime}+H^{\prime}+\mu^{\prime}K_{S^{\prime}}=\epsilon H^{\prime}+p^{\prime*}(A^{\prime})

is ample. Therefore

\frac{1}{t}H^{\prime}+K_{S^{\prime}}\quad\text{is ample for}\quad 0<t<\mu^{\prime}.

Hence its transformed system via
\phi
intersects positively any movable curve, in particular the curves
F
contracted to points by
p:S\to C
.

One has

\begin{split}&\sigma^{\prime*}(\frac{1}{t}H^{\prime}+K_{S^{\prime}})=\frac{1}{t}\sigma^{\prime*}(H^{\prime})+(K_{X}-E)=\\
&=\frac{1}{t}\sigma^{\prime*}(H^{\prime})+(\sigma^{*}(K_{S})+\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}E_{k}-E)\end{split}

where
E
is an effective divisor. Hence

\sigma_{*}(\sigma^{\prime*}(\frac{1}{t}H^{\prime}+K_{S^{\prime}}))=\frac{1}{t}H+K_{S}-\sigma_{*}(E)\quad\text{where}\quad H:=\sigma_{*}(\sigma^{\prime*}(H^{\prime})).

So, if
F
is a (movable, hence nef) curve contracted to a point by
p:S\to C
, and if
0<t<\mu^{\prime}
, we have

(\frac{1}{t}H+K_{S})\cdot F=\sigma_{*}(\sigma^{\prime*}(\frac{1}{t}H^{\prime}+K_{S^{\prime}}))\cdot F+\sigma_{*}(E)\cdot F\geqslant\sigma_{*}(\sigma^{\prime*}(\frac{1}{t}H^{\prime}+K_{S^{\prime}}))\cdot F>0

hence
\mu\geqslant\mu^{\prime}
.

It is worth noticing that so far we did not use that
K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}H
is nef. This will enter soon into play. In fact we have

(\frac{1}{t}H^{\prime}+K_{S^{\prime}})\cdot F^{\prime}<0\quad\text{for}\quad t>\mu^{\prime}

where
F^{\prime}
is a curve contracted to a point by
p^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\to C^{\prime}
. Indeed, for
t>\mu^{\prime}
we have

F^{\prime}\cdot(H^{\prime}+tK_{S^{\prime}})=F^{\prime}\cdot(p^{\prime*}(A^{\prime})+(t-\mu^{\prime})K_{S^{\prime}})=(t-\mu^{\prime})F^{\prime}\cdot K_{S^{\prime}}<0.

Then transforming by
\phi
we get that
\frac{1}{t}H+K_{S}-\sigma_{*}(E)
is not nef for
t>\mu^{\prime}
. Actually, if
Z
is the transform of
F^{\prime}
, we have
F^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{\prime}_{*}(E)=0
, hence
Z\cdot\sigma_{*}(E)=0
, thus

(\frac{1}{t}H+K_{S})\cdot Z=(\frac{1}{t}H+K_{S}-\sigma_{*}(E))\cdot Z<0\quad\text{for}\quad t>\mu^{\prime}.

Since
K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}H
is nef, we must have
\mu\leqslant\mu^{\prime}
, thus
\mu=\mu^{\prime}
as wanted.

15.6.
Step 2: invariance of the adjoints

By (
37
) and (
38
), and since
\lambda\leqslant\mu
, we have

K_{X}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{X}=\sigma^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})+\sum_{k=1}^{n}r_{k}E_{k},\quad\text{with}\quad r_{k}=a_{k}-\frac{1}{\mu}b_{k}\geqslant a_{k}-\frac{1}{\lambda}b_{k}\geqslant 0

and

\sum_{k=1}^{n}r_{k}E_{k}\leqslant R:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}E_{k}

where
R
is the ramification divisor of
\sigma:X\to S
.

On the other hand

K_{X}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{X}=\sigma^{\prime*}(K_{S^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})+R^{\prime},\quad\text{with}\quad R^{\prime}=\sum_{h=1}^{m}r^{\prime}_{h}E^{\prime}_{h}

where
R^{\prime}
is the ramification divisor of
\sigma^{\prime}
, with
E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{m}
the irreducible curves contracted to points by
\sigma^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,r^{\prime}_{m}
are positive integers.

The aim of this step is to prove that

(39)

\sum_{k=1}^{n}r_{k}E_{k}=\sum_{h=1}^{m}r^{\prime}_{h}E^{\prime}_{h}

which implies that

(40)

\sigma^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})=\sigma^{\prime*}(K_{S^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})

an equality which goes under the name of
invariance of the adjoints
.

To prove (
39
), we introduce the notation
F_{\ell}
to denote either the curves
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
or the curves
E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{m}
, with the convention that the index
\ell
varies in the following sets:

\bullet

\ell\in S_{\sigma}
if
F_{\ell}
is contracted to a point by
\sigma
but not by
\sigma^{\prime}
;

\bullet

\ell\in S_{\sigma^{\prime}}
if
F_{\ell}
is contracted to a point by
\sigma^{\prime}
but not by
\sigma
;

\bullet

\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}
if
F_{\ell}
is contracted to a point by both
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Then

\begin{split}\sum_{k=1}^{n}r_{k}E_{k}=\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma}}r_{\ell}F_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}}r_{\ell}F_{\ell},\\
\sum_{h=1}^{m}r^{\prime}_{h}E^{\prime}_{h}=\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma^{\prime}}}r^{\prime}_{\ell}F_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}}r^{\prime}_{\ell}F_{\ell}.\end{split}

We have

\sum_{k=1}^{n}r_{k}E_{k}+\sigma^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})=\sigma^{\prime*}(K_{S^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})+\sum_{h=1}^{m}r^{\prime}_{h}E^{\prime}_{h},

hence

(41)

\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma}}r_{\ell}F_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}}(r_{\ell}-r^{\prime}_{\ell})F_{\ell}=\sigma^{\prime*}(K_{S^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})-\sigma^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})+\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma^{\prime}}}r^{\prime}_{\ell}F_{\ell}

and similarly

(42)

\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma^{\prime}}}r^{\prime}_{\ell}F_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}}(r^{\prime}_{\ell}-r_{\ell})F_{\ell}=\sigma^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})-\sigma^{\prime*}(K_{S^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})+\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma}}r_{\ell}F_{\ell}.

At this point we need the following:

Lemma 15.4

(Negativity Lemma)
.

Let
f:V\to T
be a birational morphism between two surfaces
V
and
T
. Suppose
E=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}E_{i}
is an effective divisor contracted to points by
f
. Assume that for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, one has
E\cdot E_{i}\geqslant 0
. Then
a_{i}\leqslant 0
, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that there is a
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
a_{j}>0
. We have

\begin{split}&0\leqslant E\cdot(\sum_{a_{i}>0}a_{i}E_{i})=(\sum_{a_{i}\leqslant 0}a_{i}E_{i}+\sum_{a_{i}>0}a_{i}E_{i})\cdot(\sum_{a_{i}>0}a_{i}E_{i})=\\
&=(\sum_{a_{i}\leqslant 0}a_{i}E_{i})\cdot(\sum_{a_{i}>0}a_{i}E_{i}))+(\sum_{a_{i}>0}a_{i}E_{i})^{2}<0\end{split}

because
\sum_{a_{i}>0}a_{i}E_{i}
is contracted to points, so
(\sum_{a_{i}>0}a_{i}E_{i})^{2}<0
and
(\sum_{a_{i}\leqslant 0}a_{i}E_{i})\cdot(\sum_{a_{i}>0}a_{i}E_{i}))\leqslant 0
. Hence we have a contradiction, which proves the assertion.
∎

To apply this lemma, intersect both sides of (
41
) with
F_{s}
, with
s\in S_{\sigma}\cup S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}
. We get

(\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma}}r_{\ell}F_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}}(r_{\ell}-r^{\prime}_{\ell})F_{\ell})\cdot F_{s}=\sigma^{\prime*}(K_{S^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\cdot F_{s}+\sigma^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot F_{s}+(\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma^{\prime}}}r^{\prime}_{\ell}F_{\ell})\cdot F_{s}.

The first summand on the right hand side is non–negative, because
K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\mu}p^{\prime*}(A^{\prime})
is nef. The second summand is clearly 0. The third summand is non–negative. Hence

(\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma}}r_{\ell}F_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}}(r_{\ell}-r^{\prime}_{\ell})F_{\ell})\cdot F_{s}\geqslant 0,\quad\text{for all}\quad s\in S_{\sigma}\cup S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}.

By the Negativity Lemma we deduce that

\begin{split}&r_{\ell}\leqslant 0\quad\text{if}\quad\ell\in S_{\sigma},\\
&r_{\ell}\leqslant r^{\prime}_{\ell}\quad\text{if}\quad\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}.\end{split}

Arguing in the same way on (
42
), we get

\begin{split}&r^{\prime}_{\ell}\leqslant r_{\ell}\quad\text{if}\quad\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}},\\
&r^{\prime}_{\ell}\leqslant 0\quad\text{if}\quad\ell\in S_{\sigma^{\prime}}.\end{split}

In conclusion

\begin{split}&r_{\ell}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\ell\in S_{\sigma},\\
&r^{\prime}_{\ell}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\ell\in S_{\sigma^{\prime}},\\
&r_{\ell}=r^{\prime}_{\ell}\quad\text{if}\quad\ell\in S_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}.\end{split}

which proves (
39
).

15.7.
Step 3: conclusion

We have

R\geqslant\sum_{k=1}^{n}r_{k}E_{k}=\sum_{h=1}^{m}r^{\prime}_{h}E^{\prime}_{h}=R^{\prime}

and all the divisors contracted to points by
\sigma^{\prime}
are also contracted to points by
\sigma
. By the minimality assumption on
\sigma^{\prime}
, we have that
\sigma^{\prime}
is the identity, hence
X=S^{\prime}
and
\sigma=\phi^{-1}
.

Now consider the composite map
S^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\to}}S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}C
and let
\Gamma
be a general fibre of it.
Note that, since the general fibre of
p:S\to C
is connected, then the same happens for
\Gamma
.
By taking into account the definition of the quasi effective threshold, and by (
40
) proved in Step 2, we have

0=\Gamma\cdot\sigma^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})=\Gamma\cdot(K_{S^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\mu}\Gamma\cdot p^{\prime*}(A^{\prime})

This tells us that
\Gamma
is contracted to a point by
p^{\prime}
. This proves that there is a morphism
g:C\to C^{\prime}
which makes the following diagram commutative

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}}

Now note that
\phi^{-1}
is a birational morphism. If it is not an isomorphism, there is some curve
E
in
S^{\prime}
contracted to a point by
\phi^{-1}
. But then
E
is contracted to a point by
p^{\prime}
. Since the curves in fibres of
p^{\prime}
are all numerically equivalent, we clearly get a contradiction. This proves that
\phi^{-1}
, and hence
\phi
is an isomorphism, as wanted. ∎

15.8.
Sarkisov’s algorithm

In this section we prove the Noether–Castelnuovo’s theorem. The proof consists in performing an algorithm which lowers the Sarkisov’s degree, which is supposed to be lexicographically ordered. This algorithm is called
Sarkisov’s algorithm
or also the
untwisting process
.

We start with a diagram like (
35
), where the vertical arrows are Mori fibre spaces and
\phi
is a birational map, with Sarkisov degree
(\mu,\lambda,\ell)
. We have the dichotomy:

Case 1:

\lambda\leqslant\mu
,

Case 2:

\lambda>\mu
.

In case 1 we have the further dichotomy:

Case 1.1:

K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}
nef,

Case 1.2:

K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}
non–nef.

In case 1.1, by Noether–Fano–Iskovskih Theorem we conclude that
\phi
is an isomorphism, and the algorithm ends here.

In case 1.2,
p:S\to C
does not coincide with
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\to p
, where
p
is a point, because on
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
we would clearly have
K_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}\equiv 0
, hence
K_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}
would be nef, a contradiction. Hence
p:S\to C
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle over the curve
C
and
\operatorname{Num}(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
generated by the classes
H
of a section of
p:S\to C
, existing by the Noether–Enriques’ theorem, and
F
of a fibre of
p:S\to C
. Then

(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot F=0.

Since
K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}
is not nef, then
there is a class
D
of a curve generating an extremal ray, such that

(43)

(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot D<0,\quad\text{hence}\quad K_{S}\cdot D<0.

So there is another extremal contraction, the one contracting the curves in
R
, which we denote by
q:S\to T
. Since
S\neq{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, then either
q:S\to T
is the contraction of a
(-1)
–curve or it is another
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle.

Subcase 1.2.1:
q:S\to T
is the contraction of a
(-1)
–curve. In this case
T
is a surface and
\operatorname{Num}(T)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Consequently
T\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and

\textstyle{S=\mathbb{F}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{T={\mathbb{P}}^{2}:=S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{C={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x:=C_{1}}

is a link of type III.

Subcase 1.2.2:
q:S\to T
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
bundle different from
p:S\to C
. Then, since
T
is dominated by the fibres of
p:S\to C
, then
T\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. For the same reason
C\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Moreover
D
is the class of a fibre of
q:S\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, hence
D^{2}=0
.

Claim 15.5
.

One has
S={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and

\textstyle{S={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=S:=S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q:=p_{1}}
\textstyle{C={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}}^{1}:=C_{1}}

is a link of type IV.

Proof.

The final assertion follows once we prove that
S={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. To prove this, assume, to the contrary, that
S=\mathbb{F}_{a}
, with
a>0
. Then
S
has a unique section
E
of
p:S\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
such that
E^{2}=-a<0
. This is an extremal ray as well as
F
, hence, we should have that the ray spanned by
E
is the same as the ray spanned by
D
, hence
E^{2}=D^{2}=0
, a contradiction. ∎

Claim 15.6
.

In both subcases 1.2.1 and 1.2.2, we have a new Mori fibre space
p_{1}:S_{1}\to C_{1}
for which
\mu_{1}<\mu
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
be the transformed linear system on
S_{1}
and let
F_{1}
be the generator of the extremal ray contracted by
p_{1}
.

In subcase 1.2.1,
q:S=\mathbb{F}_{1}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}=S_{1}
is the contraction of a
(-1)
–curve
E
. Let
L
be the class of a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, then the class of a fibre of
p:S\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is

F=q^{*}(L)-E
and
K_{S}=q^{*}(-3L)+E
, whereas
{\mathcal{L}}
is of the form
{\mathcal{L}}=q^{*}(aL)-bE
, with
a,b
non–negative integers. We have

K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}=q^{*}\Big((-3+\frac{a}{\mu})L\Big)+(1-\frac{b}{\mu})E\quad\text{hence}\quad(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot E=\frac{b}{\mu}-1<0,

the last inequality holding because of (
43
). Then

0=(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot F=\Big(q^{*}\Big((-3+\frac{a}{\mu})L\Big)+(1-\frac{b}{\mu})E\Big)\cdot(q^{*}(L)-E)=-3+\frac{a}{\mu}+1-\frac{b}{\mu}>-3+\frac{a}{\mu},

hence

(K_{S_{1}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{1})\cdot F_{1}=(-3+\frac{a}{\mu})L\cdot L=-3+\frac{a}{\mu}<0

which implies that
\mu_{1}<\mu
.

In subcase 1.2.2, we have
S=S_{1}={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
F
and
F_{1}
are the two rulings. By (
43
) we have

(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot F_{1}<0

which again implies that
\mu_{1}<\mu
. ∎

In conclusion, in case 1, either the algorithm ends or, by making a Sarkisov’s link, we drop the Sarkisov’s degree.

Next, we have to discuss case 2, in which
\lambda>\mu
. Here we have two subcases:

Case 2.1:

S\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
;

Case 2.2:

p:S\to C
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle.

Let
x
be a (proper) point of
S
which realizes the maximal multiplicity
\lambda
of
{\mathcal{L}}
.

In case 2.1, blow–up
x
with exceptional divisor
E
, so that we have a type I Sarkisov’s link

\textstyle{S={\mathbb{P}}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{1}=S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{y}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Then, if
L
is the class of a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, we have
{\mathcal{L}}=aL
, with
a
a positive integer, and, with the usual notation, we have

\begin{split}&K_{S_{1}}=f^{*}(K_{S})+E=f^{*}(-3L)+E.\\
&{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=f^{*}({\mathcal{L}})-bE=f^{*}(aL)-bE,\quad\text{with}\quad b=\lambda>0,\\
&F_{1}=f^{*}(L)-E.\end{split}

Hence

K_{S_{1}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=f^{*}(-3L)+E+\frac{1}{\mu}\Big(f^{*}(aL)-bE\Big)=(-3+\frac{a}{\mu})f^{*}(L)+(1-\frac{b}{\mu})E

and therefore

(K_{S_{1}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{1})\cdot F_{1}=\Big((-3+\frac{a}{\mu})f^{*}(L)+(1-\frac{b}{\mu})E\Big)\cdot(f^{*}(L)-E)=-3+\frac{a}{\mu}-\frac{b}{\mu}+1<0

because

0=(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot L=(-3+\frac{a}{\mu})L\cdot L=-3+\frac{a}{\mu}

and
b=\lambda>\mu
. So, in this case too, we have
\mu_{1}<\mu
.

In case 2.2, we perform an elementary transformation at
x

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C_{1}}

Note the commutative diagram

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S_{1}}

If
F
denotes the fibre of
p
such that
x\in F
, the total transform on
Z
of
F
is of the form
F^{\prime}+E
, with
F^{\prime}
the strict transform of
F
and
E
the exceptional divisor. Then on
S_{1}
,
F^{\prime}
is contracted to a point
x_{1}
and the image
F_{1}
of
E
is the fibre of
p_{1}
through
x_{1}
.

Note that

0=(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot F=-2+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}\cdot F,\quad\text{thus}\quad{\mathcal{L}}\cdot F=2\mu.

Hence
x_{1}
is a point of multiplicity
2\mu-\lambda<\mu<\lambda
. So
\lambda_{1}\leqslant\lambda
and if equality holds, we decreased
\ell
. On the other hand we claim that
\mu_{1}=\mu
. Indeed, if we set
G=\alpha^{*}(F)=\beta^{*}(F_{1})
, with obvious meaning of the notation, we have

\begin{split}&K_{Z}=\alpha^{*}(K_{S})+E,\\
&{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}=\alpha^{*}({\mathcal{L}})-\lambda E,\\
&K_{Z}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}=\alpha^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})+(1-\frac{\lambda}{\mu})E,\\
\end{split}

hence

(K_{Z}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z})\cdot G=\alpha^{*}(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot\alpha^{*}(F)=(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot F=0

and, by the same computation

0=(K_{Z}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z})\cdot G=\beta^{*}(K_{S_{1}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{1})\cdot\beta^{*}(F_{1})=(K_{S_{1}}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{1})\cdot F_{1}

which proves that
\mu_{1}=\mu
, as wanted.

So, also in this case we lower the Sarkisov’s degree. The final observation is that the Sarkisov’s algorithm ends, because
\lambda
and
\ell
are non–negative integers and
\mu\in\frac{1}{6}\mathbb{Z}
.

In conclusion Sarkisov’s algorithm proves the Noether–Castelnuovo’s theorem, as wanted.

Example 15.7
.

Consider the
standard quadratic transformation

\varphi:[x,y,z]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\dasharrow[yz,xz,xy]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{2}.

We want to apply to
\varphi
the Sarkisov’s algorithm.

Let
L^{\prime}
be the class of a line in the target
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and
L
the class of a line in the source
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. The transformed system of
|L^{\prime}|
is the linear system
{\mathcal{L}}
of conics through the fundamental points
A=[1,0,0],B=[0,1,0],C=[0,0,1]
. Clearly we have
\lambda=1
and
\ell=3
, and
\mu
is computed by

0=(K_{S}+\frac{1}{\mu}{\mathcal{L}})\cdot L=(-3L+\frac{1}{\mu}\cdot 2L)\cdot L=-3+\frac{2}{\mu},\quad\text{hence}\quad\mu=\frac{2}{3}.

So we have
\lambda>\mu
.

Step 1: blow–up
A
. This is a type I link

\textstyle{S={\mathbb{P}}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{1}=S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}

If
L_{1}
is the class of the pull back of a line of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, and
F_{1}
a fibre of
p_{1}
, we have that
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
consists of the curves in
|F_{1}+L_{1}|
passing through the points
B_{1}
and
C_{1}
the images of
B
and
C
on
S_{1}
, so
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\cdot F_{1}=1
hence

(K_{S_{1}}+2{\mathcal{L}}_{1})\cdot F_{1}=0,

thus
\mu_{1}=\frac{1}{2}<\frac{2}{3}=\mu
, so we lowered the Sarkisov’s degree. Note that
\lambda_{1}=1
and
\ell_{1}=2
.

Step 2: make an elementary transformation at
B_{1}
. This is a type II link

\textstyle{S_{1}=\mathbb{F}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{0}=S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}

Let
F_{2}
be the fibre of
p_{2}
and
G
the other ruling of
\mathbb{F}_{0}
. The transform
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
on
S_{2}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
is now the linear system of the curves in
|F_{2}+G|
passing through the point
C_{2}
image of
C_{1}
on
S_{2}
. Hence we see that the new Sarkisov’s degree is
(\frac{1}{2},1,1)
, which has been lowered again.

Step 3: make an elementary transformation at
C_{2}
. This is a type II link

\textstyle{S_{2}=\mathbb{F}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{1}=S_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{3}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}

If
L_{3}
is the pull–back of a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
to
\mathbb{F}_{1}
, we have that
{\mathcal{L}}_{3}=|L_{3}|
and the Sarkisov’s degree now becomes
(\frac{1}{2},0,*)
, where
*
stays for undeterminate.

Step 4: blow down the
(-1)
–curve in
S_{3}=\mathbb{F}_{1}
. This is a type III link

\textstyle{S_{3}=\mathbb{F}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{3}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}=S_{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{4}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
\textstyle{x}

and the linear system
{\mathcal{L}}_{4}
is the linear system of the lines in
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. Then the Sarkisov’s degree is now
(\frac{1}{3},0,*)
.

16.
The classical Noether–Castelnuovo’s theorem

The classical Noether–Castelnuovo’s theorem is as follows:

Theorem 16.1

(Classical Noether–Castelnuovo’s theorem)
.

The group of birational transformations of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is generated by the projective transformations and by the standard quadratic transformation.

This theorem can be proved as a consequence of Noether–Castelnuovo’s theorem
15.1
. The idea is that elementary transformations appearing in the sequence of Sarkisov’s links composing a given birational transformation of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
can be rearranged in such a way as to give quadratic transformations as in Example
15.7
. However we will not do this, but we will give a direct proof of Theorem
16.1
which goes back to Castelnuovo and Alexander. This proof requires a number of preliminaries which we will now introduce.

16.1.
Infinitely near points

Let
\sigma:S^{\prime}\to S
a birational morphism of smooth, irreducible, projective surfaces. Then
\sigma
is the composition of finitely many blow–ups

\sigma:S^{\prime}=S_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{n}}}{{\to}}S_{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{n-1}}}{{\to}}\ldots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{2}}}{{\to}}S_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{1}}}{{\to}}S_{0}=S

at points
p_{i}\in S_{i}
, for
0\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
.
A point
p\in S
will be called a
proper point
. The point
q\in S_{i}
is called an
infinitely near point to
p
of order
i
, and we write
q>^{i}p
, if there are points
p_{j}\in S_{j}
such that
\sigma(p_{j})=p_{j-1}
for
0\leqslant j\leqslant n
and
p_{0}=p
and
p_{j}=q
.

For
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, let
E_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{-1}(p_{i-1})
be the exceptional divisor of the blow-up
\sigma_{i}:S_{i}\to S_{i-1}
. We denote by
E^{\prime}_{i,j}
the proper transform of
E_{i}
on
S_{j}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n
.

We say that
q
is
proximate
to
p
, and we write
q\to p
, if either
q>^{1}p
or
q>^{i}p
, with
i>1
and
q\in E_{1,j}
. In this later case we say that
q
is
satellite
to
p
and we write
q\odot p
.

If
C
is a curve on
S
, we say that
C

passes through
the infinitely near point
q>^{i}p
if the strict transform of
C
on
S_{i}
contains
q
. If
C
passes with multiplicity
m
through
p
and multiplicity
m_{i}
through a point
p_{i}\to p
, one has the obvious

proximity inequality

m\geqslant\sum_{p_{i}\to p}m_{j}.

Moreover, if a curve
C
on
S
passes through
p
and through a satellite point
q\odot p
, then
C
is singular at
p
. For details, see
[
6
]
.

16.2.
Homalodail nets

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be points in the plane, which can be proper or infinitely near. Fix positive integers
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}
, and consider the linear system

{\mathcal{L}}:={\mathcal{L}}(d;p_{1}^{m_{1}},\ldots,p_{n}^{m_{r}})

of plane curves of degree
d
having multiplicity at least
m_{i}
at
p_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
.
If the points are understood, we may simply write

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}(d;{m_{1}},\ldots,{m_{r}})

and if some multiplities are repeated we may sometimes use the exponential notation, i.e.
m_{i}^{j}
stays for
j
times
m_{i}
.
Since the
base points
can be infinitely near, we can understand the linear system
{\mathcal{L}}
as living on a suitable birational model
\sigma:S\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

Assume now
\dim({\mathcal{L}})=2
,
{\mathcal{L}}
with no fixed components, and the map

\phi_{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\dasharrow{\mathbb{P}}^{2}

determined by
{\mathcal{L}}
to be birational, i.e., a
Cremona transformation
. Then we call
{\mathcal{L}}
a
homaloidal net
and
{\mathcal{L}}
is the transform on the source
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
of the linear system of lines on the target
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
. For a homaloidal net we have

\begin{split}&d^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}^{2}=1\\
&{{d-1}\choose 2}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}{{m_{i}}\choose 2}=0\end{split}

where the first equality means that the curves in
{\mathcal{L}}
meet in one variable point off the base points and the second means that the curves in
{\mathcal{L}}
have geometric genus 0. The above relations also read

(44)

\begin{split}&d^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}^{2}=1\\
&3(d-1)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}\end{split}

Example 16.2
.

For any
d\geqslant 2
, the linear system

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}(d;d-1,1^{2d-2})

is a homaloidal net called a
De Jonquières net
and
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
is called a
De Jonquières transformation
. Note that such a map sends the pencil of lines through the point of multiplicity
d-1
to a pencil of lines. For
d=2
this is a
quadratic transformation
.

16.3.
The simplicity

Let
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}(d;m_{0},\ldots,m_{r})
be a homaloidal net, where we assume
m_{0}\geqslant\ldots\geqslant m_{r}
. If there is no base point, we set
r=-1
. Moreover we set
m_{-1}=\infty
and
m_{s}=0
for all integer
s>r
.

The
simplicity
of
{\mathcal{L}}
is the triplet
(k_{\mathcal{L}},h_{\mathcal{L}},s_{\mathcal{L}})
of integers defined as follows:

\begin{split}&k_{\mathcal{L}}:=d-m_{0};\\
&m_{h_{\mathcal{L}}}>\frac{k_{\mathcal{L}}}{2}\geqslant m_{h_{\mathcal{L}}+1};\\
&s_{\mathcal{L}}=\quad\text{the number of satellite points among}\quad p_{0},\ldots,p_{h_{\mathcal{L}}}.\end{split}

If
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\dasharrow{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is a Cremona transformation, the
simplicity
of
\phi
is, by definition, the simplicity of
{\mathcal{L}}
.

One says that
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
[resp., the Cremona transformation
\phi^{\prime}
] is
simpler
than
{\mathcal{L}}
[resp., than
\phi
] if the simplicity of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
[resp., of
\phi^{\prime}
] is lexicographically
smaller than the simplicity of
{\mathcal{L}}
[resp., of
\phi
].

Example 16.3
.

If
{\mathcal{L}}(d;d-1,1^{2d-2})
is a De Jonquières net, then its simplicity is
(1,2d-2,s)
, where
s
is the number of satellite points among the points of multiplicity 1 proximate to the point of multiplicity
d-1
. By the proximity inequality, one has
s\leqslant d-1
.

16.4.
The proof of the classical Noether–Castelnuovo’s theorem

We can now give the:

Proof of Theorem
16.1
.

Let
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\dasharrow{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
be a Cremona transformation. Assume it is neither a linear map nor a quadratic transformation. We will prove that there is a quadratic transformation
\gamma:{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\dasharrow{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
such that
\gamma\circ\phi
is simpler that
\phi
. This will prove the theorem.

Let

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}(\delta;p_{0}^{\alpha_{0}},\ldots,p_{r}^{\alpha_{r}})\quad\text{with}\quad\alpha_{0}\geqslant\ldots\geqslant\alpha_{r}.

By (
44
) we have

\begin{split}&0\geqslant\sum_{i=0}^{r}\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{0})=\sum_{i=0}^{r}\alpha_{i}^{2}-\alpha_{0}\sum_{i=0}^{r}\alpha_{i}\\
&=\delta^{2}-1-3\alpha_{0}(\delta-1)=(\delta-1)(\delta+1-3\alpha_{0}),\end{split}

hence

\begin{split}&\delta<3\alpha_{0},\quad\text{i.e.,}\quad k_{\mathcal{L}}=\delta-\alpha_{0}<2\alpha_{0},\quad\text{i.e.,}\\
&\alpha_{0}>\frac{k_{\mathcal{L}}}{2},\quad\text{hence}\quad h_{\mathcal{L}}\geqslant 0.\end{split}

Set
m:=\frac{k_{\mathcal{L}}}{2}
. Then we have

(45)

\begin{split}&(\delta-1)(\delta-3m+1)=\delta(\delta-3m)+3m-1=\\
&=\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}-m)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{i}-m).\end{split}

Indeed, by subtracting term by term

(\delta-1)(\delta-2)=\sum_{i=0}^{r}\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{i}-1)

from

(46)

\delta^{2}-1=\sum_{i=0}^{r}\alpha_{i}^{2}

we obtain

(47)

3(\delta-1)=\sum_{i=0}^{r}\alpha_{i}.

Then subtracting term by term (
47
) multiplied by
m
form (
46
), we obtain (
45
).

By (
45
) and since
2m=\delta-\alpha_{0}
, we have

2m(\alpha_{0}-m)=(\delta-\alpha_{0})(\alpha_{0}-m)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{i}-m)-3m+1.

Since
m=\frac{k_{\mathcal{L}}}{2}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
, we have

\begin{split}&2m(\alpha_{0}-m)=(\delta-\alpha_{0})(\alpha_{0}-m)<\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{i}-m)\leqslant\\
&\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathcal{L}}}\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{i}-m)\leqslant 2m\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathcal{L}}}(\alpha_{i}-m)\end{split}

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
\alpha_{i}\leqslant 2m=\delta-\alpha_{0}
, which is equivalent to
\delta\geqslant\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{i}
, which in turn holds because otherwise the line through
p_{0}
and
p_{i}
would split off
{\mathcal{L}}
, which is impossible, because the general curve of
{\mathcal{L}}
is irreducible. In conclusion we have

(48)

\alpha_{0}-m<\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathcal{L}}}(\alpha_{i}-m)

and this implies that:

\bullet

h_{\mathcal{L}}\geqslant 2
;

\bullet

p_{1},\ldots,p_{h_{\mathcal{L}}}
cannot be all proximate to
p_{0}
, otherwise we would have

\alpha_{0}\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathcal{L}}}\alpha_{i}

which contradicts (
48
).

Note also that for
0<i<j\leqslant h_{\mathcal{L}}
the points
p_{0},p_{i},p_{j}
cannot be on (the strict transform) of a line, otherwise, if this happens, since

\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}>\alpha_{0}+2m=\alpha_{0}+\delta-\alpha_{0}=\delta,

the line would split off
{\mathcal{L}}
, a contradiction.

Case 1:
there are two points
p_{i},p_{j}
among
p_{1},\ldots,p_{h_{\mathcal{L}}}
such that there is an irreducible conic through
p_{0},p_{i},p_{j}
. This means that none of
p_{i},p_{j}
is satellite. Then we can consider the quadratic transformation
based
at
p_{0},p_{1},p_{0}
, i.e., determined by the homalodal net
{\mathcal{L}}(2;p_{0},p_{i},p_{j})
. Then
\psi:=\gamma\circ\phi
is determined by the homaloidal net
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
transformed of
{\mathcal{L}}
via
\gamma
. This is a linear system of degree

\delta-\epsilon\quad\text{with}\quad\epsilon=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}-\delta=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}-2m>0

since
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}>m
. Then
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
has the following multiplicities at the points
p^{\prime}_{0},p^{\prime}_{i},p^{\prime}_{j}
, where the lines
p_{i}p_{j},p_{0}p_{j},p_{0}p_{i}
are contracted by
\gamma
:

\begin{split}&\delta-\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{0}-\epsilon<\alpha_{0}\\
&\delta-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{j}=2m-\alpha_{j}\leqslant m\\
&\delta-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{i}=2m-\alpha_{i}\leqslant m,\end{split}

whereas all the other multiplicities stay the same.

If
p^{\prime}_{0}
is still the point with the highest multiplicity, then
k_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}=k_{{\mathcal{L}}}
, but we see that
h_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}=h_{{\mathcal{L}}}-2
, so the simplicity went down. If
p^{\prime}_{0}
is no longer the point with the highest multiplicity, then the highest multiplicity is
\mu>\alpha_{0}-\epsilon
, then

k_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}=(\delta-\epsilon)-\mu<(\delta-\epsilon)-(\alpha_{0}-\epsilon)=\delta-\alpha_{0}=k_{{\mathcal{L}}},

and again the simplicity went down.

Case 2:
for any two points
p_{i},p_{j}
among
p_{1},\ldots,p_{h_{\mathcal{L}}}
there is no irreducible conic through
p_{0},p_{i},p_{j}
. This means that
p_{i}>p_{0}
, for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant h_{\mathcal{L}}
, and moreover we can find
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant h_{\mathcal{L}}
such that

p_{j}>^{1}p_{i}>^{1}p_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad p_{j}\odot p_{0}.

Choose
q\in{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
general, and consider the quadratic transformation
\gamma
based at
p_{0},p_{i},q
. Set
\psi=\gamma\circ\phi
. This is determined by the homalodail net
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
which is transformed of
{\mathcal{L}}
by
\gamma
. Then
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
has degree

2\delta-(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{i})=\delta+(\delta-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{i})=\delta+(2m-\alpha_{i})=\delta+\epsilon

where
\epsilon=\delta-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{i}=2m-\alpha_{i}
and
0\leqslant\epsilon\leqslant m
, because, as we saw,
\alpha_{i}\leqslant 2m
and
\alpha_{i}>m
. The multiplicities at the points
p^{\prime}_{0},p^{\prime}_{i},q^{\prime}
, where the lines
p_{i}q,p_{0}q,p_{0}p_{i}
are contracted by
\gamma
are:

\begin{split}&\delta-\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{0}+\epsilon\geqslant\alpha_{0}\\
&\delta-\alpha_{0}=2m\\
&\delta-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{i}=\epsilon<m,\end{split}

whereas all the other multiplicities stay the same. One has
\alpha_{0}+\epsilon\geqslant 2m
which is equivalent to
\alpha_{0}\geqslant\alpha_{i}
. Hence
k_{\mathcal{L}}=k_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}
,
m
stays the same and also
h_{\mathcal{L}}=h_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}
. However
p_{j}
is no longer satellite, therefore
s_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}=s_{\mathcal{L}}-1
, and the simplicity went down.

To finish the proof, we have to show that all quadratic transformation are composed by projective transformations and by the standard quadratic transformations. To do this, we remark that the quadratic transformations, corresponding to a homaloidal net of the form
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}_{2}(1^{3})
, are of three types:

(Type I)

the three base points
p_{0},p_{1},p_{2}
of
{\mathcal{L}}
are proper and distinct;

(Type II)

p_{0}
and
p_{2}
are proper and distinct and
p_{1}>^{1}p_{0}
;

(Type III)

p_{0}
is proper and
p_{2}>^{1}p_{1}>^{1}p_{0}
, and
p_{2}
is not satellite of
p_{0}
.

The quadratic transformations of type I are clearly composed of a projective transformation and of the standard quadratic transformation.

Claim 16.4
.

A quadratic transformation of type II [resp. of type III] is the product of two [resp. of four] quadratic transformations of type I.

Proof of the Claim
16.4
.

Consider the quadratic transformation
\alpha
of type II, with base points as
p_{0},p_{1},p_{2}
with
p_{1}>^{1}p_{0}
. Consider the quadratic transformation
\gamma
of type I with base points
p_{0},p_{2},q
where
q
is a general point of the plane. The homaloidal net determining
\alpha
is then transformed by
\gamma
in a homaloidal net
\beta
of conics with three distinct proper points: the points in which the lines
qp_{0}
and
qp_{2}
are contracted by
\gamma
and the point corresponding to
p_{1}
which is now a proper point on the line which is contracted to
p_{0}
by the inverse transformation of
\gamma
. This proves the claim in this case.

Similarly, consider the quadratic transformation
\alpha
of type III, with base points
p_{0},p_{1},p_{2}
with
p_{2}>^{1}p_{1}>^{1}p_{0}
. Consider the quadratic transformation
\gamma
of type II, with base points
p_{0},p_{1},q
, where
q
is a general point in the plane. As above one sees that the homaloidal net determining
\alpha
is transformed by
\gamma
in a homaloidal net
\beta
of conics with two distinct proper points and one point infinitely near to one of the two (we leave the details to the reader). Since the claim holds for type II transformations, this prove that transformations of type III are the product of four transformations of type I. ∎

The classical Noether–Castelnuovo’s theorem is now completely proved.∎

17.
Examples

In this section we collect a number of interesting examples which illustrate various phenomena concerning the Kleiman–Mori cone.

17.1.
Negative curves

Lemma 17.1
.

let
S
be a smooth surface.

(i)

If
R=\mathbb{R}^{+}[\alpha]
is an extremal ray of
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)
, then
\alpha^{2}\leqslant 0
;

(ii)

if
C
is an irreducible curve on
S
such that
C^{2}<0
, then
\mathbb{R}^{+}[C]
is an extremal ray of
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)
.

Proof.

(i) Assume, by contradiction, that
\alpha^{2}>0
. If
H
is ample, we have
H\cdot\alpha>0
by Kleiman’s criterion of ampleness. So there is an open neighborhood
U
of
\alpha
in
\operatorname{Num}(S)\otimes\mathbb{R}
such that for all
\beta\in U
, one has
\beta^{2}>0
and
H\cdot\beta>0
. For all rational
\beta\in U
, we have a suitable multiple
k\beta
of
\beta
such that
k\beta=D
is a divisor and for
m\gg 0
we have

h^{0}(mD)+h^{0}(K_{S}-mD)\geqslant\chi(mD)=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{S})+\frac{(mD)\cdot(mD-K_{S})}{2}>0.

But
h^{0}(K_{S}-mD)=0
because
H\cdot(K_{S}-mD)=H\cdot K_{S}-mkH\cdot\beta<0
for
m\gg 0
, so
mD
is effective which implies that
\alpha
is in the interior of
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)
.

(ii) Write
[C]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{+}[\beta_{i}]
are extremal rays for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. We have

0>C^{2}=C\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i},

hence there is some
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
C\cdot\beta_{j}<0
. Set
\beta=\beta_{j}
.
Then
\beta=\lim_{n\to\infty}\beta_{n}
, with
\beta_{n}
in the interior of
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(S)
, and we have
C\cdot\beta_{n}<0
for
n\gg 0
. Hence for
n\gg 0
we have
\beta_{n}=\lambda_{n}C+\beta^{\prime}_{n}
, with
\lambda_{n}>0
and
C\cdot\beta^{\prime}_{n}\geqslant 0
. Thus
\beta=\lambda C+\beta^{\prime}
, with
\lambda=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{n}
,
\beta^{\prime}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\beta^{\prime}_{n}
, and
C\cdot\beta^{\prime}\geqslant 0
. We claim that
\beta^{\prime}=0
. If not,
since
\mathbb{R}^{+}[\beta]
is an extremal ray, we have that

\mathbb{R}^{+}[\beta]=\mathbb{R}^{+}[\beta^{\prime}]=\mathbb{R}^{+}[C]
, which is impossible, because

C\cdot\beta<0
whereas
C\cdot\beta^{\prime}\geqslant 0
. Hence
\beta^{\prime}=0
and

\mathbb{R}^{+}[\beta]=\mathbb{R}^{+}[C]
as wanted.
∎

Example 17.2
.

Consider the
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
–bundle
S=\mathbb{F}_{n}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. One has
\operatorname{Num}(S)\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, generated by the classes
f
of a fibre
F
and the class
e
of the section
E
that
E^{2}=-n
. This section is unique if
n\neq 0
and in this case it generates an extremal ray. The other extremal ray is generated by
f
. In fact, for every irreducible curve
C\neq F
on
S
, we have
C\sim\lambda F+\beta E
, and
\beta\geqslant 0
, otherwise
C\cdot F<0
, which is impossible. We claim that also
\lambda\geqslant 0
. Otherwise,
C\cdot E=\lambda-n\beta<0
, so we can write
C=\alpha E+C^{\prime}
, with
C^{\prime}
effective not containing
E
, hence
E\cdot C^{\prime}\geqslant 0
. But then
C^{\prime}\sim\lambda F+(\beta-\alpha)E
and
\beta\geqslant\alpha
because
C^{\prime}
is effective. So
E\cdot C^{\prime}=\lambda-n(\beta-\alpha)<0
, a contradiction. This proves that
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(\mathbb{F}_{n})=\mathbb{R}^{+}[f]+\mathbb{R}^{+}[e]
if
n\neq 0
.

If
n=0
, one sees that for all curves
C
on
\mathbb{F}_{0}
, different from
E
and
F
, one has
C^{2}>0
, which again implies that
f
and
e
are the two extremal rays.

Example 17.3
.

Let
\pi:X\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
be the blow–up of the plane at three distinct points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
sitting on a line
\ell
. On
X
we have the three
(-1)
–curves
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
corresponding to
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
respectively. There is also the strict transform
C
of the line
\ell
, which has
C^{2}=-2
. These four curves are extremal rays of
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(X)
.

The anticanonical system
|-K_{X}|
is the strict transform on
X
of the
6
–dimensional linear system of cubics through
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
, hence it is nef with positive self–intersection equal to
6
, and it is base point free. So all
\alpha\in\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(X)
have
\alpha\cdot K_{X}\leqslant 0
.

The curve
C
is the only irreducible curve such that
C\cdot K_{X}=0
, i.e.,
\mathbb{R}^{+}[C]
is the intersection of
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(X)
with the hyperplane
K_{X}^{\perp}
. Indeed, suppose there is some other irreducible curve
D
such that
D\cdot K_{X}=0
, thus
D^{2}\geqslant-2
.
Suppose the image of
D
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
has degree
d>0
and multiplicities
m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}
at
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
. Then
D\cdot K_{X}=0

reads

(49)

3d-(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})=0.

Moreover
(D+C)\cdot K_{X}=0
, then by the Hodge index theorem, one has

0>(D+C)^{2}=D^{2}+C^{2}+2C\cdot D\geqslant-4+2C\cdot D,

which implies
0\leqslant C\cdot D\leqslant 1
, that in turn reads

(50)

0\leqslant d-(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})\leqslant 1

which is incompatible with (
49
).

Moreover there is no other
(-1)
–curve
E
on
X
besides
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
. In fact the image of
E
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
would have degree
d>0
and multiplicities
m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}
at

p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
. Since
E\cdot K_{X}=-1
, we have

(51)

3d-(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})=1.

Moreover
|-K_{X}|
defines a morphism
\phi_{|-K_{X}|}
which contracts
C
to a double point of the image, and
E
is mapped to a line. Therefore
0\leqslant C\cdot E\leqslant 1
, which reads again (
50
),
which is incompatible with (
51
).

If
D
is any irreducible curve on
X
, different from
E
and
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
, then
D^{2}\geqslant 0
. In fact, one has
D\cdot K_{X}<0
. If
D^{2}<0
, we would have
D\cdot K_{X}=D^{2}=-1
, which, as we saw, is impossible.

Suppose
D^{2}=0
and
the image of
D
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
has degree
d>0
and multiplicities
m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}
. Then
D\cdot K_{X}=-2
, i.e.,

(52)

3d-(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})=2,

and
|D|
is a pencil of rational curves, which is mapped by
\phi_{|-K_{X}|}
to a pencil of conics. Then, as above, we have
0\leqslant C\cdot D\leqslant 1
, i.e., (
50
) holds. Then by (
52
), we see that the only possibility is
d=1
, and
m_{i}=1
,
m_{j}=m_{k}=0
, with
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
. This implies that
D\sim C+E_{j}+E_{k}
, so that
D
cannot span an extremal ray.

In conclusion
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(X)
is the polyhedral cone spanned by the extremal rays
\mathbb{R}^{+}[C]
,
\mathbb{R}^{+}[E_{1}]
,
\mathbb{R}^{+}[E_{2}]
,
\mathbb{R}^{+}[E_{3}]
.

17.2.
The blown–up plane

Consider
\pi:X_{n}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
the blow–up of the plane at
n
general points
p_{1}\ldots,p_{n}
, with
(-1)
–curves
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
corresponding to
p_{1}\ldots,p_{n}
respectively. A linear system
{\mathcal{L}}
on
X_{n}
of the form

|dL-\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}E_{i}|

where
L
is the transform on
X_{n}
of a general line of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, is mapped to the plane to a linear system of the type
{\mathcal{L}}(d;m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
.
The integer
d
is called the
degree
of
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Note that the homaloidal nets with base points at
p_{1}\ldots,p_{n}
determine
Cremona transformations which form a group acting on
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
. We extend this group with the permutations over
p_{1}\ldots,p_{n}
and call this group
K_{n}
.

If we have
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}(d;m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
we set

\begin{split}&\nu_{\mathcal{L}}=d^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{2}\quad\text{called the self--intersection of}\quad{\mathcal{L}};\\
&g_{\mathcal{L}}={{d-1}\choose 2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}{{m_{i}}\choose 2}\quad\text{called the arithmetic genus of}\quad{\mathcal{L}}.\end{split}

we often write
\nu
and
g
instead of
\nu_{\mathcal{L}}
and
g_{\mathcal{L}}
if there is no danger of confusion. Note that
\nu
and
g
, as well as
\dim({\mathcal{L}})
are invariant for the
K_{n}
–action.

Lemma 17.4
.

Assume
n\geqslant 9
and
{\mathcal{L}}
with
\nu\geqslant 2g-2
. The the
K_{n}
–orbit of
{\mathcal{L}}
is infinite (hence
K_{n}
is infinite), unless
n=9
,
d=3m
,
m_{1}=\cdots=m_{9}=m
.

Proof.

Assume
m_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant m_{n}
. We claim that

(53)

m_{n}+m_{n-1}+m_{n-2}<d.

If so, the quadratic transformation based at
p_{n},p_{n-1},p_{n-2}
transforms
{\mathcal{L}}
in a linear system with the same
\nu
and
g
and higher degree. By iterating this, we see the conclusion holds.

To prove (
53
), we remark that

0\leqslant\nu-(2g-2)=3d-\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}.

If (
53
) does not hold, then

m_{n}+m_{n-1}+m_{n-2}\geqslant d

and, since
n\geqslant 9
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\geqslant 3d
, hence

0\leqslant\nu-(2g-2)=3d-\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\leqslant 0,

so that

\nu=2g-2,n=9,m=m_{1}\ldots,=m_{9},d=3m,

proving the assertion.
∎

Corollary 17.5
.

If
n\geqslant 9
there are on
X_{n}
infinitely many
(-1)
–curves.

Proof.

For any
(-1)
–curve one has
\nu=-1>-2=2g-2
. The assertion follows by applying Lemma
17.4
.
∎

Remark 17.6
.

If
n\leqslant 8
there are only finitely many
(-1)
–curves on
X_{n}
. Indeed, the anticanonical system is
|-K_{X_{n}}|={\mathcal{L}}_{3}(1^{8})
and it has dimension
9-n\geqslant 1
. If
E
is a
(-1)
–curve on
X_{n}
, one has
-K_{n}\cdot E=1
. If
n\leqslant 7
, then
\dim(|-K_{X_{n}}|)\geqslant 2
, so the curve
E
is contained in a curve of
|-K_{X_{n}}|
. Hence the degree of the image of a
(-1)
–curve on
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is bounded by
3
and we are done. If
n=8
, note that
|-2K_{X_{n}}|={\mathcal{L}}_{6}(2^{8})
, which has dimension at least 3. Again any
(-1)
–curve is contained in a curve of
|-2K_{X_{n}}|
and we conclude as before.

Consider now
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}(d;m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
with
m_{1}\geqslant\ldots\geqslant m_{n}
, and
m_{3}\geqslant 1
.

Lemma 17.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a linear system as above. If

(m_{3}-1)\nu\geqslant 2m_{3}(g-1),

and either the strict inequality holds or
m_{3}<m_{1}
, then there is a Cremona transformation in
K_{n}
which lowers the degree of
{\mathcal{L}}
. If the equality holds and
m_{1}=m_{2}=m_{3}=m
the same is true, unless
d=3m
.

Proof.

We want to prove that

(54)

d<m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3},

so that the quadratic transformation based at
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
lowers the degree of
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Consider the two equalities

\begin{split}&d(d-1)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}(m_{i}-1)-(2g-2)=0\\
&d^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}^{2}-\nu=0.\end{split}

by multiplying the first by
-m_{3}
, the second by
m_{3}-1
, and then adding, we find the identity

d(3m_{3}-d)+m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}(m_{1}+m_{2})-\Big[(m_{3}-1)\nu-2m_{3}(g-1)\Big]=m_{3}\sum_{i=4}^{n}m_{i}-\sum_{i=4}^{n}m^{2}_{i}

where the right hand side is clearly non–negative. The term
d(3m_{3}-d)
reaches its maximum for
d=\frac{3m_{3}}{2}
, then monotonically decreases for larger
d
. Hence if
(
54
) does not hold, we have

(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})(2m_{3}-m_{1}-m_{2})+m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}(m_{1}+m_{2})-\Big[(m_{3}-1)\nu-2m_{3}(g-1)\Big]\geqslant 0

hence

2(m_{3}^{2}-m_{1}m_{2})-\Big[(m_{3}-1)\nu-2m_{3}(g-1)\Big]\geqslant 0

whence we get a contradiction. ∎

Corollary 17.8
.

The action of
K_{n}
on
(-1)
–curves is transitive.

Proof.

If
E
is a curve of
X_{n}
different from one of the curves
E_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, and also different from one of the curves
E_{ij}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}(p_{i}^{1},p_{j}^{1})
, for

1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
, then
E={\mathcal{L}}(d;m_{1},\dots,m_{n})
with
m_{1}\geqslant\ldots\geqslant m_{n}
, and
m_{3}\geqslant 1
. Then we can apply Lemma
17.7
to
E
and lower its degree
d
. Apply this repeatedly till we arrive at degree 0 or 1. In the degree 0 case, we have one of the curves
E_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. In the degree 1 case
E
is one of the curves
E_{ij}
as above, whose degree can be lowered to 0 by the quadratic transformation based at
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
, with
k\neq i,j
. The assertion follows.∎

17.3.
Products of elliptic curves

Let
E
be a general curve of genus 1. Set
X=E\times E
. Let
f_{1},f_{2},\delta
be the classes of the fibres of the projections of
X
to the two factors and of the diagonal. One has the intersection numbers

f_{1}^{2}=f_{2}^{2}=\delta^{2}=0,f_{1}\cdot f_{2}=f_{1}\cdot\delta=f_{2}\cdot\delta=1

hence the intersection matrix has determinant 2. So
f_{1},f_{2},\delta
are independent and it is a classical fact that they span
\operatorname{Num}(X)
(e.g., this follows from
[
8
, Lemma 2.2]
).

The surface
X
is an abelian surface, and
K_{X}
is trivial.
For any irreducible curve on
X
, one has
C^{2}\geqslant 0
, because
X
is abelian and the translations move any curve in a 2–dimensional algebraic family. Moreover
C^{2}=0
if and only if
C
has genus 1. There are infinitely many such curves, e.g., the graphs of all multiplication maps
E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot n}}{{\rightarrow}}E
. The divisors
C
such that
C^{2}>0
and
C\cdot H>0
, with
H
a fixed ample divisor (e.g.,
H=f_{1}+f_{2}
), are effective. Hence
\operatorname{\overline{NE}}(X)
is the cone spanned by curves
C
such that

C\cdot H>0\quad\text{and}\quad C^{2}\geqslant 0.

It has infinitely many extremal rays, actually uncountably many, corresponding to classes
\alpha
with
\alpha\cdot H>0
and
\alpha^{2}=0
, and only countably many of them are rational and provide contractions
E\times E\to E
.
